Chapter 33

Design Data Structure Problems

Orientation

A cache returns a stale value after an eviction. A random-pick service returns a deleted item. An iterator skips one element after a second call to hasNext. In each case every method looks correct alone, and the failure appears only after a particular sequence of calls. Design problems ask for a class whose operations stay correct and fast across any such sequence.

Prerequisites

You should know hash maps and sets from the chapter on hash maps and sets, dynamic arrays and doubly linked lists from the chapters on arrays and linked lists, stacks from the stack chapter, and the in-order walk of a binary search tree. A contract is the promise an operation makes about its legal input, its output, its mutation of state, its failure behavior and its cost. An invariant is a fact about the stored fields that holds again at the end of each operation.

The Six Lessons

Each lesson answers one question about keeping state correct across public calls.

  • Write The Contract Before The Code states output, mutation, failure and cost for each operation before any field is chosen.
  • One Method Repairs Each Link gives every cross-field repair to exactly one method, so a rejected call leaves all fields unchanged.
  • Pair A Map With Ordered Storage uses a hash map for lookup and a list or array for position, with cross references that stay synchronized.
  • Keep Traversal State Between Calls caches one element so that hasNext and peek consume nothing.
  • Pick A Random Value In Constant Time removes by swapping with the last slot and repairs the moved value's index.
  • Evict The Least Recent Entry builds recency caches, first by last use and then by use count.

The Three Closing Lessons

Each closing lesson joins earlier parts into one structure and names what each part contributes.

  • Locate By Key And Order By Recency joins a map and a linked list, with exercises on relinking, resizing, capacity one and an eviction log.
  • Find A Slot And Pick From Array joins a map and a dynamic array, with exercises on swap removal, an exclusion pick, the last slot and duplicate values.
  • Traversal State Across Public Calls joins iterators with trees, wrappers and nested sources, with exercises on a node pool, a source that cannot restart, a depth limit and rotation among sources.

How To Work Through Each Lesson

A lesson opens with a failing sequence of calls. A direct implementation follows, and you predict where it breaks before the explanation appears. Later parts name the invariant, list the state, follow two traces, show the code and say when the idea stops fitting. Each exercise set runs from the basic structure through a variation and an edge case to a recognition problem. Several LeetCode problems return with a changed contract, and the problem text says what changed.

What You Can Do After This Chapter

You can write the contract of a class before coding it. You can name the invariant that links its fields and the one method that repairs each link. You can pick the pair of structures that gives constant-time lookup together with the ordering or position a problem needs. Concurrent designs are outside this chapter and wait for a later chapter on specialized services.

Write The Contract Before The Code

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Why A Counter Class Loses Data

A login service counts failed sign-ins per account and locks an account after five failures. A support script calls all() on the counter to print the day's totals and then clears the returned map to keep its own output tidy. The next morning the lockout rule sees zero failures for every account, and nobody has touched the counter class in weeks.

No single method in that class is wrong. The class never said what all() hands out, so the script assumed it was a private copy. Most problems in this chapter have the same shape. A data-structure problem is a sequence of public calls, and the structure is correct only if every call in every order leaves it usable. This lesson asks what each call must promise before any code exists.

Simple approach

Writing The Counter Without A Plan

The tempting version starts from the fields. A map from account name to count is the obvious state, so each method does whatever is shortest.

import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.Map;

final class SloppyCounter {
    private final Map<String, Integer> counts = new HashMap<>();

    int add(String key) {
        Integer old = counts.get(key);
        counts.put(key, old == null ? 1 : old + 1);
        return counts.get(key);
    }

    Integer count(String key) { return counts.get(key); }   // null when the key is absent

    int remove(String key) {
        Integer old = counts.get(key);
        if (old == null) return -1;                          // -1 when the key is absent
        counts.put(key, old - 1);                            // keeps an entry that holds 0
        return old - 1;
    }

    Map<String, Integer> all() { return counts; }            // hands out the real map
}

Every method compiles and passes a quick manual test. The trouble appears on the second and third call. One caller reads count("bob") for an account that never failed and gets null. Another caller removes an absent key and receives -1, which looks like a count. A third caller removes the last failure of an account and still finds that account in all() with a count of 0.

Predict first: A caller runs `int c = counter.count("bob");` for an account that was never added. What happens, and which line of the class is responsible?

The unboxing of null into an int throws a NullPointerException in the caller's code. The line return counts.get(key) is responsible, because it returns null as an answer and never says so.

Bottleneck

Every Caller Has To Guess

No method here is slow. Each runs in O(1) expected time, so cost is not the problem. The problem is that the class answers four questions differently in each method and states none of them. A caller cannot tell what a method returns, what it changes, what happens when the call is illegal, or what it costs.

Guessing is expensive because the guesses are spread over every caller. One caller treats null as zero, another treats -1 as an error, and a third compares two Integer results with ==. Each guess works on small test data and fails on larger data.

Fixing this by checking inside the class has a price as well. Returning a copy of the map from all() costs O(k) time and space for k distinct keys on every call. That price is acceptable only when the specification says callers need a private copy. So the code alone cannot decide, and the decision has to be written down first.

Key idea

State Four Facts For Each Operation

Listing The Four Facts

An operation is a public method of the structure. Its contract is the promise it makes to every caller, and the contract has four parts. The output says what the call returns. The mutation says which fields change, and a call that changes nothing says so. The failure behavior says what happens when the call is illegal. The cost says the time and space, such as O(1) expected time. A structure is correct when every operation keeps its contract after every earlier call, and the invariant is the fact about the state that makes this possible.

Choosing How A Call Fails

The failure contract of an operation is the part of its contract that covers an illegal call. It has two decisions. The first is how the caller learns about the failure, and there are three standard answers. The operation throws an exception, such as NoSuchElementException. It returns a sentinel, which is a reserved return value outside the range of real answers, so -1 works only when real answers are never negative. Or the contract states a precondition that the caller must establish, and the operation promises nothing if the caller breaks it. The second decision is whether a failed call changes the state, and the safest answer is that it never does.

Pick one answer for the whole structure. A stack whose pop throws and whose top returns -1 forces every caller to remember both rules. Choosing one answer still allows two exception types: a structure throws IndexOutOfBoundsException for a bad position and IllegalStateException for a call that needs data when the structure is empty.

Keeping Internal State Private

An operation must not return a reference to a mutable field. A caller who receives the real map or array can change it without calling any operation, so the invariant no longer holds. A defensive copy is a new object that holds the same data, returned in place of the field. The cost of the copy, O(k) for k elements, belongs in the contract of that operation. A read-only view, such as Collections.unmodifiableMap, avoids the copy but reflects later changes, so the contract must say which one the caller gets.

State

Names Used In A Counter Contract

A counter that supports duplicates needs a few entities with fixed meanings. The names below are used in every exercise of this lesson.

  • counts is the map from each key to its number of occurrences, holding only keys whose count is at least 1.
  • total is the sum of all counts, which total() returns in O(1).
  • distinct is the number of keys with a positive count, which equals counts.size().
  • call is one public operation invoked on the structure, such as add("bob").
  • state after a call is the triple of counts, total and distinct that the next call sees.

The invariant ties these together: every stored count is at least 1, and total equals the sum of the stored counts.

Walkthrough

Tracing Calls On Two Structures

Following The Counter Calls

Start with an empty counter that follows the contract: add increments, remove decrements and deletes the key at zero, and remove on a missing key throws and changes nothing. The calls below arrive in order, and the trace records the state after each one.

The first add("bob") creates the key with count 1. The second add("bob") raises it to 2, so total is 2 while distinct stays 1. The call add("amy") adds a second key. The call remove("bob") lowers bob to 1. The call remove("amy") brings amy to 0, so the key disappears and distinct drops to 1. The last call, remove("amy") again, finds no key and throws, and the state is exactly what it was before the call.

Following The Stack Calls

A stack contract works the same way. It promises that push appends, pop removes and returns the newest value, top returns it without removing, and both pop and top throw on an empty stack without changing it.

After push(4) and push(9), the call top() returns 9 and the stack still holds both values. The first pop() returns 9 and the second returns 4, which leaves the stack empty. The third pop() throws, and the stack stays empty. A later push(7) works normally, which proves the failed call did not damage the state. The final top() returns 7.

Code

Implementing The Counter Contract

The contract goes into the Javadoc first, one line per fact, and the code follows it. This version keeps the invariant and throws on an illegal removal.

import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.Map;
import java.util.NoSuchElementException;

final class AccountCounter {
    private final Map<String, Integer> counts = new HashMap<>();
    private int total;

    /** Output: new count of key. Mutation: count + 1, total + 1. Failure: none. Cost: O(1) expected. */
    int add(String key) {
        total++;
        return counts.merge(key, 1, Integer::sum);
    }

    /** Output: none. Mutation: count - 1, key deleted at 0. Failure: NoSuchElementException, state unchanged. Cost: O(1) expected. */
    void remove(String key) {
        Integer c = counts.get(key);
        if (c == null) throw new NoSuchElementException(key);   // checked before any write
        if (c == 1) counts.remove(key); else counts.put(key, c - 1);
        total--;
    }

    /** Output: count of key, 0 when absent. Mutation: none. Failure: none. Cost: O(1) expected. */
    int count(String key) { return counts.getOrDefault(key, 0); }

    /** Output: number of keys with a positive count. Mutation: none. Failure: none. Cost: O(1). */
    int distinct() { return counts.size(); }

    /** Output: sum of all counts. Mutation: none. Failure: none. Cost: O(1). */
    int total() { return total; }

    /** Output: independent copy of the counts. Mutation: none. Cost: O(k) time and space. */
    Map<String, Integer> snapshot() { return new HashMap<>(counts); }
}

The method count returns 0 for an absent key, so absence is not a failure and callers never meet null. The method remove runs its check before any write, so a failed call leaves counts and total as they were. The method snapshot returns a copy, so a caller who clears the result cannot reach the field.

When to use it

Checking The Contract Before Coding

Using Invariants And Calls Together

Reach for this lesson whenever a problem says "design a class" and lists methods. Correctness then lives in the order of calls, and no single call proves it. An invariant is a condition on the fields that each operation may rely on at entry and must restore before it returns. Write the invariant first, then check each operation against it, including the failing path.

Spotting A False Friend

A false friend is code that passes its first test call and then fails on a later call. The usual false friend here is the getter. Returning the internal array or list looks like a harmless read, yet the caller now owns a live handle to the state. The same trap appears when a constructor stores the caller's array without copying it.

Three Java Hazards In Contracts

Returning null for an empty result moves the failure into the caller, where it appears as a NullPointerException far from its cause. A method that returns Integer invites == comparison, which compares references, so two equal counts above 127 can compare as different. Declare int returns where absence is not a legal answer, and use equals when you must compare boxed values.

The next lesson in this chapter shows how each operation keeps the invariant when several fields change together.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Specify A Counter API

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design a class EventCounter that counts occurrences of string keys. It has operations add(key), remove(key), count(key), distinct(), total() and snapshot(). For each operation, write its output, mutation, failure behavior and cost. Then implement the class. The operation add returns the new count of key. The operation remove lowers the count of key by one and deletes the key at zero. The operation snapshot returns a map that the caller may change without affecting the counter.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Keys are non-null strings, and a null key throws NullPointerException.
  • Removal of a key whose count is 0 throws NoSuchElementException and changes no state.
  • Absent key gives count(key) = 0 and is not a failure.
  • Calls number at most 10^5, and each of add, remove, count, distinct, total costs O(1) expected time.
  • Snapshot costs O(k) for k distinct keys.

Example 1. Input add("a"), add("b"), add("a"), remove("a"), output count("a") = 1, distinct() = 2, total() = 2.

Example 2. Input a caller that clears the map returned by snapshot(), output that count("a") and total() are unchanged, and remove("zzz") throws.

Prerequisites. The counter contract in this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. First exercise in the sequence: moves the focus from writing methods to writing what each method promises.

Hint

For each operation, finish the sentence "if the caller breaks the rules, then" before you write the body. Which field would a returned reference expose?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

The contract comes first. add(key) returns the new count, increments one key and total, and never fails except for a null key. remove(key) returns nothing, decrements one key, deletes the key at zero, and throws NoSuchElementException before it writes anything when the key is absent. count(key) returns 0 for an absent key, so absence is an ordinary answer. snapshot() returns a new map, which costs O(k) and keeps the caller away from the field.

The invariant is that every stored count is at least 1 and total equals the sum of the stored counts. Deleting the key at zero keeps distinct() equal to the map size. The failure check runs before any write, so a failed remove cannot break the invariant. A class that returns its own map breaks the invariant from outside, and the demonstration below shows the damage next to the safe copy.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) expected per add, remove, count, distinct and total, because each does one hash-map lookup or update. snapshot takes O(k) for k distinct keys.
  • Space is O(k) for the stored counts, and each snapshot allocates another O(k).
import java.util.*;

public final class SpecifyCounterApi {
    /** A counter whose operations state output, mutation, failure and cost. */
    static final class EventCounter {
        private final Map<String, Integer> counts = new HashMap<>();
        private int total;

        /**
         * Adds one occurrence of key and returns its new count.
         * Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1) amortized for a new key.
         * Invariant: every stored count is at least 1 and total is their sum.
         */
        int add(String key) {
            // A null key is a caller bug, so fail before any write.
            Objects.requireNonNull(key, "key");
            // total and the key's count both grow by one, which keeps total equal to the sum.
            total++;
            return counts.merge(key, 1, Integer::sum);
        }

        /**
         * Removes one occurrence of key; throws NoSuchElementException when none exists.
         * Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: the check precedes every write, so a failed call changes nothing.
         */
        void remove(String key) {
            Objects.requireNonNull(key, "key");
            // Read the current count; null means the key is absent.
            Integer c = counts.get(key);
            if (c == null) throw new NoSuchElementException("no occurrence of " + key);
            // Delete the key at 1 so no stored count ever reaches 0.
            if (c == 1) counts.remove(key); else counts.put(key, c - 1);
            total--;
        }

        /** Returns the count of key, 0 when absent. Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1). */
        int count(String key) {
            Objects.requireNonNull(key, "key");
            // getOrDefault turns absence into a legal answer, so the caller never sees null.
            return counts.getOrDefault(key, 0);
        }

        /** Returns the number of keys with a positive count. Time: O(1). Space: O(1). */
        int distinct() { return counts.size(); }

        /** Returns the sum of all counts. Time: O(1). Space: O(1). */
        int total() { return total; }

        /** Returns an independent copy of the counts. Time: O(k). Space: O(k). */
        Map<String, Integer> snapshot() { return new HashMap<>(counts); }
    }

    /** The hazard: a counter whose getter hands out its real map. */
    static final class LeakyCounter {
        private final Map<String, Integer> counts = new HashMap<>();
        void add(String key) { counts.merge(key, 1, Integer::sum); }
        Map<String, Integer> all() { return counts; }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The sample sequence from the problem.
        EventCounter c = new EventCounter();
        if (c.add("a") != 1 || c.add("b") != 1 || c.add("a") != 2) throw new AssertionError("add results");
        c.remove("a");
        if (c.count("a") != 1 || c.distinct() != 2 || c.total() != 2) throw new AssertionError("example 1");

        // Clearing a snapshot never reaches the counter, and an absent key is not a failure.
        c.snapshot().clear();
        if (c.count("a") != 1 || c.total() != 2) throw new AssertionError("snapshot is a copy");
        if (c.count("zzz") != 0) throw new AssertionError("absent key counts 0");
        boolean threw = false;
        try { c.remove("zzz"); } catch (NoSuchElementException e) { threw = true; }
        if (!threw || c.total() != 2 || c.distinct() != 2) throw new AssertionError("failed remove keeps state");
        threw = false;
        try { c.add(null); } catch (NullPointerException e) { threw = true; }
        if (!threw || c.total() != 2) throw new AssertionError("null key");

        // The leaky version loses its data when a caller clears the returned map.
        LeakyCounter leaky = new LeakyCounter();
        leaky.add("bob");
        leaky.all().clear();
        if (!leaky.all().isEmpty()) throw new AssertionError("leak lets the caller erase state");

        // Boxed counts above 127 are separate objects, so == compares references and equals compares values.
        EventCounter big1 = new EventCounter(), big2 = new EventCounter();
        for (int i = 0; i < 1000; i++) { big1.add("x"); big2.add("x"); }
        Integer p = big1.snapshot().get("x"), q = big2.snapshot().get("x");
        if (p == q) throw new AssertionError("1000 is outside the Integer cache");
        if (!p.equals(q) || p != 1000) throw new AssertionError("equals compares values");

        // Random operation scripts replayed against a naive list model.
        Random rnd = new Random(3301);
        String[] keys = {"a", "b", "c", "d"};
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            EventCounter real = new EventCounter();
            List<String> model = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = 0; step < 60; step++) {
                String k = keys[rnd.nextInt(keys.length)];
                if (rnd.nextBoolean()) {
                    // The model counts occurrences by scanning the list.
                    real.add(k); model.add(k);
                } else {
                    boolean present = model.contains(k);
                    boolean failed = false;
                    try { real.remove(k); } catch (NoSuchElementException e) { failed = true; }
                    if (failed == present) throw new AssertionError("failure matches absence");
                    if (present) model.remove(k);
                }
                if (real.total() != model.size()) throw new AssertionError("total");
                if (real.distinct() != new HashSet<>(model).size()) throw new AssertionError("distinct");
                for (String key : keys) {
                    if (real.count(key) != Collections.frequency(model, key)) throw new AssertionError("count " + key);
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Empty Removal Policy

Author exercise

Problem Specification. An IntStack has operations push(v), pop(), top() and isEmpty(). Implement three versions that differ only in how pop and top treat an empty stack. The throwing version throws NoSuchElementException and keeps its state. The sentinel version returns -1 and keeps its state, which requires that every pushed value is at least 0. The precondition version promises nothing for an empty stack, and callers must test isEmpty() first. Each version applies its rule to both pop and top.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Values pushed to the sentinel version are in the range 0 to 10^9, and a negative value throws IllegalArgumentException.
  • Operations number at most 10^5, and each costs O(1) amortized.
  • Failed call in the throwing and sentinel versions leaves size and contents unchanged.
  • Precondition version has no behavior to test for an empty stack except that callers avoid it.

Example 1. Input push(5), pop(), pop(), output 5 and then an exception for the throwing version, 5 and then -1 for the sentinel version.

Example 2. Input push(-1) on the sentinel version, output an IllegalArgumentException, because a stored -1 would be impossible to tell from "empty".

Prerequisites. The specify-counter exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. The failure behavior changes while the output and mutation of legal calls stay the same.

Hint

Where would a caller of each version have to write an extra check? Which version needs a restriction on the values?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

All three versions share one array-backed stack and differ only in the empty case, which is applied to pop and top alike. The throwing version checks size == 0 first and throws NoSuchElementException, so a failed call changes nothing. The sentinel version checks the same condition and returns -1. That answer is only unambiguous when no stored value is -1, so push rejects negative values. The precondition version has no check at all, so its cost on the legal path is the lowest, and a violation is the caller's bug.

The demonstration pins down the difference. The first two versions are tested on the empty stack and on a state check afterward. The third is tested only on legal calls, plus one deliberate violation. In this implementation pop decrements size before it reads, so the violation throws ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException and leaves size at -1. The contract does not promise that outcome, which is exactly why callers must check isEmpty().

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) per operation in all three versions, and push is O(1) amortized because the array doubles when it fills.
  • Space is O(n) for n stored values, with at most 2n slots allocated after doubling.
import java.util.*;

public final class EmptyRemovalPolicy {
    interface IntStack {
        void push(int v);
        int pop();
        int top();
        boolean isEmpty();
        int size();
    }

    /** Shared storage; subclasses choose only the empty-stack rule. */
    static abstract class ArrayStack implements IntStack {
        protected int[] data = new int[2];
        protected int size;
        /** Appends v. Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(1) amortized. Invariant: data[0..size-1] holds the stack. */
        public void push(int v) {
            // Double the array when full so n pushes cost O(n) in total.
            if (size == data.length) data = Arrays.copyOf(data, size * 2);
            data[size++] = v;
        }
        public boolean isEmpty() { return size == 0; }
        public int size() { return size; }
    }

    /** Policy A: an empty stack throws and the state stays unchanged. */
    static final class ThrowingStack extends ArrayStack {
        public int pop() {
            // The check precedes the decrement, so a failed call writes nothing.
            if (size == 0) throw new NoSuchElementException("empty");
            return data[--size];
        }
        public int top() {
            if (size == 0) throw new NoSuchElementException("empty");
            return data[size - 1];
        }
    }

    /** Policy B: an empty stack returns -1, so stored values must be at least 0. */
    static final class SentinelStack extends ArrayStack {
        static final int EMPTY = -1;
        @Override public void push(int v) {
            // The sentinel is only unambiguous if no real value equals it.
            if (v < 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("negative value");
            super.push(v);
        }
        public int pop() { return size == 0 ? EMPTY : data[--size]; }
        public int top() { return size == 0 ? EMPTY : data[size - 1]; }
    }

    /** Policy C: the caller must check isEmpty() first; an empty call is outside the contract. */
    static final class PreconditionStack extends ArrayStack {
        public int pop() { return data[--size]; }
        public int top() { return data[size - 1]; }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Policy A on the sample: 5, then an exception, then an unchanged empty stack.
        ThrowingStack a = new ThrowingStack();
        a.push(5);
        if (a.pop() != 5) throw new AssertionError("A pop");
        boolean threw = false;
        try { a.pop(); } catch (NoSuchElementException e) { threw = true; }
        if (!threw || a.size() != 0) throw new AssertionError("A empty pop keeps state");
        threw = false;
        try { a.top(); } catch (NoSuchElementException e) { threw = true; }
        if (!threw) throw new AssertionError("A applies the same rule to top");

        // Policy B on the sample: 5, then -1 for both pop and top.
        SentinelStack b = new SentinelStack();
        b.push(5);
        if (b.pop() != 5 || b.pop() != -1 || b.top() != -1 || b.size() != 0) throw new AssertionError("B sample");
        threw = false;
        try { b.push(-1); } catch (IllegalArgumentException e) { threw = true; }
        if (!threw || b.size() != 0) throw new AssertionError("B rejects the sentinel value");

        // Policy C: a legal sequence works, and a violation corrupts this implementation's size.
        PreconditionStack c = new PreconditionStack();
        c.push(8);
        if (c.top() != 8 || c.pop() != 8 || !c.isEmpty()) throw new AssertionError("C legal calls");
        threw = false;
        try { c.pop(); } catch (ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException e) { threw = true; }
        if (!threw || c.size() != -1) throw new AssertionError("C violation leaves undefined state");

        // Random scripts against a list model; C is only called when the model is non-empty.
        Random rnd = new Random(3302);
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            ThrowingStack ra = new ThrowingStack();
            SentinelStack rb = new SentinelStack();
            PreconditionStack rc = new PreconditionStack();
            List<Integer> model = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = 0; step < 80; step++) {
                int op = rnd.nextInt(3);
                if (op == 0) {
                    int v = rnd.nextInt(1000);
                    ra.push(v); rb.push(v); rc.push(v); model.add(v);
                } else if (model.isEmpty()) {
                    // Empty model: A throws, B returns -1, C is not called.
                    boolean t = false;
                    try { if (op == 1) ra.pop(); else ra.top(); } catch (NoSuchElementException e) { t = true; }
                    if (!t) throw new AssertionError("A throws on empty");
                    int got = op == 1 ? rb.pop() : rb.top();
                    if (got != -1) throw new AssertionError("B sentinel");
                } else {
                    int want = model.get(model.size() - 1);
                    if (op == 1) {
                        model.remove(model.size() - 1);
                        if (ra.pop() != want || rb.pop() != want || rc.pop() != want) throw new AssertionError("pop");
                    } else if (ra.top() != want || rb.top() != want || rc.top() != want) throw new AssertionError("top");
                }
                if (ra.size() != model.size() || rb.size() != model.size() || rc.size() != model.size()) throw new AssertionError("size");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Repeated Calls

Author exercise

Problem Specification. A counter follows the contract of EventCounter. Given a list of calls of the form add k or remove k, report the state after each call. The state is written distinct/total. A call that succeeds is reported as ok d/t. A call that throws is reported as err d/t, and the numbers show the unchanged state. Return the list of reports in call order.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Calls number between 1 and 1000, and keys are lowercase letters.
  • Duplicates are allowed, so the same key may be added many times.
  • Removal of a key with count 0 is reported as err and changes no state.
  • Distinct drops only when a key's count reaches 0.

Example 1. Input add a, add a, remove a, output ok 1/1, ok 1/2, ok 1/1.

Example 2. Input add x, add x, add y, remove x, remove x, remove x, add x, output ok 1/1, ok 1/2, ok 2/3, ok 2/2, ok 1/1, err 1/1, ok 2/2.

Prerequisites. The two exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. Calls repeat on the same key, so the invariant that every stored count is at least 1 now matters on every call.

Hint

What happens to distinct on the first removal of a key that was added twice? Which numbers must a failed call leave alone?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

The simulation applies the counter contract call by call and records distinct/total after each one. An add always succeeds and increments the key's count and the total. A remove first looks up the key. When the count is positive, the remove lowers it, deletes the key at zero and lowers the total. When the key is absent, the remove reports err and writes nothing, so the reported numbers equal the previous report.

The boundary lies in duplicates. After two add x calls, one remove x leaves distinct unchanged, because the key survives with count 1. Only the removal that brings the count to 0 lowers distinct. An implementation that decrements distinct on every removal, or that keeps zero-count entries, fails on exactly these sequences. The invariant that every stored count is at least 1 prevents both mistakes.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(m) for m calls, because each call does one hash-map lookup and update in O(1) expected time.
  • Space is O(k) for k distinct keys plus O(m) for the report list.
import java.util.*;

public final class RepeatedCalls {
    /**
     * Replays calls such as "add x" and "remove x" and reports the state after each.
     * Time: O(m) expected for m calls. Space: O(k + m) for k keys and m reports.
     * Invariant: every stored count is at least 1, and total is the sum of the counts.
     */
    static List<String> replay(List<String> calls) {
        Map<Character, Integer> counts = new HashMap<>();
        int total = 0;
        List<String> out = new ArrayList<>();
        for (String call : calls) {
            // Split the call into its operation word and its one-letter key.
            boolean isAdd = call.startsWith("add");
            char key = call.charAt(call.length() - 1);
            boolean ok = true;
            if (isAdd) {
                // compute stores 1 for a new key and count + 1 otherwise.
                counts.compute(key, (k, v) -> v == null ? 1 : v + 1);
                total++;
            } else if (counts.containsKey(key)) {
                // computeIfPresent returning null deletes the key, so a stored count is never 0.
                counts.computeIfPresent(key, (k, v) -> v == 1 ? null : v - 1);
                total--;
            } else {
                // A failed removal writes nothing; the report repeats the old numbers.
                ok = false;
            }
            out.add((ok ? "ok " : "err ") + counts.size() + "/" + total);
        }
        return out;
    }

    /** Naive model: a list of occurrences. Distinct is the set size, total is the list size. */
    static List<String> oracle(List<String> calls) {
        List<Character> model = new ArrayList<>();
        List<String> out = new ArrayList<>();
        for (String call : calls) {
            char key = call.charAt(call.length() - 1);
            boolean ok = true;
            if (call.startsWith("add")) model.add(key);
            else if (model.contains(key)) model.remove(Character.valueOf(key));
            else ok = false;
            out.add((ok ? "ok " : "err ") + new HashSet<>(model).size() + "/" + model.size());
        }
        return out;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: a duplicate keeps the key alive after one removal.
        List<String> r1 = replay(List.of("add a", "add a", "remove a"));
        if (!r1.equals(List.of("ok 1/1", "ok 1/2", "ok 1/1"))) throw new AssertionError("example 1 " + r1);

        // Example 2: the third removal fails and the next add restores a second key.
        List<String> r2 = replay(List.of("add x", "add x", "add y", "remove x", "remove x", "remove x", "add x"));
        if (!r2.equals(List.of("ok 1/1", "ok 1/2", "ok 2/3", "ok 2/2", "ok 1/1", "err 1/1", "ok 2/2")))
            throw new AssertionError("example 2 " + r2);

        // Random scripts compared with the list model.
        Random rnd = new Random(3303);
        for (int round = 0; round < 500; round++) {
            List<String> calls = new ArrayList<>();
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(40);
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
                calls.add((rnd.nextBoolean() ? "add " : "remove ") + (char) ('a' + rnd.nextInt(3)));
            }
            if (!replay(calls).equals(oracle(calls))) throw new AssertionError("mismatch " + calls);
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Min Stack

LeetCode 155

Problem Specification. Design a stack MinStack of int values with operations push(v), pop(), top() and getMin(). Before any code, write the contract of each operation: output, mutation, failure behavior and cost. The operation pop returns the removed value. The operation getMin returns the smallest value currently stored. On an empty stack, pop, top and getMin each throw NoSuchElementException and leave the stack unchanged. Then implement the stack.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Values are any int, including Integer.MIN_VALUE and Integer.MAX_VALUE.
  • Operations number at most 10^5.
  • Cost is O(1) for pop, top and getMin, and O(1) amortized for push.
  • Empty stack is a failure for pop, top and getMin, and never returns a placeholder value.

Example 1. Input push(6), push(2), push(9), push(2), getMin(), pop(), getMin(), pop(), pop(), getMin(), output 2, 2, 2, 9, 2, 6 as the results of the six calls that return a value, in order.

Example 2. Input pop() on an empty stack and then push(3) and getMin(), output an exception and then 3.

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The original problem assumes legal calls. Here the failure behavior of every operation is part of the stated contract.

Hint

Which value is the minimum after each push, and does it depend only on the values below it? What must getMin do on an empty stack?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

The contracts come first. push(v) returns nothing, appends v, and costs O(1) amortized because the arrays double. pop() returns the removed value, removes the newest value, and throws NoSuchElementException on an empty stack before it changes anything. top() returns the newest value, changes nothing, and throws on empty. getMin() returns the smallest stored value, changes nothing, costs O(1), and throws on empty, because an empty stack has no minimum and any placeholder would collide with a legal int.

To make getMin constant time, the stack keeps a second array mins, where mins[i] is the minimum of values[0..i]. The invariant is that mins[size-1] equals the minimum of everything stored. A push computes mins[size] from the previous entry in O(1). A pop only decrements size, and the entry below already holds the correct minimum for the remaining values, so no recomputation happens.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) for pop, top and getMin, and O(1) amortized for push because doubling copies are spread over earlier pushes.
  • Space is O(n) for n stored values, since two int arrays each hold at most 2n slots.
import java.util.*;

public final class MinStackContract {
    /** A stack with constant-time minimum and a stated failure contract. */
    static final class MinStack {
        private int[] values = new int[2];
        private int[] mins = new int[2];
        private int size;

        /**
         * Appends v. Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(1) amortized.
         * Invariant: mins[i] is the minimum of values[0..i].
         */
        void push(int v) {
            // Grow both arrays together so they always have equal length.
            if (size == values.length) {
                values = Arrays.copyOf(values, size * 2);
                mins = Arrays.copyOf(mins, size * 2);
            }
            values[size] = v;
            // The running minimum depends only on v and the entry below it.
            mins[size] = size == 0 ? v : Math.min(v, mins[size - 1]);
            size++;
        }

        /** Removes and returns the newest value; throws on empty with no change. Time: O(1). Space: O(1). */
        int pop() {
            // Check first so a failed call changes nothing.
            if (size == 0) throw new NoSuchElementException("empty stack");
            return values[--size];
        }

        /** Returns the newest value; throws on empty. Time: O(1). Space: O(1). */
        int top() {
            if (size == 0) throw new NoSuchElementException("empty stack");
            return values[size - 1];
        }

        /** Returns the smallest stored value; throws on empty. Time: O(1). Space: O(1). */
        int getMin() {
            if (size == 0) throw new NoSuchElementException("empty stack");
            // The top running minimum covers every stored value.
            return mins[size - 1];
        }
    }

    static boolean throwsEmpty(Runnable r) {
        try { r.run(); return false; } catch (NoSuchElementException e) { return true; }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: results of the six value-returning calls.
        MinStack s = new MinStack();
        s.push(6); s.push(2); s.push(9); s.push(2);
        int[] got = new int[6];
        got[0] = s.getMin(); got[1] = s.pop(); got[2] = s.getMin();
        got[3] = s.pop(); got[4] = s.pop(); got[5] = s.getMin();
        if (!Arrays.equals(got, new int[] {2, 2, 2, 9, 2, 6})) throw new AssertionError("example 1 " + Arrays.toString(got));

        // Example 2: a failed pop leaves the stack usable.
        MinStack e = new MinStack();
        if (!throwsEmpty(e::pop) || !throwsEmpty(e::top) || !throwsEmpty(e::getMin)) throw new AssertionError("empty failures");
        e.push(3);
        if (e.getMin() != 3) throw new AssertionError("example 2");

        // Extreme values must not be confused with a placeholder.
        MinStack x = new MinStack();
        x.push(Integer.MAX_VALUE); x.push(Integer.MIN_VALUE); x.push(Integer.MAX_VALUE);
        if (x.getMin() != Integer.MIN_VALUE) throw new AssertionError("extremes");

        // Random scripts against a list model that scans for the minimum.
        Random rnd = new Random(3304);
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            MinStack real = new MinStack();
            List<Integer> model = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = 0; step < 80; step++) {
                int op = rnd.nextInt(4);
                if (op == 0) {
                    int v = rnd.nextInt(10) == 0 ? Integer.MIN_VALUE : rnd.nextInt(21) - 10;
                    real.push(v); model.add(v);
                } else if (model.isEmpty()) {
                    // Every query fails on empty, and the next push still works.
                    Runnable r = op == 1 ? real::pop : op == 2 ? real::top : real::getMin;
                    if (!throwsEmpty(r)) throw new AssertionError("must throw on empty");
                } else if (op == 1) {
                    if (real.pop() != model.remove(model.size() - 1)) throw new AssertionError("pop");
                } else if (op == 2) {
                    if (real.top() != model.get(model.size() - 1)) throw new AssertionError("top");
                } else if (real.getMin() != Collections.min(model)) throw new AssertionError("getMin");
            }
        }
    }
}verified

One Method Repairs Each Link

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Why The Tab Count Drifts

A browser-style tab strip keeps at most three tabs open. Its code stores the tabs in a linked list, an id lookup table and a counter named size. A user opens a fourth tab, and the strip correctly refuses it with an exception. Later the user closes one tab and tries again, and the strip still says it is full. The list shows two tabs, but the counter shows three.

Nothing crashed, and every method looks reasonable when read alone. The bug sits between the methods. Three fields describe one collection, and the failed call changed one of them before it noticed the problem. This lesson shows how to keep several fields in agreement across every operation, including the operations that fail.

Simple approach

Editing Every Field Inside Each Operation

The direct approach writes each operation from scratch. Every public method edits the links, the lookup table and the counter itself, in whatever order feels natural. The class below shows the usual result. The class keeps sentinel nodes at both ends of the list, a lookup table byId and a counter size.

final class NaiveTabs {
    private static final class Node {
        final int id; Node prev, next;
        Node(int id) { this.id = id; }
    }
    private final Node head = new Node(-1), tail = new Node(-1);
    private final java.util.Map<Integer, Node> byId = new java.util.HashMap<>();
    private final int capacity;
    private int size;

    NaiveTabs(int capacity) { this.capacity = capacity; head.next = tail; tail.prev = head; }

    void open(int id) {
        size++;                                   // the counter changes first
        if (size > capacity) throw new IllegalStateException("full");
        Node n = new Node(id);
        byId.put(id, n);
        n.prev = tail.prev; n.next = tail;
        tail.prev.next = n; tail.prev = n;
    }

    void close(int id) {
        Node n = byId.get(id);
        n.prev.next = n.next; n.next.prev = n.prev;
        byId.remove(id);
        size--;
    }
}

Each method is short, and each one works when the call succeeds. Opening a fourth tab with capacity three raises the exception, as required. The method has already incremented size before it checks the limit.

Predict first: The strip holds tabs 4, 7 and 9 with capacity 3. The call open(5) throws. Then close(7) succeeds. What do size and the list say, and does open(5) work now?

After the failed open(5), size is 4 while the list still holds three tabs. After close(7), size is 3 and the list holds two tabs. A second open(5) throws again, because size is already 3 and the increment makes it 4. The counter now disagrees with the list for good.

Bottleneck

The Cost Of Scattered Updates

Every operation here runs in O(1) time, so speed is not the problem. The cost is that correctness depends on each method remembering every field. With three fields and two operations, a reader can still check the code by eye. A real class has open, close, reopenLast, moveToEnd and clear, and each of them must touch the links, the table and the counter in a consistent way.

The number of places that can go wrong grows with the number of operations times the number of fields. A fix in one method does not reach the others, because each carries its own copy of the same edit. The failed call above shows a second cost. A method that changes a field and then discovers a problem has no cheap way back. The caller sees an exception, but the object keeps the half-finished change, and every later call starts from a wrong state.

Key idea

One Owner For Each Repair

Naming The Facts That Must Agree

The fields of a class encode one structure in several forms. Here the list gives the order, the table gives lookup by id, and size gives the count. The class keeps one statement true between calls. The list, the table and the counter describe the same set of tabs, and size equals the number of nodes between the sentinels. That statement is the invariant of the class, and every operation must leave it true when it returns, whether it succeeded or threw.

Giving Each Change One Owner

Instead of letting every public operation edit every field, give each cross-field change exactly one owner, which is a private method that performs the whole change. A method named attach links a node into the list, adds it to the table and increments size in one place. A method named detach does the reverse. The public operations never touch those fields directly. They decide what to do and then call the owner. A new operation reuses the owner and cannot forget the counter, because it never writes the counter. When the repair of a link or count is wrong, only one method needs a fix.

Checking Before Changing Anything

The second half of the idea covers failure. Every rejection happens before the first write. The operation first runs its precheck, which reads the state and tests every condition that could make the call illegal. Only after all checks pass does it call the owner. A rejected call then leaves every field exactly as it found it, and no undo code is needed, because nothing was done yet.

State

Fields That Describe One Collection

The tab strip has five fields: head, tail, byId, size and capacity. The invariant ties the list between head and tail to byId and size. Each entity below has a single job. The constructor sets head, tail and capacity, and only the owner methods attach and detach change the links, byId and size afterwards.

  • head and tail are sentinel nodes that hold no tab and always exist. The constructor links them to each other, and a real node always has a neighbour on each side.
  • byId maps a tab id to its node, so the lookup table holds exactly the ids that appear in the list.
  • size counts the real nodes and changes by one inside attach and detach only.
  • capacity is the fixed limit and never changes after construction.

The invariant can be checked at any moment between calls by walking the list from head.next to tail. The number of nodes seen must equal size and byId.size().

Walkthrough

Tracing Six Calls On Three Slots

The Version With One Owner

Run the script open 4, open 7, open 9, open 5, close 7, open 5 on a strip with capacity 3. The first three calls pass the precheck. Each one calls attach, so the list, the table and size grow together to three.

The fourth call, open 5, fails the precheck because size equals capacity. It throws before attach runs. The list, the table and size keep their values. The call to close 7 reaches detach, which unlinks the node, removes id 7 from the table and lowers size to two. The last open 5 now passes the precheck and ends with the list 4, 9, 5 and size 3.

The Version With Scattered Edits

The same script on the naive code shows the drift. The fourth call increments size to 4 and then throws, so the list holds three tabs while the counter says four. The call to close 7 lowers the counter to 3 with only two tabs left. The final open 5 increments the counter to 4, exceeds the capacity and throws, although the strip has room. The step notes below show the counter and the real list length side by side.

Code

Implementing The Strip With Two Owners

final class OpenTabs {
    private static final class Node {
        final int id; Node prev, next;
        Node(int id) { this.id = id; }
    }
    private final Node head = new Node(-1), tail = new Node(-1);   // sentinels, never removed
    private final java.util.Map<Integer, Node> byId = new java.util.HashMap<>();
    private final int capacity;
    private int size;

    OpenTabs(int capacity) { this.capacity = capacity; head.next = tail; tail.prev = head; }

    // Owner of every insertion: links, table and count change together here and nowhere else.
    private void attach(Node n) {
        n.prev = tail.prev; n.next = tail;
        tail.prev.next = n; tail.prev = n;
        byId.put(n.id, n);
        size++;
    }

    // Owner of every removal: the mirror of attach.
    private void detach(Node n) {
        n.prev.next = n.next; n.next.prev = n.prev;
        byId.remove(n.id);
        size--;
    }

    void open(int id) {
        if (byId.containsKey(id)) throw new IllegalArgumentException("already open");   // precheck
        if (size == capacity) throw new IllegalStateException("full");                  // precheck
        attach(new Node(id));                                                           // first write
    }

    void close(int id) {
        Node n = byId.get(id);
        if (n == null) throw new IllegalArgumentException("not open");                  // precheck
        detach(n);                                                                      // first write
    }

    int size() { return size; }
}

Both public operations follow one shape: test every condition, then make one call to an owner. Each operation runs in O(1) time with O(1) extra space. The only allocation, the Node created in open, happens after the checks pass, so a rejected call allocates nothing that stays reachable.

When to use it

Applying One Owner Per Field Group

Recognizing The Pattern

Look for a class where two or more fields must change together. Typical cases are a list with a size counter, a map next to a list, or an array next to an index table. In each case the class keeps an invariant, which is a fact about the fields that must hold whenever no operation is running. Write that fact as one sentence before you write any method.

Then name the private method that owns each change to the fields. The test is simple. Search the class for each field, and every write must sit inside its owner. Each public operation reads the state, checks its conditions and then calls an owner once or a fixed number of times.

A False Friend And A Java Hazard

A false friend is a technique that looks like the right fix and fails in a quiet way. The nearest one here is a try block with a catch that undoes the edits after a failure. The undo code is a second copy of the repair, and it must itself run without error. It also misses any field that a later change adds. Checking first is simpler than rolling back.

The Java hazard is the exception thrown halfway through a mutation. Java does not restore fields when an exception leaves a method. Every assignment that ran before the throw stays in place, and an uncaught exception from the middle of a method leaves the object half updated.

Chapter 33 reuses this pattern in the cache lessons, where a map and a list must agree after every call.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Size Ownership

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design a bounded sequence of nonnegative int values stored in an array of fixed capacity. It has the operations insertAt(index, v), add(v) and removeAt(index). Field size counts the stored values. Write two private methods, openGap and closeGap, that shift values and are the only code allowed to write size. A rejected call changes nothing. insertAt and add return true on success and false when rejected. removeAt returns the removed value, or -1 when rejected.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Capacity is 1 <= capacity <= 1000, fixed at construction.
  • Values are int in 0..10^6.
  • Index for insertAt is valid in 0..size, and for removeAt it is valid in 0..size-1.
  • Rejection covers an invalid index and a full sequence, and leaves size and every stored value unchanged.

Example 1. Input capacity 3 and the calls add(5), add(6) and add(7) return true. Then add(8), and output false with size 3. Then removeAt(0) gives output 5 with size 2.

Example 2. Input the stored values [5,6] with capacity 3 and the call insertAt(1, 9), output [5,9,6] with size 3. Then removeAt(5) gives output -1 and size stays 3.

Prerequisites. The owner and precheck idea in this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. The count moves out of the public operations and into the two shifting helpers, so every successful call changes size exactly once.

Hint

Which two methods move values and therefore know how many values exist afterward? How many writes to size should one successful call make?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

The public operations validate first and then call a helper. insertAt accepts an index in 0..size while the array has room, so it rejects everything else before any write. Its helper openGap shifts the values from the end down to the index one slot to the right and then increments size. removeAt accepts an index in 0..size-1, reads the value, and calls closeGap, which shifts the later values one slot left and decrements size.

The invariant is that data[0..size-1] holds the stored values in order. Only the two helpers write size, so each successful call changes it exactly once, and a rejected call never reaches a helper. The harness counts writes through a field sizeWrites and compares it with the number of successful calls.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n) per insert or removal, because the helpers shift up to size values, and O(1) for add at the end.
  • Space is O(1) beyond the fixed array, because each call keeps a few integer locals.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;

public final class SizeOwnership {
    static final class IntSeq {
        private final int[] data;
        private int size;
        int sizeWrites;   // counts every write to size, so the harness can check "exactly once"

        IntSeq(int capacity) { data = new int[capacity]; }

        /**
         * Shifts data[index..size-1] one slot right and grows size; the only insertion owner.
         * Time: O(size). Space: O(1). Invariant: data[0..size-1] stays contiguous.
         */
        private void openGap(int index) {
            // Walk from the end so each value moves before its slot is overwritten.
            for (int i = size; i > index; i--) data[i] = data[i - 1];
            // The count changes here and nowhere else on the insertion path.
            size++;
            sizeWrites++;
        }

        /**
         * Shifts data[index+1..size-1] one slot left and shrinks size; the only removal owner.
         * Time: O(size). Space: O(1). Invariant: data[0..size-1] stays contiguous.
         */
        private void closeGap(int index) {
            // Walk forward so each value moves into a slot that is already free.
            for (int i = index; i < size - 1; i++) data[i] = data[i + 1];
            size--;
            sizeWrites++;
        }

        /** Inserts v at index. Time: O(size). Space: O(1). Rejects before any write. */
        boolean insertAt(int index, int v) {
            // Precheck: bad index or full array, so a rejected call never reaches the owner.
            if (index < 0 || index > size || size == data.length) return false;
            openGap(index);
            // The slot is free now, so the value lands without losing a neighbour.
            data[index] = v;
            return true;
        }

        /** Appends v. Time: O(1). Space: O(1). */
        boolean add(int v) { return insertAt(size, v); }

        /** Removes and returns the value at index, or -1 when rejected. Time: O(size). Space: O(1). */
        int removeAt(int index) {
            // Precheck before the first read of data.
            if (index < 0 || index >= size) return -1;
            int v = data[index];
            closeGap(index);
            return v;
        }

        int size() { return size; }
        int get(int i) { return data[i]; }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: three adds succeed, the fourth is rejected, then removeAt(0) returns 5.
        IntSeq s = new IntSeq(3);
        if (!s.add(5) || !s.add(6) || !s.add(7)) throw new AssertionError("adds");
        if (s.add(8) || s.size() != 3) throw new AssertionError("full add must be rejected");
        if (s.removeAt(0) != 5 || s.size() != 2) throw new AssertionError("removeAt");
        // Example 2: middle insert and an invalid removal.
        IntSeq t = new IntSeq(3);
        t.add(5); t.add(6);
        if (!t.insertAt(1, 9) || t.size() != 3 || t.get(0) != 5 || t.get(1) != 9 || t.get(2) != 6) throw new AssertionError("insertAt");
        if (t.removeAt(5) != -1 || t.size() != 3) throw new AssertionError("invalid removal");
        // Random operation scripts against a plain ArrayList model.
        Random rnd = new Random(3302);
        for (int round = 0; round < 200; round++) {
            int cap = 1 + rnd.nextInt(8);
            IntSeq seq = new IntSeq(cap);
            List<Integer> model = new ArrayList<>();
            int successes = 0;
            for (int step = 0; step < 200; step++) {
                int kind = rnd.nextInt(3);
                int idx = rnd.nextInt(cap + 3) - 1;
                int v = rnd.nextInt(100);
                if (kind == 0) {
                    // Model accepts the insert only for a valid index on a non-full list.
                    boolean ok = idx >= 0 && idx <= model.size() && model.size() < cap;
                    if (ok) { model.add(idx, v); successes++; }
                    if (seq.insertAt(idx, v) != ok) throw new AssertionError("insert result");
                } else if (kind == 1) {
                    boolean ok = model.size() < cap;
                    if (ok) { model.add(v); successes++; }
                    if (seq.add(v) != ok) throw new AssertionError("add result");
                } else {
                    boolean ok = idx >= 0 && idx < model.size();
                    int expected = ok ? model.remove(idx) : -1;
                    if (ok) successes++;
                    if (seq.removeAt(idx) != expected) throw new AssertionError("remove result");
                }
                // Contents, size and the exactly-once write count must match after every call.
                if (seq.size() != model.size()) throw new AssertionError("size");
                for (int i = 0; i < model.size(); i++) if (seq.get(i) != model.get(i)) throw new AssertionError("content");
                if (seq.sizeWrites != successes) throw new AssertionError("size written once per success");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Sentinel List Links

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Design a doubly linked list of nonnegative int values with a head sentinel and a tail sentinel. Only two private methods may change links: detach(node) and attachAfter(position, node). Provide addFirst, addLast, removeFirst, removeLast, remove(node) and moveToFront(node). The add operations return the new node. The two remove operations that take no node return the removed value, or -1 on an empty list. Every operation reaches the links only through the two helpers.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Values are int in 0..10^6, and duplicates are allowed.
  • Length is at most 10^5 nodes.
  • Node argument is a node currently in this list, so no check for foreign nodes is needed.
  • Empty list has head.next == tail and tail.prev == head.

Example 1. Input addLast(1), addLast(2) and addLast(3), output the list [1,2,3]. Then moveToFront on the node of 3 gives output [3,1,2], and removeLast() gives output 2.

Example 2. Input removeFirst() on an empty list, output -1, and the sentinels still point at each other.

Prerequisites. The size-ownership exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. The shifting array becomes linked nodes, and every link edit moves into one detach method and one attach method.

Hint

Which operation is just a detach followed by an attach? Which pointer pairs must change when a node leaves, and which when it enters?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

Two sentinels remove every special case for the first and last real node, because each real node always has a neighbour on both sides. detach joins the neighbours of a node, and attachAfter splices a node between a position and that position's successor. Each helper also adjusts size, so the count has one owner per direction.

Every public operation becomes a short composition. addFirst attaches after the head, addLast attaches after tail.prev, and the two removals detach head.next or tail.prev. moveToFront detaches the node and attaches it after the head, so it needs no link code of its own. The invariant is that walking forward from the head and backward from the tail visits the same nodes, and size equals their count.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) per operation, because each one performs a fixed number of pointer writes through the helpers.
  • Space is O(1) extra per call, and the list itself stores one node per value.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;

public final class SentinelLinks {
    static final class Node {
        int val; Node prev, next;
    }

    static final class Chain {
        final Node head = new Node(), tail = new Node();
        private int size;

        Chain() { head.next = tail; tail.prev = head; }

        /** Unlinks n from its neighbours. Time: O(1). Space: O(1). The only code that removes links. */
        private void detach(Node n) {
            // Join the two neighbours around n.
            n.prev.next = n.next;
            n.next.prev = n.prev;
            n.prev = null; n.next = null;
            size--;
        }

        /** Links n directly after at. Time: O(1). Space: O(1). The only code that adds links. */
        private void attachAfter(Node at, Node n) {
            // Point n at both neighbours first, then point the neighbours at n.
            n.prev = at; n.next = at.next;
            at.next.prev = n;
            at.next = n;
            size++;
        }

        Node addFirst(int v) { Node n = new Node(); n.val = v; attachAfter(head, n); return n; }
        Node addLast(int v) { Node n = new Node(); n.val = v; attachAfter(tail.prev, n); return n; }

        int removeFirst() {
            // Empty list: head.next is the tail sentinel, so reject before detaching.
            if (size == 0) return -1;
            Node n = head.next; detach(n); return n.val;
        }

        int removeLast() {
            if (size == 0) return -1;
            Node n = tail.prev; detach(n); return n.val;
        }

        void remove(Node n) { detach(n); }

        /** Moves n to the front using only the two helpers. Time: O(1). Space: O(1). */
        void moveToFront(Node n) { detach(n); attachAfter(head, n); }

        int size() { return size; }

        Node nodeAt(int i) { Node n = head.next; while (i-- > 0) n = n.next; return n; }

        /** Reads the list forward and checks the backward walk and size agree. */
        List<Integer> toList() {
            List<Integer> fwd = new ArrayList<>();
            for (Node n = head.next; n != tail; n = n.next) fwd.add(n.val);
            List<Integer> back = new ArrayList<>();
            for (Node n = tail.prev; n != head; n = n.prev) back.add(0, n.val);
            if (!fwd.equals(back)) throw new AssertionError("forward and backward walks differ");
            if (fwd.size() != size) throw new AssertionError("size differs from node count");
            return fwd;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: build [1,2,3], move 3 to the front, remove the last value.
        Chain c = new Chain();
        c.addLast(1); c.addLast(2); Node three = c.addLast(3);
        if (!c.toList().equals(List.of(1, 2, 3))) throw new AssertionError("build");
        c.moveToFront(three);
        if (!c.toList().equals(List.of(3, 1, 2))) throw new AssertionError("moveToFront");
        if (c.removeLast() != 2 || !c.toList().equals(List.of(3, 1))) throw new AssertionError("removeLast");
        // Example 2: empty list removal leaves the sentinels linked to each other.
        Chain e = new Chain();
        if (e.removeFirst() != -1 || e.head.next != e.tail || e.tail.prev != e.head) throw new AssertionError("empty");
        // Random operation scripts against an ArrayList model.
        Random rnd = new Random(3303);
        for (int round = 0; round < 200; round++) {
            Chain ch = new Chain();
            List<Integer> model = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = 0; step < 200; step++) {
                int kind = rnd.nextInt(6);
                int v = rnd.nextInt(1000);
                if (kind == 0) { ch.addFirst(v); model.add(0, v); }
                else if (kind == 1) { ch.addLast(v); model.add(v); }
                else if (kind == 2) {
                    int expected = model.isEmpty() ? -1 : model.remove(0);
                    if (ch.removeFirst() != expected) throw new AssertionError("removeFirst");
                } else if (kind == 3) {
                    int expected = model.isEmpty() ? -1 : model.remove(model.size() - 1);
                    if (ch.removeLast() != expected) throw new AssertionError("removeLast");
                } else if (!model.isEmpty()) {
                    int i = rnd.nextInt(model.size());
                    if (kind == 4) { ch.moveToFront(ch.nodeAt(i)); model.add(0, model.remove(i)); }
                    else { ch.remove(ch.nodeAt(i)); model.remove(i); }
                }
                // After every call the list must match the model and keep both walks consistent.
                if (!ch.toList().equals(model)) throw new AssertionError("list differs from model");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Failed Mutation

Author exercise

Problem Specification. A bounded list stores up to capacity nonnegative int values in an array and tracks size and a counter modCount that rises by one on every successful change. The operation insertAt(index, v) throws IndexOutOfBoundsException when index < 0 or index > size. It throws IllegalStateException when the list is full and the index is valid. A thrown call must leave size, modCount and every array slot exactly as before the call. Implement the operation so that this holds.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Capacity is 1 <= capacity <= 1000, fixed at construction.
  • Values are int in 0..10^6.
  • Check order tests the index first and fullness second.
  • Rejected call performs no write to size, modCount or the array.

Example 1. Input capacity 2, stored values [4,5] and the call insertAt(0, 9), output an IllegalStateException. The list still reads [4,5], and size and modCount keep their values.

Example 2. Input capacity 3, stored values [4,5] and the call insertAt(4, 9), output an IndexOutOfBoundsException, and nothing changes.

Prerequisites. The two exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The edge case is the rejected call, so every condition moves ahead of the first write.

Hint

What does the method read before it writes anything? Which version of the method increments size first, and what does a later throw leave behind?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

The method checks the index and then fullness, and it throws before it writes anything. Only after both tests pass does it shift the values, store the new one, and update size and modCount. A thrown call therefore touches no field, and the caller can catch the exception and keep using the object.

The harness also includes a flawed variant that increments size first and then tests the capacity. Java keeps that increment after the exception leaves the method, so the flawed list reports a size larger than its capacity. The random check takes a snapshot of size, modCount and every array slot before each call and compares it after each rejected call.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n) for a successful insert, because it shifts up to size values, and O(1) for a rejected call.
  • Space is O(1) extra, and a rejected call allocates nothing.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.Arrays;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;

public final class FailedMutation {
    static final class Bounded {
        final int[] data;
        int size, modCount;

        Bounded(int capacity) { data = new int[capacity]; }

        /**
         * Inserts v at index; throws before any write when the call is illegal.
         * Time: O(size) on success, O(1) on rejection. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: a thrown call leaves size, modCount and data unchanged.
         */
        void insertAt(int index, int v) {
            // Precheck 1: index range, tested first by contract.
            if (index < 0 || index > size) throw new IndexOutOfBoundsException("index " + index);
            // Precheck 2: capacity, tested before the first write.
            if (size == data.length) throw new IllegalStateException("full");
            // All writes happen below this line.
            for (int i = size; i > index; i--) data[i] = data[i - 1];
            data[index] = v;
            size++;
            modCount++;
        }
    }

    /** The hazard: the count changes before the test, and Java does not undo it on a throw. */
    static final class Flawed {
        final int[] data;
        int size;

        Flawed(int capacity) { data = new int[capacity]; }

        void insertAt(int index, int v) {
            size++;
            if (size > data.length) throw new IllegalStateException("full");
            for (int i = size - 1; i > index; i--) data[i] = data[i - 1];
            data[index] = v;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: a full list rejects a valid index and keeps every field.
        Bounded b = new Bounded(2);
        b.insertAt(0, 5); b.insertAt(0, 4);
        int mods = b.modCount;
        try { b.insertAt(0, 9); throw new AssertionError("must throw"); } catch (IllegalStateException expected) { }
        if (b.size != 2 || b.modCount != mods || b.data[0] != 4 || b.data[1] != 5) throw new AssertionError("full rejection changed state");
        // Example 2: an index beyond size throws the other exception type.
        Bounded c = new Bounded(3);
        c.insertAt(0, 5); c.insertAt(0, 4);
        try { c.insertAt(4, 9); throw new AssertionError("must throw"); } catch (IndexOutOfBoundsException expected) { }
        if (c.size != 2 || c.data[2] != 0) throw new AssertionError("index rejection changed state");
        // The flawed variant keeps its increment after the exception, so size exceeds capacity.
        Flawed f = new Flawed(1);
        f.insertAt(0, 7);
        try { f.insertAt(0, 8); throw new AssertionError("must throw"); } catch (IllegalStateException expected) { }
        if (f.size != 2) throw new AssertionError("flawed size must show the half-finished update");
        // Random scripts: valid and invalid calls against an ArrayList model with snapshots.
        Random rnd = new Random(3304);
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            int cap = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6);
            Bounded list = new Bounded(cap);
            List<Integer> model = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = 0; step < 100; step++) {
                int idx = rnd.nextInt(cap + 4) - 1;
                int v = rnd.nextInt(100);
                int sizeBefore = list.size, modsBefore = list.modCount;
                int[] dataBefore = list.data.clone();
                boolean badIndex = idx < 0 || idx > model.size();
                boolean full = model.size() == cap;
                try {
                    list.insertAt(idx, v);
                    if (badIndex || full) throw new AssertionError("should have thrown");
                    model.add(idx, v);
                } catch (IndexOutOfBoundsException ex) {
                    if (!badIndex) throw new AssertionError("wrong exception");
                    // Rejected: every field must equal its snapshot.
                    if (list.size != sizeBefore || list.modCount != modsBefore || !Arrays.equals(list.data, dataBefore)) throw new AssertionError("state changed");
                } catch (IllegalStateException ex) {
                    if (badIndex || !full) throw new AssertionError("wrong exception");
                    if (list.size != sizeBefore || list.modCount != modsBefore || !Arrays.equals(list.data, dataBefore)) throw new AssertionError("state changed");
                }
                // The accepted contents must always match the model.
                if (list.size != model.size()) throw new AssertionError("size");
                for (int i = 0; i < model.size(); i++) if (list.data[i] != model.get(i)) throw new AssertionError("content");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

LC 146 LRU Cache

LeetCode 146

Problem Specification. Design a cache with a fixed capacity that maps nonnegative int keys to nonnegative int values. The operation get(key) returns the value and marks the key as most recently used, or returns -1 when the key is absent. The operation put(key, value) stores the pair and marks the key as most recently used. When the key is new and the cache is full, put first removes the least recently used key and returns that evicted key. In every other case put returns -1. All list edits live in two private helpers, detach and attach. After every call the number of map entries equals the number of list nodes.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Capacity is 1 <= capacity <= 3000.
  • Keys and values are int in 0..10^5, so -1 is never a real key or value.
  • Updating an existing key never evicts and returns -1.
  • Cost of each operation is O(1) on average.

Example 1. Input capacity 3 and the calls put(5,50), put(6,60), put(7,70), get(5), put(8,80), put(7,71), put(9,90), output -1, -1, -1, 50, 6, -1, 5.

Example 2. Input capacity 1 and the calls put(1,1), put(2,2), put(2,3), get(1), get(2), output -1, 1, -1, -1, 3.

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. put reports the evicted key instead of returning nothing, and the list and map edits are fenced into two helpers.

Hint

Which fields describe the same set of keys? Which two helpers can be the only code that touches both the list and the map?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

The map finds a node by key, and the doubly linked list orders the nodes from most to least recently used. Two private helpers own every change. attach puts a node right after the head sentinel and registers its key in the map. detach unlinks a node and removes its key from the map. The map size and the list length therefore change together, and the harness asserts that they match after every call.

get detaches and re-attaches the node, which refreshes its position. put on an existing key updates the value and refreshes the node the same way. put on a new key first checks map.size() == capacity. When the cache is full, it detaches tail.prev, which is the least recently used node, and remembers its key as the return value. Only then does it attach the new node, so the cache never exceeds its capacity.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) average per call, because each call does a fixed number of hash operations and pointer writes.
  • Space is O(capacity), since the map and the list store one entry per cached key, up to the limit.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Map;
import java.util.Random;

public final class LruEvictedKey {
    static final class Node {
        final int key; int val; Node prev, next;
        Node(int key, int val) { this.key = key; this.val = val; }
    }

    static final class Cache {
        private final int capacity;
        private final Map<Integer, Node> map = new HashMap<>();
        private final Node head = new Node(-1, -1), tail = new Node(-1, -1);

        Cache(int capacity) { this.capacity = capacity; head.next = tail; tail.prev = head; }

        /** Unlinks n and removes its key from the map. Time: O(1) average. Space: O(1). */
        private void detach(Node n) {
            n.prev.next = n.next;
            n.next.prev = n.prev;
            map.remove(n.key);
        }

        /** Links n after the head and registers its key. Time: O(1) average. Space: O(1). */
        private void attach(Node n) {
            n.prev = head; n.next = head.next;
            head.next.prev = n;
            head.next = n;
            map.put(n.key, n);
        }
