        int get(int key) {
            Node n = map.get(key);
            // Absent key: nothing to refresh.
            if (n == null) return -1;
            detach(n); attach(n);
            return n.val;
        }

        /** Stores the pair; returns the evicted key or -1. Time: O(1) average. Space: O(1). */
        int put(int key, int val) {
            Node n = map.get(key);
            if (n != null) {
                // Update in place and refresh recency; no eviction for an existing key.
                n.val = val;
                detach(n); attach(n);
                return -1;
            }
            int evicted = -1;
            // Full cache: the node before the tail sentinel is least recently used.
            if (map.size() == capacity) {
                Node lru = tail.prev;
                detach(lru);
                evicted = lru.key;
            }
            attach(new Node(key, val));
            return evicted;
        }

        int mapSize() { return map.size(); }

        int listLength() { int c = 0; for (Node n = head.next; n != tail; n = n.next) c++; return c; }
    }

    /** Naive model: a recency-ordered list of {key, value}, most recent first. */
    static final class Model {
        final int capacity; final List<int[]> items = new ArrayList<>();
        Model(int capacity) { this.capacity = capacity; }
        int indexOf(int key) { for (int i = 0; i < items.size(); i++) if (items.get(i)[0] == key) return i; return -1; }
        int get(int key) {
            int i = indexOf(key);
            if (i < 0) return -1;
            int[] e = items.remove(i); items.add(0, e);
            return e[1];
        }
        int put(int key, int val) {
            int i = indexOf(key);
            if (i >= 0) { items.remove(i); items.add(0, new int[] {key, val}); return -1; }
            int evicted = -1;
            if (items.size() == capacity) evicted = items.remove(items.size() - 1)[0];
            items.add(0, new int[] {key, val});
            return evicted;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: capacity 3 with a get, an eviction of 6, an update and an eviction of 5.
        Cache c = new Cache(3);
        if (c.put(5, 50) != -1 || c.put(6, 60) != -1 || c.put(7, 70) != -1) throw new AssertionError("fill");
        if (c.get(5) != 50) throw new AssertionError("get 5");
        if (c.put(8, 80) != 6) throw new AssertionError("evict 6");
        if (c.put(7, 71) != -1) throw new AssertionError("update");
        if (c.put(9, 90) != 5) throw new AssertionError("evict 5");
        if (c.mapSize() != c.listLength()) throw new AssertionError("map and list sizes");
        // Example 2: capacity 1.
        Cache d = new Cache(1);
        if (d.put(1, 1) != -1 || d.put(2, 2) != 1 || d.put(2, 3) != -1) throw new AssertionError("capacity one puts");
        if (d.get(1) != -1 || d.get(2) != 3) throw new AssertionError("capacity one gets");
        // Random scripts: results and the map/list size match after every call.
        Random rnd = new Random(3305);
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            int cap = 1 + rnd.nextInt(5);
            Cache cache = new Cache(cap);
            Model model = new Model(cap);
            for (int step = 0; step < 200; step++) {
                int key = rnd.nextInt(9), val = rnd.nextInt(1000);
                if (rnd.nextBoolean()) {
                    if (cache.put(key, val) != model.put(key, val)) throw new AssertionError("put result");
                } else {
                    if (cache.get(key) != model.get(key)) throw new AssertionError("get result");
                }
                // The synchronization invariant: map entries, list nodes and the model agree.
                if (cache.mapSize() != cache.listLength()) throw new AssertionError("map size differs from list length");
                if (cache.mapSize() != model.items.size()) throw new AssertionError("size differs from model");
            }
        }
    }
}verified

Pair A Map With Ordered Storage

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Why Disconnects Slow A Sampling Service

A gateway tracks the id of every open connection. A health monitor asks for the connection at position i so it can sample one at random. A disconnect handler removes the id of a closed connection. With 200,000 open connections and thousands of disconnects per second, the handler becomes the busiest code in the process. Profiling shows that nearly all of its time sits in one call that removes an id from a list.

The list answers the sampling question well, because reading position i takes one step. It answers the removal question badly, because the handler must find the id first and then close the gap. This lesson asks how one class can answer both questions in constant time, and what it must keep true between calls.

Simple approach

Keeping Only A List

The tempting design stores the ids in one ArrayList and nothing else. Insertion appends to the end. Sampling reads a position. Removal searches for the id and deletes it.

import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;

final class ListOnlyIds {
    private final List<Integer> ids = new ArrayList<>();

    boolean insert(int id) {
        if (ids.contains(id)) return false;   // a linear scan on every insert
        ids.add(id);
        return true;
    }

    boolean remove(int id) {
        return ids.remove(Integer.valueOf(id));   // a linear scan, then a shift of the tail
    }

    int getAt(int i) {
        return ids.get(i);
    }
}

The class is correct. With ids [40, 17, 93, 52, 8], calling remove(17) leaves [40, 93, 52, 8], and getAt(1) returns 93.

Predict first: Which operations of ListOnlyIds cost O(1) with 200,000 ids, and which cost O(n)?

Only getAt costs O(1), because it reads one position. insert must scan for a duplicate, and remove must scan for the id and then shift every later element left, so both cost O(n).

Bottleneck

Two Linear Scans Hide In One Class

The list gives position access in O(1) but gives no way to ask where an id sits. The method contains compares the id against elements one by one, so it takes O(n) time. The method remove(Object) does the same scan and then shifts the elements after the gap, so a removal near the front moves almost n elements. With n equal to 200,000 and thousands of calls per second, the scans dominate the whole service.

A HashSet fixes the lookup, because membership takes O(1) on average. A HashSet has no positions, so it cannot answer getAt(i). Keeping both a set and a list fails differently. The removal from the list still scans and shifts, and the two collections can disagree about which ids exist. The class needs one structure whose strength is lookup, one whose strength is position, and a rule that connects them.

Key idea

Letting Each Structure Answer One Question

Dividing The Work

A HashMap from id to slot answers "where is this id" in O(1) on average. An ArrayList of ids answers "which id is at slot i" in O(1) and appends in amortized O(1). Neither structure alone answers both questions. Together they do, as long as every entry of the map points at the slot that really holds that id. Each stored fact is a cross-reference, a fact stored in one structure that names a location in the other.

The invariant of the class is that for every id in the list, slotOf.get(id) equals the position of that id in the list, and the map holds no other entries. Insertion keeps it by appending the id and recording its slot. A removal must break it for a moment and then restore it before the operation returns.

Closing The Gap Without Shifting

Deleting from the middle of a list shifts every later element, which costs O(n). The standard remedy is swap with last. Copy the final element into the freed slot, then delete the final slot, which costs O(1) because nothing sits after it. The order of the ids changes, since one id jumped to a new slot. The contract must say so, and this lesson makes the jump visible by returning the id that moved.

Restoring The Moved Entry

The id that jumped now sits at a different position, so its map entry is wrong. The repair is one put that rewrites that entry to the freed slot. The id being removed also needs its entry deleted. When the removed id was itself the final element, nothing jumps. The repair must then not run in a way that writes the removed id back into the map. Placing the delete of the removed entry after the repair of the moved entry makes both cases correct.

State

Variables Of The Paired Class

The class holds two fields, and each operation touches both. These entities need names before the trace.

  • values is the ArrayList<Integer> that owns the order and answers getAt(i).
  • slotOf is the HashMap<Integer, Integer> from a value to its position in values.
  • slot is the position of the value being removed, read from slotOf.
  • last is the value at the final position of values, which is the one that moves. The trace marks its position with the pointer lastSlot.
  • moved is the returned result: last when it changed position, or -1 when no value moved.

Values are non-negative, so -1 never collides with a real value.

Walkthrough

Tracing Two Removals

Removing A Value From The Middle

Start with values [40, 17, 93, 52, 8]. The call remove(17) first reads slotOf.get(17), which gives slot 1. The final value is 8. The class copies 8 into slot 1, so the list reads [40, 8, 93, 52, 8] for one moment, with 8 stored twice. Then it writes slotOf.put(8, 1), which is the repair. It deletes the entry for 17 and shrinks the list. The result is [40, 8, 93, 52], and the call returns 8.

Removing The Final Value

The second case removes the value that is itself last. The call remove(8) reads slot 4, and the final value is also 8. The copy writes 8 into slot 4, which changes nothing. The repair writes slotOf.put(8, 4), and the next step deletes the entry for 8, so the stale write disappears. The list shrinks to [40, 17, 93, 52], and the call returns -1 because no value moved.

If the delete ran before the repair, the repair would put the entry for 8 back into the map. The map would then name a slot that no longer exists.

Code

Implementing The Paired Class

import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Map;

final class PairedIds {
    private final List<Integer> values = new ArrayList<>();
    private final Map<Integer, Integer> slotOf = new HashMap<>();

    boolean insert(int v) {
        if (slotOf.containsKey(v)) return false;
        slotOf.put(v, values.size());   // record the slot before the append changes size
        values.add(v);
        return true;
    }

    int remove(int v) {
        Integer slot = slotOf.get(v);
        if (slot == null) return -1;                 // absent: nothing changes, nothing moves
        int last = values.get(values.size() - 1);
        values.set(slot, last);                      // swap with last: close the gap in O(1)
        slotOf.put(last, slot);                      // repair the moved entry first
        slotOf.remove(v);                            // then delete the removed entry
        values.remove(values.size() - 1);            // index form: drops the final slot
        return last == v ? -1 : last;
    }

    int getAt(int i) {
        if (i < 0 || i >= values.size()) throw new IndexOutOfBoundsException("slot " + i);
        return values.get(i);
    }
}

Every operation runs in O(1) on average. The values.remove(values.size() - 1) call uses an int argument, so Java treats it as a position and not as an element to find.

When to use it

Recognizing A Paired Design

Spotting The Cue

Look for a contract that asks for lookup by key and also for a position, an order or a neighbor, each in constant time. Hash-based sets and maps give the first and have no order to read by position. Arrays and linked lists give the second and cannot find a key quickly. When both questions appear in one contract, pair the two structures and write down which one answers which question.

An invariant is a fact about the state that holds before and after every operation. For a pair, the invariant says that every cross-reference names the right location. Check it at every exit of every operation, including the early returns and the empty and single-element cases. A linked list works the same way. The map holds the node instead of a slot, and a detach rewrites two neighbor links, prev.next and next.prev, instead of one entry.

Naming The False Friends

A false friend is a tool that looks like a fit and breaks the contract. LinkedHashSet gives O(1) lookup and O(1) removal, but it offers no way to read the element at position i in O(1), so it fails a contract with getAt. An ArrayList plus a HashSet with no slot map still scans to remove. Keeping slots in a map without repairing them after a swap returns the wrong value from getAt later, long after the faulty call.

Two Java hazards belong here. Comparing two Integer objects with == is true for small values and false for others, so compare slots with equals or unbox them first. Removing entries from the map while a loop iterates over its keys throws ConcurrentModificationException, so copy the keys before the loop. Lesson 05 reuses this design for randomized sets, and lesson 06 uses a linked list for caches.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Map To List Node

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Implement OrderedKeys, a doubly linked list of distinct non-negative integer keys with a HashMap from each key to its node. The operation append(key) adds the key at the tail and returns false when the key already exists. The operation detach(key) unlinks the node of the key in O(1) and returns the key that followed it, or -1 when the detached node was the tail. The operation order() returns the keys from head to tail. The operation detach of an absent key throws IllegalArgumentException.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Keys are int values in the range 0 to 10^9.
  • Calls number at most 10^5.
  • Cost of append and detach is O(1) on average.
  • Empty list is allowed, and order() returns an empty array.

Example 1. Input: append 5, 9, 2, 7 in order, then call detach(9). Output: the call returns 2, and order() is [5, 2, 7].

Example 2. Input: append 4 to an empty list, then call detach(4) and append(6). Output: detach(4) returns -1, order() is empty after it, and order() is [6] at the end.

Prerequisites. The paired class in this lesson and a doubly linked list.

What This Exercise Changes. First exercise in the sequence: the second structure is a linked list, so the map stores a node and the repair rewrites links.

Hint

Which two neighbors point at the node you remove? What must the list's head and tail become when the node has no previous or no next neighbor?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

The map stores the node of every key, so detach finds the node in O(1) on average and never walks the list. The invariant is that the map holds exactly the keys reachable from the head, and that next and prev links agree with each other. Detaching a node rewrites the link of its previous neighbor, or the head when there is none. It then rewrites the link of its next neighbor, or the tail when there is none. The method returns the key of the next neighbor, which makes the rewritten link observable. The detached key leaves the map in the same call. Iterating over nodeOf.keySet() while detaching throws ConcurrentModificationException, so any loop over keys works on a copy.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) on average for append and detach, because each does a constant number of hash lookups and link writes.
  • Space is O(n) for n live keys, because each key owns one node and one map entry.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.ConcurrentModificationException;
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Map;
import java.util.Random;

public final class MapToListNode {
    private static final class Node {
        final int key;
        Node prev, next;
        Node(int key) { this.key = key; }
    }

    private final Map<Integer, Node> nodeOf = new HashMap<>();
    private Node head, tail;

    /**
     * Adds the key at the tail; returns false when it already exists.
     * Time: O(1) average. Space: O(1) for one node and one map entry.
     * Invariant: nodeOf holds exactly the keys reachable from head.
     */
    boolean append(int key) {
        // A present key must not get a second node, or the map would lose the first one.
        if (nodeOf.containsKey(key)) return false;
        Node n = new Node(key);
        // The new node points back at the old tail.
        n.prev = tail;
        // An empty list makes the node the head; otherwise the old tail links forward to it.
        if (tail == null) head = n; else tail.next = n;
        tail = n;
        // Record the node so a later detach finds it without walking.
        nodeOf.put(key, n);
        return true;
    }

    /**
     * Unlinks the node of the key; returns the key after it, or -1 when it was the tail.
     * Time: O(1) average. Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: after the call, the neighbors of the removed node link to each other.
     */
    int detach(int key) {
        // Removing the entry from the map is the O(1) lookup that replaces a walk.
        Node n = nodeOf.remove(key);
        if (n == null) throw new IllegalArgumentException("absent key " + key);
        // The previous side: the head moves forward when there is no previous node.
        if (n.prev == null) head = n.next; else n.prev.next = n.next;
        // The next side: the tail moves back when there is no next node.
        if (n.next == null) tail = n.prev; else n.next.prev = n.prev;
        // The returned key shows which neighbor now follows the gap.
        return n.next == null ? -1 : n.next.key;
    }

    /** Returns the keys from head to tail. Time: O(n). Space: O(n) for the result. */
    List<Integer> order() {
        List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
        // Walk forward through next links; the loop visits each live node once.
        for (Node n = head; n != null; n = n.next) out.add(n.key);
        return out;
    }

    /** Returns the keys from tail to head. Time: O(n). Space: O(n) for the result. */
    List<Integer> orderBackward() {
        List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
        // Walk backward through prev links to prove both link directions agree.
        for (Node n = tail; n != null; n = n.prev) out.add(0, n.key);
        return out;
    }

    int mapSize() { return nodeOf.size(); }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: detach from the middle returns the next key.
        MapToListNode a = new MapToListNode();
        for (int k : new int[] {5, 9, 2, 7}) a.append(k);
        if (a.detach(9) != 2) throw new AssertionError("example 1 return");
        if (!a.order().equals(List.of(5, 2, 7))) throw new AssertionError("example 1 order");
        // Example 2: a single node leaves the list empty, and the list still accepts appends.
        MapToListNode b = new MapToListNode();
        b.append(4);
        if (b.detach(4) != -1) throw new AssertionError("example 2 return");
        if (!b.order().isEmpty()) throw new AssertionError("example 2 empty");
        b.append(6);
        if (!b.order().equals(List.of(6))) throw new AssertionError("example 2 reuse");
        // Duplicates are rejected and an absent key throws.
        if (a.append(5)) throw new AssertionError("duplicate append");
        boolean threw = false;
        try { a.detach(1234); } catch (IllegalArgumentException e) { threw = true; }
        if (!threw) throw new AssertionError("absent key");
        // Java claim: removing from a map while iterating its keys throws.
        Map<Integer, Integer> m = new HashMap<>(Map.of(1, 1, 2, 2, 3, 3));
        boolean cme = false;
        try { for (int k : m.keySet()) m.remove(k); } catch (ConcurrentModificationException e) { cme = true; }
        if (!cme) throw new AssertionError("map mutation during iteration must throw");
        // Random replay against a plain list model.
        Random rnd = new Random(33);
        MapToListNode s = new MapToListNode();
        List<Integer> model = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int step = 0; step < 20000; step++) {
            if (model.isEmpty() || rnd.nextInt(100) < 55) {
                int k = rnd.nextInt(300);
                boolean expect = !model.contains(k);
                if (s.append(k) != expect) throw new AssertionError("append flag");
                if (expect) model.add(k);
            } else {
                int k = model.get(rnd.nextInt(model.size()));
                int at = model.indexOf(k);
                int expectNext = at == model.size() - 1 ? -1 : model.get(at + 1);
                if (s.detach(k) != expectNext) throw new AssertionError("detach return");
                model.remove(at);
            }
            if (!s.order().equals(model) || !s.orderBackward().equals(model)) throw new AssertionError("order");
            if (s.mapSize() != model.size()) throw new AssertionError("map size");
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Map To Array Index

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Implement IndexedKeys, an array-backed list of distinct non-negative integer keys with a HashMap from each key to its position. The operation add(key) appends the key and returns false when it exists. The operation removeAt(i) deletes the key stored at position i by moving the final key into position i. It returns the key that moved, or -1 when no key moved. The operation slotOf(key) returns the position of the key, or -1 when it is absent. The operation removeAt(i) throws IndexOutOfBoundsException for a position outside 0 to size - 1.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Keys are int values in the range 0 to 10^9.
  • Calls number at most 10^5.
  • Cost of every operation is O(1) on average.
  • Positions after a removal keep every untouched key at its old position.

Example 1. Input: add 30, 11, 64, 25, then call removeAt(1). Output: the call returns 25, the keys read [30, 25, 64], and slotOf(25) is 1.

Example 2. Input: add 30, then call removeAt(0) and add(30). Output: removeAt(0) returns -1 and leaves the structure empty, and add(30) returns true.

Prerequisites. The first exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. The removal request changes from a key to a position, so the map is needed only to repair the entry of the key that moves.

Hint

The caller names a position and not a key. Which key must you read from the array to find out whose map entry changes?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

The caller names a position, so the removed key comes straight from the array and the map is not needed to find it. The map matters only for the key that moves. The method copies the final key into position i, so the removal costs O(1) instead of a shift of the tail. The moved key now sits at position i, so put(last, i) repairs its entry. The removed key's entry is deleted after that, which also handles the case where i is the final position. The array then drops its final slot using the index form of remove. The returned value is the moved key, or -1 when the removed key was the final one.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) on average, because the method does one array read, one array write, two map updates and one tail removal.
  • Space is O(n) for n keys, because the array and the map each hold one record per key.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Map;
import java.util.Random;

public final class MapToArrayIndex {
    private final List<Integer> keys = new ArrayList<>();
    private final Map<Integer, Integer> slots = new HashMap<>();

    /**
     * Appends the key; returns false when it exists.
     * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(1) per key.
     * Invariant: slots.get(k) equals the position of k in keys.
     */
    boolean add(int key) {
        if (slots.containsKey(key)) return false;
        // Record the slot before the append, because the slot equals the old size.
        slots.put(key, keys.size());
        keys.add(key);
        return true;
    }

    /**
     * Removes the key at position i by moving the final key into it; returns the moved key or -1.
     * Time: O(1) average. Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: after the call, every remaining key maps to its true position.
     */
    int removeAt(int i) {
        // Reject positions outside the live range before touching any state.
        if (i < 0 || i >= keys.size()) throw new IndexOutOfBoundsException("position " + i);
        int removed = keys.get(i);
        int lastPos = keys.size() - 1;
        int last = keys.get(lastPos);
        // Overwrite the freed slot with the final key; no tail shift happens.
        keys.set(i, last);
        // Repair the moved key first, so its entry names the new position.
        slots.put(last, i);
        // Delete the removed key last; when removed equals last this drops the repair write.
        slots.remove(removed);
        // The int argument removes by position, which is O(1) at the end of the list.
        keys.remove(lastPos);
        return removed == last ? -1 : last;
    }

    /** Returns the position of the key, or -1 when absent. Time: O(1) average. Space: O(1). */
    int slotOf(int key) {
        Integer s = slots.get(key);
        return s == null ? -1 : s;
    }

    List<Integer> keys() { return keys; }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: removing position 1 moves the final key 25 into it.
        MapToArrayIndex a = new MapToArrayIndex();
        for (int k : new int[] {30, 11, 64, 25}) a.add(k);
        if (a.removeAt(1) != 25) throw new AssertionError("example 1 return");
        if (!a.keys().equals(List.of(30, 25, 64))) throw new AssertionError("example 1 keys");
        if (a.slotOf(25) != 1 || a.slotOf(11) != -1) throw new AssertionError("example 1 slots");
        // Example 2: a single key leaves the structure empty and reusable.
        MapToArrayIndex b = new MapToArrayIndex();
        b.add(30);
        if (b.removeAt(0) != -1 || !b.keys().isEmpty()) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        if (!b.add(30)) throw new AssertionError("example 2 reuse");
        // Out-of-range positions throw.
        boolean threw = false;
        try { b.removeAt(1); } catch (IndexOutOfBoundsException e) { threw = true; }
        if (!threw) throw new AssertionError("range check");
        // Random replay against a list model that shifts on removal and then swaps by hand.
        Random rnd = new Random(34);
        MapToArrayIndex s = new MapToArrayIndex();
        List<Integer> model = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int step = 0; step < 20000; step++) {
            if (model.isEmpty() || rnd.nextInt(100) < 55) {
                int k = rnd.nextInt(400);
                boolean expect = !model.contains(k);
                if (s.add(k) != expect) throw new AssertionError("add flag");
                if (expect) model.add(k);
            } else {
                int i = rnd.nextInt(model.size());
                int fin = model.remove(model.size() - 1);
                int expect = -1;
                if (i < model.size()) { model.set(i, fin); expect = fin; }
                if (s.removeAt(i) != expect) throw new AssertionError("removeAt return");
            }
            if (!s.keys().equals(model)) throw new AssertionError("keys");
            for (int p = 0; p < model.size(); p++)
                if (s.slotOf(model.get(p)) != p) throw new AssertionError("slot of " + model.get(p));
            if (s.slots.size() != model.size()) throw new AssertionError("stale map entry");
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Remove Last Element

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Implement IndexedKeys with the operation removeKey(key), which deletes the key by moving the final key into its slot. The operation returns true when the key existed and false otherwise. After every call, mapSize() must equal size(), and slotOf(k) must equal the position of k for every stored key. The case under test is a removal of the key stored at the final position.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Keys are int values in the range 0 to 10^9.
  • Calls number at most 10^5.
  • Cost of every operation is O(1) on average.
  • Absent key returns false and leaves both structures unchanged.

Example 1. Input: add 8, 3, 6, then call removeKey(6). Output: the call returns true, the keys read [8, 3], slotOf(6) is -1 and mapSize() is 2.

Example 2. Input: add 8, 3, 6, then call removeKey(3) and removeKey(9). Output: the keys read [8, 6], slotOf(6) is 1, mapSize() is 2, and removeKey(9) returns false.

Prerequisites. The two exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The removed key and the moved key can be the same key, so the order of the repair and the delete decides whether a stale entry survives.

Hint

When the removed key is the final key, the move copies it onto itself. Which of the two map updates must run last so the removed key stays out of the map?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

The invariant is that the map holds exactly one entry per stored key and each entry names the true position. A removal by swap with last moves the final key onto the freed slot. When the removed key is the final key, the move copies the key onto itself. The moved key and the removed key are then the same key, so the order of the two map updates decides the result. Repairing first and deleting second leaves the key out of the map, which is correct. Deleting first and repairing second writes the removed key back, and the map keeps an entry for a position that no longer exists. The solution below holds both orders in one class so the assertions can compare them. The faulty order passes every case where the removed key is not last, which is why a test must cover the last position directly.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) on average, because each removal does a constant number of map and list operations.
  • Space is O(n) for n keys, because the list and the map each hold one record per key.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Map;
import java.util.Random;

public final class RemoveLastElement {
    private final List<Integer> keys = new ArrayList<>();
    private final Map<Integer, Integer> slots = new HashMap<>();

    boolean add(int key) {
        if (slots.containsKey(key)) return false;
        slots.put(key, keys.size());
        keys.add(key);
        return true;
    }

    /**
     * Removes the key by moving the final key into its slot; returns false when absent.
     * Time: O(1) average. Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: slots has one correct entry per key and no other entry.
     */
    boolean removeKey(int key) {
        Integer slot = slots.get(key);
        // An absent key changes nothing.
        if (slot == null) return false;
        int last = keys.get(keys.size() - 1);
        // Move the final key into the freed slot; when key equals last this writes it onto itself.
        keys.set(slot, last);
        // Repair first, then delete: the delete must be the final write to the map.
        slots.put(last, slot);
        slots.remove(key);
        keys.remove(keys.size() - 1);
        return true;
    }

    /** Faulty order kept for comparison: it deletes first, so removing the final key leaves a stale entry. */
    boolean removeKeyFaulty(int key) {
        Integer slot = slots.get(key);
        if (slot == null) return false;
        int last = keys.get(keys.size() - 1);
        keys.set(slot, last);
        slots.remove(key);
        // When key equals last, this write puts the removed key back into the map.
        slots.put(last, slot);
        keys.remove(keys.size() - 1);
        return true;
    }

    int slotOf(int key) {
        Integer s = slots.get(key);
        return s == null ? -1 : s;
    }

    int size() { return keys.size(); }
    int mapSize() { return slots.size(); }
    List<Integer> keys() { return keys; }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: removing the final key leaves no stale entry.
        RemoveLastElement a = new RemoveLastElement();
        for (int k : new int[] {8, 3, 6}) a.add(k);
        if (!a.removeKey(6) || !a.keys().equals(List.of(8, 3))) throw new AssertionError("example 1 keys");
        if (a.slotOf(6) != -1 || a.mapSize() != 2) throw new AssertionError("example 1 map");
        // Example 2: a middle removal moves 6 into slot 1, and an absent key returns false.
        RemoveLastElement b = new RemoveLastElement();
        for (int k : new int[] {8, 3, 6}) b.add(k);
        b.removeKey(3);
        if (!b.keys().equals(List.of(8, 6)) || b.slotOf(6) != 1 || b.mapSize() != 2) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        if (b.removeKey(9)) throw new AssertionError("absent key");
        // The faulty order keeps an entry for the removed final key.
        RemoveLastElement f = new RemoveLastElement();
        for (int k : new int[] {8, 3, 6}) f.add(k);
        f.removeKeyFaulty(6);
        if (f.mapSize() != 3 || f.size() != 2) throw new AssertionError("faulty order must leave a stale entry");
        // The faulty order is correct when the removed key is not last, so only a last-slot test exposes it.
        RemoveLastElement g = new RemoveLastElement();
        for (int k : new int[] {8, 3, 6}) g.add(k);
        g.removeKeyFaulty(8);
        if (g.mapSize() != g.size()) throw new AssertionError("middle removal is fine in both orders");
        // Random replay against a list model; the map size must equal the list size after every call.
        Random rnd = new Random(35);
        RemoveLastElement s = new RemoveLastElement();
        List<Integer> model = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int step = 0; step < 20000; step++) {
            int k = rnd.nextInt(60);
            if (rnd.nextInt(100) < 50) {
                boolean expect = !model.contains(k);
                if (s.add(k) != expect) throw new AssertionError("add flag");
                if (expect) model.add(k);
            } else {
                int at = model.indexOf(k);
                if (s.removeKey(k) != (at >= 0)) throw new AssertionError("remove flag");
                if (at >= 0) {
                    int fin = model.remove(model.size() - 1);
                    if (at < model.size()) model.set(at, fin);
                }
            }
            if (!s.keys().equals(model) || s.mapSize() != s.size()) throw new AssertionError("state");
            for (int p = 0; p < model.size(); p++)
                if (s.slotOf(model.get(p)) != p) throw new AssertionError("slot");
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Insert Delete GetRandom O(1) With Positions

LeetCode 380

Problem Specification. Design IndexedSet, a set of distinct non-negative integers, in the style of LeetCode 380 with a changed contract. The operation insert(v) returns true when v was added and false when it was present. The operation remove(v) deletes v and returns the value that moved into the freed position, or -1 when no value moved or v was absent. The operation getAt(i) returns the value at position i. The operation size() returns the count of values. New values take the next free position, and a removal moves the final value into the freed position.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Values are int values in the range 0 to 10^9, so -1 is never a value.
  • Calls number at most 10^5.
  • Cost of every operation is O(1) on average.
  • Positions for getAt run from 0 to size() - 1, and any other position throws IndexOutOfBoundsException.

Example 1. Input: insert 12, 5, 30, 9, then call remove(5). Output: the call returns 9, getAt(1) is 9 and size() is 3.

Example 2. Input: insert 7, 2, 7, then call remove(2) and remove(99). Output: the second insert(7) returns false, both removals return -1 because 2 was last and 99 is absent, getAt(0) is 7 and size() is 1.

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The prompt asks for position reads and for the moved value as output, so the repair order and the slot map are both observable.

Hint

The return value of remove exposes the order of the repair. Which entry is wrong right after the move, and what happens when you compare two boxed slots with == for values above 127?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

The contract needs membership, insertion, removal and position reads, all in O(1). A hash map answers membership and finds a value's position. A list answers getAt and supports append and tail removal. Every value in the list has exactly one map entry that names its position, and that fact is the invariant. Insertion records the position before the append. Removal looks up the position, copies the final value into it, repairs the moved value's entry and then deletes the removed value's entry. That order keeps the map correct when the removed value is last. The return value of remove is the moved value, or -1 when the value was absent or last, so a wrong repair shows up in later getAt calls. Slots read from the map are boxed Integer objects, and == on two boxed values above 127 compares object identity, so the code unboxes to int before any comparison.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) on average for every operation, because each does a constant number of hash and list operations at the tail or at one position.
  • Space is O(n) for n values, because the list and the map each hold one record per value.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Map;
import java.util.Random;

public final class IndexedSet {
    private final List<Integer> values = new ArrayList<>();
    private final Map<Integer, Integer> slotOf = new HashMap<>();

    /**
     * Adds v at the next free position; returns false when present.
     * Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(1) per value.
     * Invariant: slotOf.get(v) equals the position of v in values.
     */
    boolean insert(int v) {
        // A present value keeps its position and its entry.
        if (slotOf.containsKey(v)) return false;
        // The new position equals the size before the append.
        slotOf.put(v, values.size());
        values.add(v);
        return true;
    }

    /**
     * Deletes v and returns the value that moved into its position, or -1 when none moved.
     * Time: O(1) average. Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: after the call, every remaining value maps to its true position.
     */
    int remove(int v) {
        Integer boxed = slotOf.get(v);
        // Absent values change nothing and move nothing.
        if (boxed == null) return -1;
        // Unbox once so no later comparison uses object identity.
        int slot = boxed;
        int last = values.get(values.size() - 1);
        // Swap with last: the final value fills the freed position.
        values.set(slot, last);
        // Repair the moved value's entry, then delete the removed value's entry.
        slotOf.put(last, slot);
        slotOf.remove(v);
        // The int argument drops the final position without searching.
        values.remove(values.size() - 1);
        return last == v ? -1 : last;
    }

    /** Returns the value at position i. Time: O(1). Space: O(1). */
    int getAt(int i) {
        if (i < 0 || i >= values.size()) throw new IndexOutOfBoundsException("position " + i);
        return values.get(i);
    }

    int size() { return values.size(); }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: removing 5 moves the final value 9 into position 1.
        IndexedSet a = new IndexedSet();
        for (int v : new int[] {12, 5, 30, 9}) a.insert(v);
        if (a.remove(5) != 9 || a.getAt(1) != 9 || a.size() != 3) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        // Example 2: a duplicate insert, a last-position removal and an absent removal.
        IndexedSet b = new IndexedSet();
        b.insert(7);
        b.insert(2);
        if (b.insert(7)) throw new AssertionError("duplicate");
        if (b.remove(2) != -1) throw new AssertionError("last removal");
        if (b.remove(99) != -1) throw new AssertionError("absent removal");
        if (b.getAt(0) != 7 || b.size() != 1) throw new AssertionError("example 2 state");
        // Out-of-range reads throw.
        boolean threw = false;
        try { b.getAt(1); } catch (IndexOutOfBoundsException e) { threw = true; }
        if (!threw) throw new AssertionError("range check");
        // Java claim: boxed Integers above 127 are not guaranteed identical, so == is unsafe; equals is safe.
        Integer x = Integer.valueOf(127), y = Integer.valueOf(127);
        Integer p = Integer.valueOf(1000), q = Integer.valueOf(1000);
        if (x != y) throw new AssertionError("small boxed values are cached");
        if (p == q) throw new AssertionError("large boxed values are separate objects");
        if (!p.equals(q)) throw new AssertionError("equals compares values");
        // Random replay against a list model; values above 127 exercise the boxed comparison.
        Random rnd = new Random(36);
        IndexedSet s = new IndexedSet();
        List<Integer> model = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int step = 0; step < 20000; step++) {
            int v = rnd.nextInt(600);
            if (rnd.nextInt(100) < 50) {
                boolean expect = !model.contains(v);
                if (s.insert(v) != expect) throw new AssertionError("insert flag");
                if (expect) model.add(v);
            } else {
                int at = model.indexOf(v);
                int expect = -1;
                if (at >= 0) {
                    int fin = model.remove(model.size() - 1);
                    if (at < model.size()) { model.set(at, fin); expect = fin; }
                }
                if (s.remove(v) != expect) throw new AssertionError("remove return");
            }
            if (s.size() != model.size()) throw new AssertionError("size");
            for (int i = 0; i < model.size(); i++)
                if (s.getAt(i) != model.get(i)) throw new AssertionError("getAt " + i);
        }
    }
}verified

Keep Traversal State Between Calls

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Why A Parser Loses Tokens

A SQL parser reads tokens from a tokenizer one call at a time. To decide whether a comma ends the column list, the parser must look at the next token without taking it. The first version of the tokenizer wrapper answered that look by calling the tokenizer's next(). After the release, the parser skipped every token it had looked at, and queries with a trailing comma produced wrong syntax errors.

Every bug of this kind has the same cause. A class stands between a caller and a sequence, and it must remember a position across separate public calls. Looking at an element and taking an element are two different promises, and one stored position cannot keep both. This lesson shows what a class must store so that a look leaves the sequence unchanged and a take moves it forward by exactly one step.

Simple approach

Peeking By Calling next Twice

The tempting wrapper forwards both operations to the source iterator, because that is the only way the source reports an element.

import java.util.Iterator;

final class NaivePeek {
    private final Iterator<Integer> source;

    NaivePeek(Iterator<Integer> source) { this.source = source; }

    // Look at the next element.
    int peek() { return source.next(); }

    // Take the next element.
    int next() { return source.next(); }

    boolean hasNext() { return source.hasNext(); }
}

Wrap the sequence [4, 7, 9] and call peek() and then next(). The first call returns 4 and moves the source forward. The second call returns 7. The 4 never reaches the caller as a taken element, and a caller who loops with hasNext() ends up one element early.

Predict first: A caller wraps the sequence [4, 7, 9] in NaivePeek and calls peek() once, then next() once. What does next() return, and which element can the caller never take afterward?

next() returns 7, because the earlier peek() already moved the source past 4. The element 4 is lost: no later call can return it, since the source never moves backward.

Bottleneck

Why Observation Must Not Consume

The naive wrapper runs in O(1) time per call and uses O(1) space, so speed is not the problem. The cost is a wrong sequence. Each peek() removes one element from what the caller will later see, so a program that peeks p times loses p elements from a sequence of n, and the loop reads only n - p values.

Repeating the peek makes it worse. Two peek() calls in a row return two different elements, although nothing was taken between them. A parser that asks the same question twice gets two different answers, and the answer to a question about the next token must not depend on how often the parser asks.

The source iterator offers no way to move backward, and a source such as a network stream or a file reader cannot be restarted at all. The wrapper therefore cannot undo a source.next() after it has made one. The only remaining option is to store the pulled element inside the wrapper, which costs O(1) extra space and keeps every call at O(1) time.

Key idea

Storing One Pulled Element

Adding A Lookahead Slot

Give the wrapper one lookahead slot and one flag filled that says whether the slot holds an element. The slot holds the single element that the source already gave away but the caller has not yet taken. Two operations then separate cleanly. peek() fills the slot when it is empty and returns the slot without clearing it. next() fills the slot the same way, returns it, and clears the flag.

The invariant is stated in two cases. When filled is false, the source holds every untaken element. When filled is true, the slot holds the next element and the source holds all the elements after it. Each operation restores one of those two cases before it returns.

Making Repeated Calls Idempotent

An operation is idempotent when calling it twice leaves the same state as calling it once. peek() becomes idempotent because it pulls from the source only when the flag is false. hasNext() becomes idempotent in a stronger way, because it never pulls at all. It answers filled || source.hasNext(), and source.hasNext() reports availability without consuming.

Handling An Exhausted Source

A source is exhausted when hasNext() returns false and no element remains. A peek() or next() on an exhausted wrapper must throw NoSuchElementException. The check comes before any assignment, so the failed call leaves the slot and the flag as they were. The flag is a separate boolean because the slot is an int, and every int value is a legal element. No value of the slot can mean empty, so the flag alone says whether the lookahead is filled.

State

Variables The Wrapper Keeps

Three fields of the wrapper hold the traversal state between calls. The two public reads and the one public take all work on these three. A fourth entity, the counter advances, is not a field. Only the demonstration harness keeps it, to show how often the source is pulled.

  • source is the underlying iterator, which only moves forward and which the wrapper pulls from at most once per element.
  • lookahead is the field that holds the single pulled element that the caller has not taken yet.
  • filled is the boolean that says whether lookahead holds a real element.
  • advances counts how many times the wrapper called source.next(), which equals the taken count plus one when filled is true. The harness keeps this counter, and the wrapper itself has no such field.
Walkthrough

Tracing Calls On Three Elements

Peeking Without Moving

The first trace wraps [4, 7, 9]. The pointer pos marks the cell most recently pulled from the source, and -1 means nothing is pulled. The variable advances counts the pulls, and lookahead shows the stored element or empty.

At the start nothing has been pulled, so pos is -1, advances is 0 and lookahead is empty. The first peek() pulls the 4, moves pos to 0, raises advances to 1 and fills lookahead. The second peek() finds filled true, so it returns 4 again and advances stays 1. The call next() then returns the stored 4 and empties lookahead, still without touching the source.

Taking And Reaching The End

The next hasNext() asks the source and answers true without pulling. The following next() pulls the 7, fills lookahead and empties it again in one call, so advances becomes 2. A peek() pulls the 9 and next() returns it, and the last hasNext() answers false because lookahead is empty and the source has nothing left.

Walking A Stack Of Iterators

The second trace follows the nested case, where the sequence is [[1, [2]], [], 3]. The pointer out marks the last integer taken, and -1 means none is taken. The variable frames is the number of unfinished iterators on the stack. Each next() first scans forward through the nested lists until it finds an integer. It pushes an iterator when it enters a list and pops one when that list has no element left. The empty list [] is entered and popped without producing anything.

Code

Implementing The Lookahead Wrapper

import java.util.Iterator;
import java.util.NoSuchElementException;

// Contract: hasNext() never consumes; peek() pulls at most once per element; next() advances exactly once.
// remove() is deliberately not overridden, so the interface default throws UnsupportedOperationException.
final class PeekIterator implements Iterator<Integer> {
    private final Iterator<Integer> source;
    private int lookahead;
    private boolean filled;

    PeekIterator(Iterator<Integer> source) { this.source = source; }

    // Reports availability only; the source is asked but never advanced.
    @Override public boolean hasNext() { return filled || source.hasNext(); }

    // Pulls from the source only when the slot is empty, so repeated peeks cost one advance.
    int peek() {
        if (!filled) {
            lookahead = source.next();   // throws NoSuchElementException before any field changes
            filled = true;
        }
        return lookahead;
    }

    // Returns the same element peek() showed, then empties the slot.
    @Override public Integer next() {
        int value = peek();
        filled = false;
        return value;
    }
}

Every operation runs in O(1) time and the wrapper adds O(1) space. A caller who only calls next() pays for the slot with one boolean test. The class inherits remove() from the interface and does not override it, so the hazards listed in the last stage stay visible.

When to use it

Checking The Next Element Invariant

Recognizing An Incremental Sequence

Use this design when a class must expose one logical sequence a piece at a time, and a caller may ask a question about the next element before taking it. Tree iterators, nested list flatteners, merging several streams and token readers all have this shape.

The invariant is a fact that every public operation must restore before it returns. Here it says that the slot and the source together hold exactly the untaken elements, in order, with at most one element in the slot. If you can state that sentence for a new class, you can check each operation against it.

Spotting The False Friend

A false friend is a design that looks like the right tool and fails on a boundary case. The nearest false friend is copying the whole sequence into a list and walking an index. It is correct for an array, but it costs O(n) space and time at construction, and it breaks for a source that is large, lazy or one-shot. It also reads a list snapshot, so later changes to the source are invisible.

Avoiding Three Java Hazards

The interface method Iterator.remove() has a default body that throws UnsupportedOperationException. A wrapper with a filled slot must not forward remove() to the source, because the source would remove the cached element and not the last one returned. A java.util.ArrayList iterator counts structural changes in modCount, and the check happens in next(), not in hasNext(). A cached element therefore hides a list change until the next pull, and the ConcurrentModificationException appears one call late. Last, an Iterator<Integer> may yield null. Unboxing it into an int throws NullPointerException, and a null sentinel for an empty slot cannot tell a missing element from a null one, so use a separate boolean.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Array Peeking Iterator

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Implement a class ArrayPeekingIterator over an int[] data. The constructor stores the array without copying it. Operation hasNext() returns true when an element has not been taken. Operation peek() returns the next element without taking it. Operation next() returns the next element and takes it. The class keeps a cursor pos that counts how many elements it has read from data, and it stores at most one read element that the caller has not taken. Operation advances() returns pos. Both peek() and next() throw NoSuchElementException when every element has been taken.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Length is 0 <= data.length <= 10^5.
  • Values are int, and ties are allowed.
  • Mutation of data is not allowed, and the caller does not change it during use.
  • Cost of every operation is O(1).
  • Failure is NoSuchElementException and leaves all state unchanged.

Example 1. Input data = [4,7,9] and the calls peek(), peek(), next(), next(), output 4, 4, 4, 7 and advances() = 2.

Example 2. Input data = [5] and the calls peek(), next(), hasNext(), output 5, 5, false, and a further peek() throws with advances() = 1.

Prerequisites. The lookahead wrapper in this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. First exercise in the sequence: it separates reading from the array from handing the element to the caller.

Hint

Which call reads from data, and what must the other calls do when the cached element exists?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

The class keeps a cursor pos into data, one cached element slot and a boolean hasSlot. The invariant is that either hasSlot is false and data[pos..] holds every untaken element, or hasSlot is true, slot is the next element and data[pos..] holds the elements after it.

Operation peek() reads data[pos] only when hasSlot is false, and it checks the length before any assignment, so a failed call changes nothing. Operation next() calls peek() and then clears the flag, so one taken element costs exactly one read. Operation hasNext() answers from the flag and the cursor without reading.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) per operation, because each call does a constant number of comparisons and at most one array read.
  • Space is O(1) extra, because the class stores one cursor, one value and one flag and never copies data.
import java.util.NoSuchElementException;
import java.util.Random;

public final class ArrayPeekingIterator {
    private final int[] data;
    private int pos;
    private int slot;
    private boolean hasSlot;

    ArrayPeekingIterator(int[] data) { this.data = data; }

    /**
     * Reports whether an element is left to take.
     * Time: O(1), two reads. Space: O(1). Invariant: no field changes.
     */
    boolean hasNext() {
        // Either the slot holds the next element or the array still has unread positions.
        return hasSlot || pos < data.length;
    }

    /**
     * Returns the next element without taking it.
     * Time: O(1), at most one array read. Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: afterwards hasSlot is true and slot holds the next untaken element.
     */
    int peek() {
        // Read from the array only when the slot is empty, so repeated peeks cost one read.
        if (!hasSlot) {
            // Check before assigning, so a failed call leaves every field as it was.
            if (pos >= data.length) throw new NoSuchElementException("no element left");
            slot = data[pos++];
            hasSlot = true;
        }
        return slot;
    }

    /**
     * Takes the next element.
     * Time: O(1). Space: O(1). Invariant: afterwards the slot is empty.
     */
    int next() {
        // peek() fills the slot once, then the flag clears so the element is taken exactly once.
        int value = peek();
        hasSlot = false;
        return value;
    }

    /** Number of reads from the array so far; equals taken elements plus one when the slot is full. */
    int advances() { return pos; }

    static boolean throwsOnPeek(ArrayPeekingIterator it) {
        try { it.peek(); return false; } catch (NoSuchElementException e) { return true; }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: peek twice, then take two elements.
        ArrayPeekingIterator a = new ArrayPeekingIterator(new int[] {4, 7, 9});
        if (a.peek() != 4 || a.peek() != 4) throw new AssertionError("repeated peek");
        if (a.next() != 4 || a.next() != 7) throw new AssertionError("next after peek");
        if (a.advances() != 2) throw new AssertionError("advances after four calls");
        // Example 2: a single element, then the failing peek leaves state alone.
        ArrayPeekingIterator b = new ArrayPeekingIterator(new int[] {5});
        if (b.peek() != 5 || b.next() != 5 || b.hasNext()) throw new AssertionError("single element");
        if (!throwsOnPeek(b) || b.advances() != 1) throw new AssertionError("failed peek changes nothing");
        // Empty input throws on both read operations.
        ArrayPeekingIterator empty = new ArrayPeekingIterator(new int[0]);
        if (empty.hasNext() || !throwsOnPeek(empty)) throw new AssertionError("empty");
        boolean threw = false;
        try { empty.next(); } catch (NoSuchElementException e) { threw = true; }
        if (!threw) throw new AssertionError("next on empty");
        // Random scripts compare the class with an index-based model of the sequence.
        Random rnd = new Random(33);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 400; trial++) {
            int n = rnd.nextInt(9);
            int[] data = new int[n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) data[i] = rnd.nextInt(11) - 5;
            ArrayPeekingIterator it = new ArrayPeekingIterator(data);
            int taken = 0;
            boolean peeked = false;
            for (int step = 0; step < 30; step++) {
                int op = rnd.nextInt(3);
                boolean left = taken < n;
                if (op == 0) {
                    if (it.hasNext() != left) throw new AssertionError("hasNext");
                } else if (op == 1) {
                    if (left) {
                        if (it.peek() != data[taken]) throw new AssertionError("peek value");
                        peeked = true;
                    } else if (!throwsOnPeek(it)) throw new AssertionError("peek should throw");
                } else {
                    if (left) {
                        if (it.next() != data[taken]) throw new AssertionError("next value");
                        taken++;
                        peeked = false;
                    } else {
                        boolean t = false;
                        try { it.next(); } catch (NoSuchElementException e) { t = true; }
                        if (!t) throw new AssertionError("next should throw");
                    }
                }
                // The read count never exceeds taken elements plus the cached one.
                if (it.advances() != taken + (peeked ? 1 : 0)) throw new AssertionError("advances");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Peeking Iterator

LeetCode 284

Problem Specification. Implement PeekingCountdown over an Iterator<Integer> source and an integer size equal to the number of elements the source will give. Operations hasNext(), peek() and next() behave as in the previous exercise. The changed contract has two parts. Operation peek() on an exhausted wrapper throws NoSuchElementException, and the throw leaves every field and remaining() as they were. Operation remaining() returns size minus the number of elements next() has returned, and a peek() never changes it.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Size is 0 <= size <= 10^5, and it equals the true length of the source.
  • Values are int and fit in a boxed Integer with no null.
  • Source moves forward only and cannot be restarted.
  • Cost of every operation is O(1).
  • Failure is NoSuchElementException on peek() or next() when nothing remains.

Example 1. Input a source [3,8,1] and size = 3, with the calls peek(), remaining(), next(), remaining(), next(), next(), remaining(), output 3, 3, 3, 2, 8, 1, 0.

Example 2. Input an empty source and size = 0, output that hasNext() is false, remaining() is 0, and two peek() calls in a row both throw while remaining() stays 0.

Prerequisites. The array peeking exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. The contract now adds a counted view of the sequence and a failure that must leave state untouched.

Hint

Where in peek() must the check for an empty source sit so that nothing has changed when the exception leaves?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

The class wraps the source with a one-element cache slot and a flag has, and it adds a counter remaining that starts at size. The counter changes only in next(), so remaining is always size minus the number of taken elements, and a peek() cannot disturb it.

The changed failure contract lives in peek(). When the cache is empty, the method asks source.hasNext() first and throws before it assigns slot or has. That order makes the failed call a pure observation, so the same call can be repeated with the same result. Operation next() reuses peek(), which means it also throws before it decrements remaining.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) per operation, because each call does a fixed number of field reads and at most one source.next().
  • Space is O(1) extra, because the wrapper stores one value, one flag and one counter beside the source.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.Iterator;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.NoSuchElementException;
import java.util.Random;

public final class PeekingCountdown {
    private final Iterator<Integer> source;
    private int remaining;
    private int slot;
    private boolean has;

    PeekingCountdown(Iterator<Integer> source, int size) {
        this.source = source;
        this.remaining = size;
    }

    /**
     * True when an element can still be taken.
     * Time: O(1). Space: O(1). Invariant: no field changes.
     */
    boolean hasNext() { return has || source.hasNext(); }

    /**
     * Shows the next element; on an exhausted wrapper it throws and changes nothing.
     * Time: O(1). Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: the cached slot, when full, holds the element after the last taken one.
     */
    int peek() {
        // Pull only when the cache is empty, so a repeated peek reuses the stored element.
        if (!has) {
            // Throw before any assignment so a failed call leaves slot, has and remaining unchanged.
            if (!source.hasNext()) throw new NoSuchElementException("exhausted");
            slot = source.next();
            has = true;
        }
        return slot;
    }

    /**
     * Takes the next element and counts it.
     * Time: O(1). Space: O(1). Invariant: remaining equals size minus taken.
     */
    int next() {
        // peek() throws first on an exhausted wrapper, so remaining never goes below zero.
        int value = peek();
        has = false;
        remaining--;
        return value;
    }

    /** Elements not yet taken; a peek never changes it. */
    int remaining() { return remaining; }

    static boolean peekThrows(PeekingCountdown p) {
        try { p.peek(); return false; } catch (NoSuchElementException e) { return true; }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: remaining is unchanged by the peek and drops by one per taken element.
        PeekingCountdown a = new PeekingCountdown(List.of(3, 8, 1).iterator(), 3);
        int[] seen = {a.peek(), a.remaining(), a.next(), a.remaining(), a.next(), a.next(), a.remaining()};
        int[] expected = {3, 3, 3, 2, 8, 1, 0};
        for (int i = 0; i < expected.length; i++) {
            if (seen[i] != expected[i]) throw new AssertionError("example 1 at " + i);
        }
        // Example 2: an empty source throws twice in a row with remaining still zero.
        PeekingCountdown e = new PeekingCountdown(new ArrayList<Integer>().iterator(), 0);
        if (e.hasNext() || e.remaining() != 0) throw new AssertionError("empty start");
        if (!peekThrows(e) || !peekThrows(e) || e.remaining() != 0) throw new AssertionError("repeated failing peek");
        // Random operation scripts replayed against a list model.
        Random rnd = new Random(284);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 400; trial++) {
            int n = rnd.nextInt(8);
            List<Integer> model = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) model.add(rnd.nextInt(100));
            PeekingCountdown p = new PeekingCountdown(new ArrayList<>(model).iterator(), n);
            for (int step = 0; step < 25; step++) {
                int op = rnd.nextInt(4);
                if (op == 0) {
                    if (p.hasNext() != !model.isEmpty()) throw new AssertionError("hasNext");
                } else if (op == 1) {
                    if (model.isEmpty()) {
                        if (!peekThrows(p)) throw new AssertionError("peek should throw");
                    } else if (p.peek() != model.get(0)) throw new AssertionError("peek value");
                } else if (op == 2) {
                    if (model.isEmpty()) {
                        boolean t = false;
                        try { p.next(); } catch (NoSuchElementException ex) { t = true; }
                        if (!t) throw new AssertionError("next should throw");
                    } else if (p.next() != model.remove(0)) throw new AssertionError("next value");
                }
                // Every step, including failed ones, keeps the counter equal to the model size.
                if (p.remaining() != model.size()) throw new AssertionError("remaining");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Repeated hasNext And Peek

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Implement a generic class Lookahead<T> that implements Iterator<T> over a source iterator. Operations hasNext() and peek() must be idempotent, so calling either one any number of times in a row changes nothing after the first call. Count the underlying advances, which are the calls the wrapper makes to source.next(). Show that after any call sequence the count equals the number of taken elements plus one when an element is cached, and no more.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Elements are of type T, and null is allowed as an element.
  • Sequence length is at most 10^5, and it may be empty.
  • Count is observed through a source wrapper that increments a counter on every successful next().
  • hasNext() is not allowed to call source.next().
  • Remove is not supported and throws UnsupportedOperationException.

Example 1. Input a source [6,2,9] and the calls hasNext() three times, peek() three times, output true three times, then 6 three times, with 1 advance. Then next(), hasNext(), peek() output 6, true, 2 and 2 advances.

Example 2. Input a source [5] and the calls peek(), next(), hasNext(), peek(), output 5, 5, false and then a NoSuchElementException, with exactly 1 advance.

Prerequisites. The two exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The edge case is repetition: the same read-only call repeated must not change the number of advances.

Hint

If hasNext() asked the source to produce an element, how many elements would ten calls consume?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

The wrapper reads from the source only inside peek(), and only when the cache is empty. Operation hasNext() returns has || source.hasNext(), which asks the source for availability and never advances it. Both operations are therefore idempotent: the first call may fill the cache, and every repeat finds the state already in place.

A counting source wraps the real iterator and increments a counter after each successful next(). The invariant is that the counter equals the number of taken elements plus one when the cache is full. The hazards are checked in the harness. The class never overrides remove(), so the interface default throws. A list iterator detects a change on its next pull, so a cached element hides the change for one call. A boolean flag keeps a null element distinct from an empty cache, while unboxing that null into an int throws.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) per operation, because each call performs at most one source call and constant field work.
  • Space is O(1) extra, because the wrapper holds one element reference and one flag.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.Arrays;
import java.util.ConcurrentModificationException;
import java.util.Iterator;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.NoSuchElementException;
import java.util.Random;

public final class RepeatedLookahead {
    /** Counts successful source.next() calls; hasNext() does not count. */
    static final class CountingSource<T> implements Iterator<T> {
        private final Iterator<T> inner;
        int calls;
        CountingSource(Iterator<T> inner) { this.inner = inner; }
        @Override public boolean hasNext() { return inner.hasNext(); }
        @Override public T next() {
            // Count only after the inner call succeeds, so a failed pull is not an advance.
            T value = inner.next();
            calls++;
            return value;
        }
    }

    /**
     * Wrapper with idempotent hasNext() and peek().
     * Time: O(1) per operation. Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: slot plus the source hold exactly the untaken elements, at most one in the slot.
     */
    static final class Lookahead<T> implements Iterator<T> {
        private final Iterator<T> source;
        private T slot;
        private boolean cached;

        Lookahead(Iterator<T> source) { this.source = source; }

        // Asks the source for availability only, so any number of calls consume nothing.
        @Override public boolean hasNext() { return cached || source.hasNext(); }

        T peek() {
            // The only place that pulls; the flag, not the value, says whether the slot is full.
            if (!cached) {
                slot = source.next();
                cached = true;
            }
            return slot;
        }

        @Override public T next() {
            // peek() throws on an exhausted source before this method changes any field.
            T value = peek();
            cached = false;
            slot = null;
            return value;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: repeated hasNext and peek cost one advance in total.
        CountingSource<Integer> src = new CountingSource<>(List.of(6, 2, 9).iterator());
        Lookahead<Integer> la = new Lookahead<>(src);
        for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) if (!la.hasNext()) throw new AssertionError("hasNext");
        for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) if (la.peek() != 6) throw new AssertionError("peek");
        if (src.calls != 1) throw new AssertionError("one advance for six read-only calls");
        if (la.next() != 6 || !la.hasNext() || la.peek() != 2 || src.calls != 2) throw new AssertionError("second element");
        // Example 2: a failed peek on an exhausted wrapper is not an advance.
        CountingSource<Integer> one = new CountingSource<>(List.of(5).iterator());
        Lookahead<Integer> single = new Lookahead<>(one);
        if (single.peek() != 5 || single.next() != 5 || single.hasNext()) throw new AssertionError("single");
        boolean threw = false;
        try { single.peek(); } catch (NoSuchElementException e) { threw = true; }
        if (!threw || one.calls != 1) throw new AssertionError("failed peek");
        // Java hazard 1: remove() is not overridden, so the Iterator default throws.
        threw = false;
        try { new Lookahead<>(new ArrayList<>(List.of(1)).iterator()).remove(); }
        catch (UnsupportedOperationException e) { threw = true; }
        if (!threw) throw new AssertionError("default remove");
        // Java hazard 2: ArrayList checks modCount in next(), so the cached element hides the change one call.
        List<Integer> list = new ArrayList<>(List.of(1, 2, 3));
        Lookahead<Integer> late = new Lookahead<>(list.iterator());
        if (late.peek() != 1) throw new AssertionError("first peek");
        list.add(4);
        if (late.next() != 1) throw new AssertionError("cached element still returned");
        threw = false;
        try { late.peek(); } catch (ConcurrentModificationException e) { threw = true; }
        if (!threw) throw new AssertionError("late ConcurrentModificationException");
        // Java hazard 3: a null element is a real element, and unboxing it throws.
        Lookahead<Integer> nulls = new Lookahead<>(Arrays.asList(1, null, 3).iterator());
        if (nulls.next() != 1) throw new AssertionError("before null");
        Integer boxed = nulls.peek();
        if (boxed != null || !nulls.hasNext()) throw new AssertionError("null stays an element");
        threw = false;
        try { int bad = boxed; if (bad == 0) bad++; } catch (NullPointerException e) { threw = true; }
        if (!threw) throw new AssertionError("unboxing null");
        if (nulls.next() != null || nulls.next() != 3 || nulls.hasNext()) throw new AssertionError("after null");
        // Random scripts against a list model, checking the advance count on every step.
        Random rnd = new Random(341);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 400; trial++) {
            int n = rnd.nextInt(8);
            List<Integer> data = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) data.add(rnd.nextInt(50));
            CountingSource<Integer> cs = new CountingSource<>(data.iterator());
            Lookahead<Integer> w = new Lookahead<>(cs);
            int taken = 0;
            boolean peeked = false;
            for (int step = 0; step < 30; step++) {
                int op = rnd.nextInt(3);
                boolean left = taken < n;
                if (op == 0) {
                    if (w.hasNext() != left) throw new AssertionError("hasNext");
                } else if (op == 1) {
                    try {
                        int v = w.peek();
                        if (!left || v != data.get(taken)) throw new AssertionError("peek value");
                        peeked = true;
                    } catch (NoSuchElementException e) {
                        if (left) throw new AssertionError("unexpected throw");
                    }
                } else {
                    try {
                        int v = w.next();
                        if (!left || v != data.get(taken)) throw new AssertionError("next value");
                        taken++;
                        peeked = false;
                    } catch (NoSuchElementException e) {
                        if (left) throw new AssertionError("unexpected throw");
                    }
                }
                if (cs.calls != taken + (peeked ? 1 : 0)) throw new AssertionError("advance count");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Flatten Nested List Iterator

LeetCode 341

Problem Specification. A nested item implements the interface NestedItem with the operations boolean isInt(), int value() and List<NestedItem> children(). The operation value() is defined only when isInt() is true, and children() only when it is false. Implement NestedFlattener over a top-level List<NestedItem>. Operation hasNext() returns true when an integer has not been returned, next() returns the integers in left-to-right order, and liveFrames() returns the number of iterators currently on the stack. Skip empty lists. Return only integers.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Total number of items across all lists is at most 10^5.
  • Depth of list nesting is at most 1000.
  • Values are int.
  • Empty lists are allowed at any depth, including the top level.
  • Frames counted by liveFrames() include the top-level list, and a frame is removed when a scan finds it has no element left, and both hasNext() and next() run that scan.
  • Failure is NoSuchElementException from next() when no integer remains.

Example 1. Input [[1,[2]],[],3], output 1, 2, 3, and liveFrames() reads 2, 3, 1 right after each next().

Example 2. Input [[[]],[[]]], output that hasNext() is false, and liveFrames() reads 1 before the first call and 0 after it.

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The sequence is now nested, so one saved cursor becomes a stack of unfinished cursors. The scan is lazy: it runs only inside next() or hasNext(), and a frame pops only when a scan finds it finished. The lookahead slot holds the integer that scan finds.

Hint

A list that you enter is a new iterator on top of the stack. When is it safe to remove it?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

The lookahead idea from the earlier exercises carries over, but the next integer is no longer one step away. The class keeps a stack of iterators, one per list that is entered and not yet finished, and a cache pending for the next integer it has found. The invariant is that the stack iterators, from top to bottom, hold exactly the unvisited items in order, and pending holds at most one integer that precedes all of them.

Operation hasNext() scans only while the cache is empty. It looks at the top iterator. If that iterator has no item, it pops the frame. If the item is an integer, the scan stores it and stops. If the item is a list, the scan pushes an iterator over its children and continues, so an empty list is entered and popped without producing a value. Repeated hasNext() calls stop at the first test, which makes them idempotent. Operation next() calls hasNext(), clears the cache and returns the integer.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) amortized per call, because each item is pushed or visited once and each frame is popped once, so total work over all calls is proportional to the item count.
  • Space is O(d), where d is the maximum list nesting depth, because the stack holds at most one iterator per open level.
import java.util.ArrayDeque;
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.Deque;
import java.util.Iterator;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.NoSuchElementException;
import java.util.Random;

public final class NestedFlattener {
    interface NestedItem {
        boolean isInt();
        int value();
        List<NestedItem> children();
    }

    record IntItem(int value) implements NestedItem {
        public boolean isInt() { return true; }
        public List<NestedItem> children() { throw new UnsupportedOperationException("an integer has no children"); }
    }

    record ListItem(List<NestedItem> children) implements NestedItem {
        public boolean isInt() { return false; }
        public int value() { throw new UnsupportedOperationException("a list has no value"); }
    }

    private final Deque<Iterator<NestedItem>> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
    private int pending;
    private boolean hasPending;

    NestedFlattener(List<NestedItem> top) { stack.push(top.iterator()); }

    /**
