     * Finds the next integer without consuming it; repeated calls do nothing after the first.
     * Time: O(1) amortized, each item and frame is handled once overall. Space: O(d) for the stack.
     * Invariant: the stack iterators hold exactly the unvisited items, and pending precedes them.
     */
    boolean hasNext() {
        // Scan only while no integer is cached, so a second hasNext() returns immediately.
        while (!hasPending && !stack.isEmpty()) {
            Iterator<NestedItem> top = stack.peek();
            if (!top.hasNext()) {
                // The innermost list is finished, so its frame leaves the stack.
                stack.pop();
            } else {
                NestedItem item = top.next();
                if (item.isInt()) {
                    // An integer ends the scan and waits in the cache.
                    pending = item.value();
                    hasPending = true;
                } else {
                    // A list opens a frame; an empty list is popped on the next pass.
                    stack.push(item.children().iterator());
                }
            }
        }
        return hasPending;
    }

    /** Returns the next integer. Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(d). */
    int next() {
        // hasNext() fills the cache, or this call fails before changing any state.
        if (!hasNext()) throw new NoSuchElementException("no integer left");
        hasPending = false;
        return pending;
    }

    /** Number of iterators currently on the stack, including the top-level list. */
    int liveFrames() { return stack.size(); }

    static NestedItem i(int v) { return new IntItem(v); }
    static NestedItem l(NestedItem... items) { return new ListItem(List.of(items)); }

    static List<NestedItem> gen(Random rnd, int depth) {
        List<NestedItem> out = new ArrayList<>();
        int size = rnd.nextInt(5);
        for (int k = 0; k < size; k++) {
            if (depth < 5 && rnd.nextInt(3) == 0) out.add(new ListItem(gen(rnd, depth + 1)));
            else out.add(new IntItem(rnd.nextInt(21) - 10));
        }
        return out;
    }

    // Oracle: plain recursion that appends every integer in order.
    static void flatten(List<NestedItem> items, List<Integer> sink) {
        for (NestedItem it : items) {
            if (it.isInt()) sink.add(it.value());
            else flatten(it.children(), sink);
        }
    }

    static int depthOf(List<NestedItem> items) {
        int best = 0;
        for (NestedItem it : items) if (!it.isInt()) best = Math.max(best, depthOf(it.children()));
        return best + 1;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: [[1,[2]],[],3] yields 1, 2, 3 with live frames 2, 3, 1 after each next().
        NestedFlattener f = new NestedFlattener(List.of(l(i(1), l(i(2))), l(), i(3)));
        if (f.liveFrames() != 1) throw new AssertionError("only the top-level frame before any call");
        int[] values = new int[3];
        int[] frames = new int[3];
        for (int k = 0; k < 3; k++) {
            values[k] = f.next();
            frames[k] = f.liveFrames();
        }
        if (values[0] != 1 || values[1] != 2 || values[2] != 3) throw new AssertionError("example 1 values");
        if (frames[0] != 2 || frames[1] != 3 || frames[2] != 1) throw new AssertionError("example 1 frames");
        if (f.hasNext() || f.liveFrames() != 0) throw new AssertionError("finished stack is empty");
        // Example 2: only empty lists, so hasNext() is false and every frame is popped.
        NestedFlattener e = new NestedFlattener(List.of(l(l()), l(l())));
        if (e.liveFrames() != 1 || e.hasNext() || e.liveFrames() != 0) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        boolean threw = false;
        try { e.next(); } catch (NoSuchElementException ex) { threw = true; }
        if (!threw) throw new AssertionError("next on finished");
        // Random nested inputs against recursive flattening.
        Random rnd = new Random(2026);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 500; trial++) {
            List<NestedItem> top = gen(rnd, 1);
            List<Integer> expected = new ArrayList<>();
            flatten(top, expected);
            int maxFrames = depthOf(top);
            NestedFlattener it = new NestedFlattener(top);
            List<Integer> got = new ArrayList<>();
            while (true) {
                // Repeated hasNext() calls must agree and must not change the frame count.
                boolean first = it.hasNext();
                int framesAfter = it.liveFrames();
                for (int r = rnd.nextInt(3); r > 0; r--) {
                    if (it.hasNext() != first || it.liveFrames() != framesAfter) throw new AssertionError("hasNext not idempotent");
                }
                if (framesAfter > maxFrames) throw new AssertionError("more frames than nesting levels");
                if (!first) {
                    if (framesAfter != 0) throw new AssertionError("frames left after the end");
                    break;
                }
                if (framesAfter < 1) throw new AssertionError("an integer needs a live frame");
                got.add(it.next());
            }
            if (!got.equals(expected)) throw new AssertionError("flattened order " + got + " vs " + expected);
        }
    }
}verified

Pick A Random Value In Constant Time

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Why A Random Pick Gets Slow

A load balancer keeps the addresses of healthy backend servers. Health checks add a server when it recovers and remove it when it fails. Every incoming request must pick one healthy server at random, with every server equally likely. The balancer handles tens of thousands of requests per second. The pool changes a few times per second.

A first version works in testing with ten servers. In production the pool holds tens of thousands of entries, and the pick shows up at the top of the profile. Insert, remove and membership checks are all fast. Only the random pick is slow. This lesson asks how one structure can support insert, remove, membership and a random pick, each in constant expected time.

Simple approach

Copying The Set On Every Pick

The tempting design stores the values in a HashSet<Integer>. Insert, remove and membership are O(1) expected. A set has no position to draw, so the pick first copies the values into a list.

static int pickFrom(java.util.HashSet<Integer> pool, java.util.Random rng) {
    java.util.List<Integer> copy = new java.util.ArrayList<>(pool);
    return copy.get(rng.nextInt(copy.size()));
}

The copy visits every element in the set's internal iteration order. That order depends on hash buckets, not on insertion order, and the set promises no order at all. The pick is still correct, because every copy position holds one value. It is slow because the copy is rebuilt for each pick.

Bottleneck

The Cost Of Copying Per Pick

Predict first: A pool holds 50,000 values and receives 20,000 picks per second. About how many element copies per second does the copying design perform, and which operation is responsible?

About one billion element copies per second, because each pick copies all 50,000 values. The pick is responsible. Insert and remove stay cheap.

Each pick costs O(n) time and O(n) extra space for the copy, where n is the number of current values. Over m picks the total is O(m * n). For 20,000 picks on 50,000 values that is one billion copies per second, which no single core sustains.

Keeping the copy between picks does not fix this. Every insert or remove makes the copy stale, so the next pick must rebuild it. A pool that changes between nearly every pick pays the full cost each time. The fast random pick needs a structure that already has stable positions, so a random number can name a position directly. An array has that property. An array alone fails the other direction, because finding a value to remove scans O(n) slots.

Key idea

Keeping Values In An Array

Removing By Swapping With The Last Slot

Store the current values in a dynamic array, in any order. Insert appends to the end in O(1) amortized time. Removing from the middle would shift every later element, which costs O(n). The swap-with-last move avoids the shift. Copy the last value into the slot of the removed value, then delete the last slot. Order is not part of the contract, so the move is allowed, and it takes O(1) time.

Tracking Where Each Value Sits

Removal needs to find the slot of the value. A hash map from value to array index answers that in O(1) expected time. Call it the index map. Insert writes the new value into the array and records its slot in the index map. Remove reads the slot from the index map, performs the swap-with-last move and then repairs the index map. The value that moved now sits in a new slot, so its entry must change. The removed value must lose its entry.

Why A Random Index Is A Fair Pick

The array holds exactly the current values, one per slot. A random pick draws an index from 0 to size - 1, and each index has probability 1 / size. That draw is uniform: every current value is equally likely. The value follows from the index, because each slot holds exactly one value and no value holds two slots. If the array held a stale or duplicate slot, the pick would favor that value. So the correctness of the pick depends on the invariant that the array and the index map always describe the same set of values.

State

State The Set Keeps

The class needs two fields. The contract also fixes who supplies the random source, which is a parameter and not a field.

  • values is the dynamic array holding exactly the current values, with no gaps and no stale slots.
  • index is the map from each current value to its slot in values.
  • rng is a java.util.Random that the caller passes into getRandom, so a test can seed it. The class does not store it.

The invariant ties the fields together. For every slot i in 0..values.size() - 1, index.get(values.get(i)) equals i. The map holds no other key. Every operation must restore this fact before it returns.

Walkthrough

Tracing Two Removals

Removing A Middle Value

Start with the array [10, 20, 30, 40] and the index map 10 -> 0, 20 -> 1, 30 -> 2, 40 -> 3. Remove 20.

The index map says 20 sits in slot 1, and the last slot is 3 and holds 40. The operation copies 40 into slot 1, so the array reads [10, 40, 30, 40]. It then records that 40 now sits in slot 1. Next it drops the last slot, which leaves [10, 40, 30]. Finally it deletes the entry for 20. The map now agrees with the array again.

Removing The Last Value

Start with the array [10, 20, 40] and remove 40, which sits in the last slot. Here the removed slot and the last slot are the same slot.

The copy step writes 40 into the slot that already holds 40. The repair step records slot 2 for 40, which rewrites the entry with the value it already has. That entry is the one the operation is about to delete. The operation then drops the last slot. Only the final step, which deletes the entry for 40, makes the map correct. The order of those steps matters. If the delete of the entry for 40 ran before the repair, the repair would put the entry back, and the map would keep a key for a value that is no longer in the array.

Code

Implementing The Set With Injected Random

final class RandomPool {
    private final java.util.ArrayList<Integer> values = new java.util.ArrayList<>();
    private final java.util.HashMap<Integer, Integer> index = new java.util.HashMap<>();

    // Contract: true when val was absent and is now stored; false when it was already present.
    boolean insert(int val) {
        if (index.containsKey(val)) return false;    // the value is present, so state is unchanged
        index.put(val, values.size());                // the new value will occupy the next free slot
        values.add(val);                              // append in O(1) amortized time
        return true;
    }

    // Contract: true when val was present and is now gone; false when it was absent.
    boolean remove(int val) {
        Integer slot = index.get(val);                // O(1) lookup of the slot
        if (slot == null) return false;               // absent, so nothing to change
        int last = values.get(values.size() - 1);     // the value that fills the hole
        values.set(slot, last);                       // overwrite the removed value
        index.put(last, slot);                        // repair the moved value first
        values.remove(values.size() - 1);             // int argument, so this removes by position
        index.remove(val);                            // delete the removed value last
        return true;
    }

    // Contract: every current value has probability 1/size; the caller supplies rng.
    int getRandom(java.util.Random rng) {
        if (values.isEmpty()) throw new IllegalStateException("empty");   // nextInt(0) would throw a less clear error
        return values.get(rng.nextInt(values.size()));                    // uniform index, so uniform value
    }
}

Every operation costs O(1) expected time. The class uses O(n) space for the array and the map. The insert operation checks the map before it writes anything, so a repeated value leaves the state unchanged. The remove operation writes the map entry of the moved value before it deletes the entry of the removed value. The getRandom operation reads the size once and never allocates.

When to use it

Deciding When This Structure Fits

Recognizing The Pattern

Reach for this structure when one structure must support insert, remove, membership and a random pick, all in constant expected time. An invariant is a fact about the state that every operation must restore before it returns. Here the invariant says that the array holds exactly the current values and that the index map gives each value's exact slot. The recognition cue is the pair of demands "remove by value" and "pick by position".

Spotting The False Friends

A false friend is a structure that looks like the right tool and fails one demand. A HashSet fails the pick, because it has no positions. An ArrayList alone fails the removal, because indexOf scans O(n) slots. A LinkedHashSet keeps insertion order but still offers no index. A TreeSet picks a random rank only in O(log n) with extra bookkeeping, so it breaks the constant-time demand.

Watching The Java Hazards

Three Java details cause real bugs here. First, Random.nextInt(bound) throws IllegalArgumentException when the bound is 0, so an empty set needs its own check. Second, HashSet<Integer> iterates in bucket order, which is not insertion order, so no design may rely on "the first element". Third, on an ArrayList<Integer>, the call remove(int) removes by position and remove(Object) removes by value. Passing a plain int selects the first form, which is what the array tail needs. A test that seeds the random source turns the pick into an exact, repeatable check.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Swap-With-Last Removal

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Implement a class IntPool with three operations. insert(val) stores val when it is absent and returns true, and it returns false without any change when val is present. remove(val) deletes val when present and returns true, and it returns false without any change when val is absent. contains(val) returns whether val is present. Store the values in an array and keep a map from each value to its slot. After remove, the last value must fill the slot of the removed value.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Values are int and may be negative.
  • Cost of each operation is O(1) expected time.
  • Slots hold no gaps, so slot indexes run from 0 to size - 1.
  • Order of the values in the array is not part of the contract.
  • Empty pool is allowed, and remove on it returns false.

Example 1. Input insert(5), insert(8), insert(2), insert(9), then remove(8), output true for the removal, the array [5, 9, 2] and the map 5 -> 0, 9 -> 1, 2 -> 2.

Example 2. Input the same pool and remove(4), output false, with the array [5, 8, 2, 9] unchanged.

Prerequisites. The array plus index map structure in this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. First exercise in the sequence: the array removal replaces the shift of later elements with a copy of the last value.

Hint

Which value moves when slot 1 is removed from a four-slot array? After it moves, which map entry is now wrong?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

The array values holds exactly the current values, and the map slotOf stores the exact slot of each one. Insert checks the map, appends the value and records its slot. Remove reads the slot from the map and takes the last value out of the array tail. It writes that value into the freed slot, because shifting every later element would cost O(n).

Two entries then need repair. The moved value now sits in the freed slot, so its map entry changes first. The removed value has left the set, so its map entry is deleted last. The invariant is that slotOf.get(values.get(i)) equals i for every slot, and the map has no other key. The random-script check below replays thousands of operations against a plain list and compares the arrays after each one.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) expected per operation, because each call makes a constant number of hash and array-end accesses.
  • Space is O(n) for the array and the map together, because each current value appears once in each.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;

public final class SwapLastRemoval {
    /** Set of ints with array storage and a value-to-slot map. */
    static final class IntPool {
        private final ArrayList<Integer> values = new ArrayList<>();
        private final HashMap<Integer, Integer> slotOf = new HashMap<>();

        /**
         * Stores val when absent.
         * Time: O(1) expected, one lookup and one append. Space: O(1) extra per stored value.
         * Invariant: slotOf.get(values.get(i)) equals i for every slot i.
         */
        boolean insert(int val) {
            // A present value changes nothing, so the false result is the whole effect.
            if (slotOf.containsKey(val)) return false;
            // The new value takes the next free slot, which is the current size.
            slotOf.put(val, values.size());
            // Append in O(1) amortized time.
            values.add(val);
            return true;
        }

        /**
         * Removes val by copying the last value into its slot.
         * Time: O(1) expected, a fixed number of map and array-end accesses. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: afterward every slot and every map entry agree again.
         */
        boolean remove(int val) {
            // Look up the slot of the value to delete.
            Integer slot = slotOf.get(val);
            // An absent value means there is nothing to repair.
            if (slot == null) return false;
            // The last value fills the freed slot.
            int last = values.get(values.size() - 1);
            // Overwrite the removed value with the last value.
            values.set(slot, last);
            // Repair the moved value's entry before deleting anything.
            slotOf.put(last, slot);
            // The int argument selects remove(int), which deletes the last position in O(1).
            values.remove(values.size() - 1);
            // Delete the removed value's entry last, so it stays deleted even when it was the last value.
            slotOf.remove(val);
            return true;
        }

        boolean contains(int val) { return slotOf.containsKey(val); }

        List<Integer> snapshot() { return new ArrayList<>(values); }

        /** Checks the invariant: each slot is named by its value's entry and no other key exists. */
        void check() {
            // Every slot must be found through the map.
            for (int i = 0; i < values.size(); i++) {
                if (slotOf.get(values.get(i)) != i) throw new AssertionError("slot mismatch at " + i);
            }
            // The map has exactly one entry per slot.
            if (slotOf.size() != values.size()) throw new AssertionError("map size differs from array size");
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: removing 8 from [5, 8, 2, 9] moves 9 into slot 1.
        IntPool pool = new IntPool();
        for (int v : new int[] {5, 8, 2, 9}) pool.insert(v);
        if (!pool.remove(8)) throw new AssertionError("remove of a present value");
        if (!pool.snapshot().equals(List.of(5, 9, 2))) throw new AssertionError("example 1 array");
        pool.check();
        if (pool.contains(8)) throw new AssertionError("8 is gone");
        // Example 2: removing an absent value changes nothing.
        IntPool other = new IntPool();
        for (int v : new int[] {5, 8, 2, 9}) other.insert(v);
        if (other.remove(4)) throw new AssertionError("absent value");
        if (!other.snapshot().equals(List.of(5, 8, 2, 9))) throw new AssertionError("example 2 unchanged");
        // Random scripts: replay against a plain list that also swaps with the last element.
        Random rnd = new Random(33_051);
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            IntPool p = new IntPool();
            ArrayList<Integer> model = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = 0; step < 60; step++) {
                int v = rnd.nextInt(12) - 3;
                if (rnd.nextBoolean()) {
                    // Insert expectation: true exactly when the model lacks v.
                    boolean expect = !model.contains(v);
                    if (p.insert(v) != expect) throw new AssertionError("insert result");
                    if (expect) model.add(v);
                } else {
                    // indexOf(Object) scans the model in O(n), which is fine for an oracle.
                    int at = model.indexOf(v);
                    if (p.remove(v) != (at >= 0)) throw new AssertionError("remove result");
                    if (at >= 0) {
                        model.set(at, model.get(model.size() - 1));
                        model.remove(model.size() - 1);
                    }
                }
                // After every operation the arrays match and the invariant holds.
                if (!p.snapshot().equals(model)) throw new AssertionError("array differs from model");
                p.check();
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Insert Delete GetRandom O(1)

LeetCode 380

Problem Specification. Implement RandomizedSet with insert(val), remove(val) and getRandom(Random rng). Both insert and remove return a boolean: true when the call changed the set. The operation getRandom takes a caller-supplied java.util.Random and returns one current value, each with probability 1 / size. It must draw exactly one index with rng.nextInt(size). It throws IllegalStateException when the set is empty. Lesson 03 built the same structure with getAt and a moved-value return. This version replaces both: the pick takes an injected random source, and the removal returns only a boolean.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Values are int.
  • Cost of each operation is O(1) expected time.
  • Random source is the argument rng; the class never creates its own.
  • Empty set makes getRandom throw IllegalStateException.
  • Tests may pass a seeded Random or a subclass that scripts nextInt.

Example 1. Input insert(4), insert(4), insert(7), insert(9), output true, false, true, true, and a script that makes nextInt return 1 makes getRandom return 7 after being asked for a bound of 3.

Example 2. Input the values 1 to 5 and 10,000 picks from new Random(42), output five counts whose chi-square statistic stays below 18.47, the 0.1% critical value for four degrees of freedom.

Prerequisites. The swap-with-last exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. The random source moves from the class to the caller, so a test can enumerate the exact index and seed a statistical check.

Hint

If nextInt(size) is uniform over the indexes, why does the array make it uniform over the values? What breaks if a removed value stays in the array?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

The structure is the array plus value-to-slot map from the previous exercise. The contract changes in two places. First, getRandom receives the random source as an argument instead of owning one. Second, insert and remove return only booleans, with no moved-value report and no getAt.

The pick calls rng.nextInt(size) once. The bound equals the current size, so the draw is uniform over the slots. Every slot holds a distinct current value, so the pick is uniform over the values. The empty case checks the size first, because nextInt(0) throws IllegalArgumentException and the contract promises IllegalStateException.

Two checks prove the uniform claim. An exact check uses a scripted Random that returns each index in turn. It asserts the bound passed and the value returned for every slot. A statistical check draws from seeded generators and computes a chi-square statistic against the expected count. The seed fixes the draws, so the check never flakes.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) expected for all three operations, because each does a constant number of map and array accesses.
  • Space is O(n) for the array and the map, and the pick allocates nothing.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;

public final class InjectedRandomSet {
    /** Set with a caller-supplied random source for the pick. */
    static final class RandomizedSet {
        private final ArrayList<Integer> values = new ArrayList<>();
        private final HashMap<Integer, Integer> slotOf = new HashMap<>();

        /**
         * Adds val when absent.
         * Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1) extra per stored value.
         * Invariant: values holds exactly the current values and slotOf names each slot.
         */
        boolean insert(int val) {
            // Present values leave the state unchanged.
            if (slotOf.containsKey(val)) return false;
            // The new value goes to the next free slot.
            slotOf.put(val, values.size());
            values.add(val);
            return true;
        }

        /**
         * Removes val by moving the last value into its slot.
         * Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: slotOf and values agree again on return.
         */
        boolean remove(int val) {
            Integer slot = slotOf.get(val);
            // Absent values change nothing.
            if (slot == null) return false;
            int last = values.get(values.size() - 1);
            // Move the last value into the hole, then repair its entry.
            values.set(slot, last);
            slotOf.put(last, slot);
            // Drop the tail by position, then delete the removed value's entry last.
            values.remove(values.size() - 1);
            slotOf.remove(val);
            return true;
        }

        /**
         * Returns one current value, each with probability 1/size.
         * Time: O(1), one draw and one array read. Space: O(1).
         * The uniform index implies a uniform value, because slots hold distinct values.
         */
        int getRandom(Random rng) {
            // nextInt(0) would throw IllegalArgumentException, so the empty case gets its own error.
            if (values.isEmpty()) throw new IllegalStateException("empty set");
            return values.get(rng.nextInt(values.size()));
        }

        List<Integer> snapshot() { return new ArrayList<>(values); }
    }

    /** Random source that replays scripted answers and records the bound it was asked for. */
    static final class ScriptedRandom extends Random {
        private final int[] script;
        private int next = 0;
        int lastBound = -1;
        ScriptedRandom(int... script) { this.script = script; }

        @Override
        public int nextInt(int bound) {
            // Record the bound so the test can check it equals the size.
            lastBound = bound;
            return script[next++];
        }
    }

    /** Returns sum over cells of (observed - expected)^2 / expected for equal expected counts. */
    static double chiSquare(int[] counts, int draws) {
        double expected = (double) draws / counts.length;
        double stat = 0;
        for (int c : counts) stat += (c - expected) * (c - expected) / expected;
        return stat;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: the insert results are true, false, true, true.
        RandomizedSet set = new RandomizedSet();
        boolean[] got = {set.insert(4), set.insert(4), set.insert(7), set.insert(9)};
        if (!(got[0] && !got[1] && got[2] && got[3])) throw new AssertionError("insert results");
        // A script returning 1 selects slot 1, which holds 7, and the bound is the size 3.
        ScriptedRandom one = new ScriptedRandom(1);
        if (set.getRandom(one) != 7) throw new AssertionError("scripted pick");
        if (one.lastBound != 3) throw new AssertionError("bound must equal size");
        // Exact enumeration: every index maps to the value stored in that slot.
        List<Integer> snap = set.snapshot();
        for (int k = 0; k < snap.size(); k++) {
            ScriptedRandom r = new ScriptedRandom(k);
            if (set.getRandom(r) != snap.get(k)) throw new AssertionError("slot " + k);
        }
        // The empty set throws IllegalStateException, and the raw JDK call with bound 0 throws IllegalArgumentException.
        try { new RandomizedSet().getRandom(new Random(1)); throw new AssertionError("no exception"); }
        catch (IllegalStateException expected) { }
        try { new Random(1).nextInt(0); throw new AssertionError("no exception"); }
        catch (IllegalArgumentException expected) { }
        // Example 2: values 1 to 5 and 10,000 seeded picks stay under the 0.1% critical value 18.47.
        RandomizedSet five = new RandomizedSet();
        for (int v = 1; v <= 5; v++) five.insert(v);
        Random seeded = new Random(42);
        int[] counts = new int[5];
        for (int i = 0; i < 10_000; i++) counts[five.getRandom(seeded) - 1]++;
        if (chiSquare(counts, 10_000) >= 18.47) throw new AssertionError("chi-square, five values");
        // After churn the pick stays uniform: eight inserts and two removes leave six values (critical value 20.52 for 5 degrees).
        RandomizedSet churn = new RandomizedSet();
        for (int v = 1; v <= 8; v++) churn.insert(v);
        churn.remove(2);
        churn.remove(8);
        int[] counts2 = new int[6];
        List<Integer> left = churn.snapshot();
        Random seeded2 = new Random(7);
        for (int i = 0; i < 12_000; i++) counts2[left.indexOf(churn.getRandom(seeded2))]++;
        if (chiSquare(counts2, 12_000) >= 20.52) throw new AssertionError("chi-square after churn");
        // Random scripts against a plain list: same array order and the same pick for the same seed.
        Random rnd = new Random(380);
        for (int round = 0; round < 200; round++) {
            RandomizedSet s = new RandomizedSet();
            ArrayList<Integer> model = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = 0; step < 50; step++) {
                int v = rnd.nextInt(10);
                int op = rnd.nextInt(3);
                if (op == 0) {
                    boolean expect = !model.contains(v);
                    if (s.insert(v) != expect) throw new AssertionError("insert");
                    if (expect) model.add(v);
                } else if (op == 1) {
                    int at = model.indexOf(v);
                    if (s.remove(v) != (at >= 0)) throw new AssertionError("remove");
                    if (at >= 0) {
                        model.set(at, model.get(model.size() - 1));
                        model.remove(model.size() - 1);
                    }
                } else if (!model.isEmpty()) {
                    long seed = rnd.nextLong();
                    int expect = model.get(new Random(seed).nextInt(model.size()));
                    if (s.getRandom(new Random(seed)) != expect) throw new AssertionError("pick");
                }
                if (!s.snapshot().equals(model)) throw new AssertionError("array order");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Removing The Last Value

Author exercise

Problem Specification. In the swap-with-last removal, the value to remove can already be the last one. Then the moved value and the removed value are identical. Implement remove(val) so that the deleted map entry is never restored. Include the case where the set holds only one value. State the order of the four state updates and explain why a different order resurrects the entry.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Case covers a removed value that sits in the last slot, and that includes a set of size 1.
  • Result after removing the only value is an empty array and an empty map.
  • Membership of the removed value is false afterward.
  • Reinsertion of the same value works and appends it again.
  • Cost is O(1) expected time.

Example 1. Input a pool holding only 6, then remove(6), contains(6), insert(6), output true, false, true, and the final array is [6].

Example 2. Input the array [3, 6], then remove(6), output the array [3] and the map 3 -> 0. Then remove(3) on the original array [3, 6] outputs the array [6] and the map 6 -> 0.

Prerequisites. Both exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The edge case changes the order of the updates, not the algorithm: the entry for the removed value must be deleted after the moved value's entry is written.

Hint

When the slot equals the last slot, which map entry does the repair step write? Which step must run after it?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

When the value to remove sits in the last slot, the freed slot and the last slot are the same. The copy step writes the value onto itself. The repair step then writes slotOf.put(last, slot), and last equals the removed value, so that step re-creates its entry. The entry deleted last is the only one that stays deleted.

The correct order has four updates. Overwrite the freed slot with the last value. Write the moved value's map entry. Remove the last array position. Delete the removed value's map entry. The wrong order deletes the entry first and then writes the repair, which puts the removed value back in the map. After that, contains reports a value that the array no longer holds, and a later pick or insert acts on a broken invariant. When the set holds only one value, the array and the map both end empty.

The second hazard is ArrayList<Integer>.remove. A call with an int argument removes by position, and a call with an Integer argument removes by value. The tail removal needs the position form.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) expected, because the four updates are a fixed number of map and array-end accesses.
  • Space is O(1) extra, because the operation allocates only a few locals.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;

public final class LastValueRemoval {
    /** Array plus slot map, with the correct and the broken removal order side by side. */
    static final class Pool {
        final ArrayList<Integer> values = new ArrayList<>();
        final HashMap<Integer, Integer> slotOf = new HashMap<>();

        void insert(int val) {
            // Append and record the slot; callers in this test never insert a present value.
            slotOf.put(val, values.size());
            values.add(val);
        }

        /**
         * Correct order: overwrite, repair, drop the tail, delete the removed entry.
         * Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: when val is the last value, the final delete leaves no entry for it.
         */
        boolean remove(int val) {
            Integer slot = slotOf.get(val);
            if (slot == null) return false;
            int last = values.get(values.size() - 1);
            // Copy the last value over the freed slot.
            values.set(slot, last);
            // Repair the moved value's entry; this recreates the entry for val when val is last.
            slotOf.put(last, slot);
            // Remove the tail by position.
            values.remove(values.size() - 1);
            // The delete runs last, so it also removes the entry the repair just wrote.
            slotOf.remove(val);
            return true;
        }

        /** Broken order: deletes the entry before the repair, which resurrects it for the last value. */
        void removeBroken(int val) {
            int slot = slotOf.get(val);
            int last = values.get(values.size() - 1);
            values.set(slot, last);
            // Deleting first lets the repair write the entry again.
            slotOf.remove(val);
            slotOf.put(last, slot);
            values.remove(values.size() - 1);
        }

        List<Integer> snapshot() { return new ArrayList<>(values); }

        void check() {
            // Every slot is named by its map entry, and the map has no extra keys.
            for (int i = 0; i < values.size(); i++) {
                if (slotOf.get(values.get(i)) != i) throw new AssertionError("slot mismatch");
            }
            if (slotOf.size() != values.size()) throw new AssertionError("stale map entry");
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: remove the only value, check membership, then insert it again.
        Pool one = new Pool();
        one.insert(6);
        if (!one.remove(6)) throw new AssertionError("remove only");
        if (one.slotOf.containsKey(6)) throw new AssertionError("entry must stay deleted");
        if (!one.values.isEmpty() || !one.slotOf.isEmpty()) throw new AssertionError("empty after removal");
        one.insert(6);
        if (!one.snapshot().equals(List.of(6))) throw new AssertionError("reinsertion");
        // Example 2: [3, 6] minus 6 is [3]; [3, 6] minus 3 is [6] mapped to slot 0.
        Pool a = new Pool();
        a.insert(3);
        a.insert(6);
        a.remove(6);
        if (!a.snapshot().equals(List.of(3)) || a.slotOf.get(3) != 0 || a.slotOf.size() != 1) throw new AssertionError("example 2a");
        Pool b = new Pool();
        b.insert(3);
        b.insert(6);
        b.remove(3);
        if (!b.snapshot().equals(List.of(6)) || b.slotOf.get(6) != 0) throw new AssertionError("example 2b");
        // The broken order resurrects the entry for the only value.
        Pool bad = new Pool();
        bad.insert(6);
        bad.removeBroken(6);
        if (!bad.values.isEmpty()) throw new AssertionError("array is empty");
        if (!bad.slotOf.containsKey(6)) throw new AssertionError("the broken order resurrects the entry");
        // remove(int) removes by position and remove(Object) removes by value.
        ArrayList<Integer> list = new ArrayList<>(List.of(10, 20, 30, 1));
        list.remove(1);
        if (!list.equals(List.of(10, 30, 1))) throw new AssertionError("remove(int) is by position");
        list.remove(Integer.valueOf(1));
        if (!list.equals(List.of(10, 30))) throw new AssertionError("remove(Object) is by value");
        // Random scripts over a tiny value range, so removing the last value happens often.
        Random rnd = new Random(33_053);
        for (int round = 0; round < 400; round++) {
            Pool p = new Pool();
            ArrayList<Integer> model = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = 0; step < 40; step++) {
                int v = rnd.nextInt(4);
                if (rnd.nextBoolean()) {
                    // Insert only absent values, as the contract of this helper requires.
                    if (!model.contains(v)) { p.insert(v); model.add(v); }
                } else {
                    int at = model.indexOf(v);
                    if (p.remove(v) != (at >= 0)) throw new AssertionError("remove result");
                    if (at >= 0) {
                        model.set(at, model.get(model.size() - 1));
                        model.remove(model.size() - 1);
                    }
                }
                if (!p.snapshot().equals(model)) throw new AssertionError("array differs");
                p.check();
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Duplicates Allowed

LeetCode 381

Problem Specification. Implement RandomizedCollection that accepts repeated values. insert(val) always stores one more occurrence and returns true only when val had no occurrence before. remove(val) removes the occurrence with the greatest array index and returns the number of occurrences of val that remain. It returns -1 when val has no occurrence. getRandom(Random rng) draws one array index with rng.nextInt(size), so a value is returned with probability proportional to its count. Store a map from each value to the set of its indexes.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Values are int.
  • Index set per value must support finding the greatest index.
  • Removal returns the remaining count, which is 0 when the last occurrence goes, and -1 when the value is absent.
  • Cost is O(log k) for insert and remove, where k is the count of that value, and O(1) for getRandom.
  • Empty collection makes getRandom throw IllegalStateException.

Example 1. Input insert(4), insert(4), insert(6), insert(4), then remove(4), output true, false, true, false, and 2, with the array [4, 4, 6].

Example 2. Input the collection that Example 1 leaves, which holds the array [4, 4, 6], then remove(4), remove(9), output 1 and -1, with the array [4, 6].

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. One index per value becomes a set of indexes per value, and the removal rule becomes "greatest index first", which also decides when the moved value and the removed value coincide.

Hint

Which index leaves the array when the greatest index of the value is removed? When is that index equal to the last slot, and what does that say about the moved value?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

The array now holds every occurrence, so a value can fill several slots. The map from value to index set replaces the map from value to one index. The invariant is that, for every slot i, the index set of values.get(i) contains i, and the sets together hold exactly the slots 0..size-1.

remove(val) takes the greatest index p in the set of val. The rule has a useful effect: the last slot holds val exactly when p equals the last slot. In that case the operation only deletes the last slot and its index. Otherwise the last slot holds a different value moved. The operation writes moved into slot p, then removes the old last slot from the set of moved and adds p. The set of moved is a different object from the set of val, so the two edits cannot collide. The remaining count is the size of the set of val after the removal, and an empty set is deleted from the map.

A TreeSet finds the greatest index in O(log k). A HashSet finds none, because its iteration follows hash buckets and not insertion order, so iterator().next() returns an arbitrary index and the greatest-index rule needs an O(k) scan. The pick is as before: a random slot over all occurrences, so a value with more occurrences is more likely.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(log k) for insert and remove, where k is the number of occurrences of the value involved, and O(1) for getRandom.
  • Space is O(n), because each occurrence takes one array slot and one index in a set.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.Collections;
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.HashSet;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Random;
import java.util.TreeSet;

public final class DuplicatesAllowed {
    /** Multiset with array storage and a sorted index set per value. */
    static final class RandomizedCollection {
        private final ArrayList<Integer> values = new ArrayList<>();
        private final HashMap<Integer, TreeSet<Integer>> indexes = new HashMap<>();

        /**
         * Stores one more occurrence of val.
         * Time: O(log k) for the sorted set insert, k occurrences of val. Space: O(1) extra per occurrence.
         * Returns true when val had no occurrence before.
         */
        boolean insert(int val) {
            // Create the index set on the first occurrence.
            TreeSet<Integer> set = indexes.computeIfAbsent(val, key -> new TreeSet<>());
            boolean fresh = set.isEmpty();
            // The new occurrence takes the next free slot.
            set.add(values.size());
            values.add(val);
            return fresh;
        }

        /**
         * Removes the occurrence of val with the greatest index; returns the count that remains, or -1 when absent.
         * Time: O(log k) for the sorted set edits. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: every slot appears in exactly the index set of its value.
         */
        int remove(int val) {
            TreeSet<Integer> set = indexes.get(val);
            // An absent value has no occurrence to remove.
            if (set == null) return -1;
            // The greatest index leaves the set of val first.
            int slot = set.pollLast();
            int lastSlot = values.size() - 1;
            // A different last value must move into the freed slot; slot == lastSlot means val was last.
            if (slot != lastSlot) {
                int moved = values.get(lastSlot);
                values.set(slot, moved);
                // The set of moved is a different object from set, so these two edits cannot collide.
                TreeSet<Integer> movedSet = indexes.get(moved);
                movedSet.remove(lastSlot);
                movedSet.add(slot);
            }
            // The int argument removes the tail by position.
            values.remove(lastSlot);
            // The remaining count is the set size; an empty set leaves the map.
            int remaining = set.size();
            if (remaining == 0) indexes.remove(val);
            return remaining;
        }

        /** Returns the value in a random slot; Time: O(1). Space: O(1). */
        int getRandom(Random rng) {
            if (values.isEmpty()) throw new IllegalStateException("empty collection");
            return values.get(rng.nextInt(values.size()));
        }

        List<Integer> snapshot() { return new ArrayList<>(values); }

        /** Checks the invariant: sets name exactly the slots of their value, and the sets cover every slot once. */
        void check() {
            int total = 0;
            for (var e : indexes.entrySet()) {
                if (e.getValue().isEmpty()) throw new AssertionError("empty set kept in map");
                for (int i : e.getValue()) {
                    if (values.get(i) != e.getKey().intValue()) throw new AssertionError("wrong value at slot " + i);
                }
                total += e.getValue().size();
            }
            if (total != values.size()) throw new AssertionError("sets do not cover the array");
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: inserts report true only for the first occurrence.
        RandomizedCollection c = new RandomizedCollection();
        boolean[] ins = {c.insert(4), c.insert(4), c.insert(6), c.insert(4)};
        if (!(ins[0] && !ins[1] && ins[2] && !ins[3])) throw new AssertionError("insert results");
        // The greatest index of 4 is 3, which is the last slot, so no value moves.
        if (c.remove(4) != 2) throw new AssertionError("remaining count");
        if (!c.snapshot().equals(List.of(4, 4, 6))) throw new AssertionError("example 1 array");
        c.check();
        // Example 2: the next removal takes index 1, so the last value 6 moves into slot 1.
        if (c.remove(4) != 1) throw new AssertionError("remaining 1");
        if (!c.snapshot().equals(List.of(4, 6))) throw new AssertionError("example 2 array");
        if (c.remove(9) != -1) throw new AssertionError("absent value");
        c.check();
        // A HashSet iterates by bucket, not by insertion order: 9 goes in first and 2 comes out first.
        HashSet<Integer> hs = new HashSet<>();
        hs.add(9);
        hs.add(2);
        if (hs.iterator().next() != 2) throw new AssertionError("HashSet order follows buckets");
        // A TreeSet returns the greatest element directly.
        TreeSet<Integer> ts = new TreeSet<>(List.of(9, 2, 5));
        if (ts.last() != 9) throw new AssertionError("greatest element");
        // The pick is proportional to the count: value 4 fills 3 of 4 slots.
        RandomizedCollection w = new RandomizedCollection();
        w.insert(4); w.insert(4); w.insert(4); w.insert(6);
        Random seeded = new Random(381);
        int fours = 0;
        for (int i = 0; i < 8_000; i++) if (w.getRandom(seeded) == 4) fours++;
        double z = (fours - 6_000.0) / Math.sqrt(8_000 * 0.75 * 0.25);
        if (Math.abs(z) > 3.3) throw new AssertionError("pick is proportional to the count");
        // Random scripts against a plain list that removes lastIndexOf and swaps with the last element.
        Random rnd = new Random(33_054);
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            RandomizedCollection s = new RandomizedCollection();
            ArrayList<Integer> model = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = 0; step < 60; step++) {
                int v = rnd.nextInt(5);
                int op = rnd.nextInt(3);
                if (op == 0) {
                    boolean expect = !model.contains(v);
                    if (s.insert(v) != expect) throw new AssertionError("insert");
                    model.add(v);
                } else if (op == 1) {
                    // lastIndexOf(Object) finds the greatest index in O(n), fine for an oracle.
                    int at = model.lastIndexOf(v);
                    int expectRemaining = -1;
                    if (at >= 0) {
                        model.set(at, model.get(model.size() - 1));
                        model.remove(model.size() - 1);
                        expectRemaining = Collections.frequency(model, v);
                    }
                    if (s.remove(v) != expectRemaining) throw new AssertionError("remaining count");
                } else if (!model.isEmpty()) {
                    long seed = rnd.nextLong();
                    int expect = model.get(new Random(seed).nextInt(model.size()));
                    if (s.getRandom(new Random(seed)) != expect) throw new AssertionError("pick");
                }
                if (!s.snapshot().equals(model)) throw new AssertionError("array order");
                s.check();
            }
        }
    }
}verified

Evict The Least Recent Entry

ProblemSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Why Scanning For A Victim Is Slow

A profile service keeps recent user records in memory so that most requests skip the database. The cache holds 10,000 records. When record 10,001 arrives, the service must drop one, and the best candidate is the record that nobody has asked for the longest time. The first version finds that record by scanning a timestamp stored on every entry.

Under load, every miss touches ten thousand entries, and the latency of the whole service follows the scan. The cache exists to save time, and its own bookkeeping spends the savings. This lesson answers one question. How can a lookup, a refresh and an eviction each finish in constant time, both when the rule is "least recently used" and when the rule is "least frequently used"?

Simple approach

Keeping Usage Order In An Array List

The simplest design stores values in a hash map and keeps the keys in an ArrayList ordered from least recent to most recent. Every use of a key removes it from the list and appends it at the end. An eviction removes index 0.

import java.util.*;

final class ListCache {
    private final int capacity;
    private final Map<Integer, Integer> values = new HashMap<>();
    private final List<Integer> order = new ArrayList<>();   // index 0 is the least recently used key

    ListCache(int capacity) { this.capacity = capacity; }

    int get(int key) {
        if (!values.containsKey(key)) return -1;
        order.remove(Integer.valueOf(key));   // scans the list to find the key
        order.add(key);
        return values.get(key);
    }

    void put(int key, int value) {
        if (values.containsKey(key)) order.remove(Integer.valueOf(key));
        else if (values.size() == capacity) values.remove(order.remove(0));   // shifts every later key left
        values.put(key, value);
        order.add(key);
    }
}

The behavior is correct. After put(1, 10), put(2, 20) and get(1) on a cache of capacity 2, the list reads [2, 1], so a third key evicts key 2.

Predict first: A cache of capacity 10,000 is full and receives a get for a key that was used a moment ago. How many list slots does the get touch, and does it matter that the hash map answers in constant time?

The get scans up to 10,000 list slots to find the key and then shifts later slots to close the gap. The hash map's constant lookup does not help, because the list update dominates the cost.

Bottleneck

Where The List Spends Its Time

The hash map finds a value in O(1) expected time, but the list cannot find a key without a search. The call order.remove(Integer.valueOf(key)) scans from the front, which costs O(n) for a cache of n entries. The call order.remove(0) is also O(n), because an array-backed list shifts every later element one slot to the left.

So get, put and eviction each cost O(n), even though the cache was built to answer in constant time. A cache of 10,000 entries wastes up to ten thousand steps on every hit, and hits are the common case. The data itself is fine. The order is stored in a shape that cannot move one element without visiting its neighbors. The fix has to let the cache move a known entry in constant time, which requires a way to reach that entry directly and a shape that does not shift others when it leaves.

Key idea

Pair A Map With Linked Nodes

Using One Map And One Recency List

The least recent use order needs a structure where removing a known element is O(1). A doubly linked list gives that, because a node knows both neighbors and can unlink itself by rewriting two links. The list is not searched, because the hash map takes the key straight to its node.

A recency list is a doubly linked list ordered from most recently used at the front to least recently used at the back. A use of key k finds the node through the map, detaches it and reinserts it at the front. An eviction detaches the node at the back and deletes its key from the map.

A sentinel is a dummy node at each end of the list that never holds a key. With a sentinel at the front and one at the back, every real node always has a previous and a next node. Insertion and removal then follow one code path with no null checks, including when the list has one node or none.

Adding Counts For Frequency Eviction

The least frequently used rule evicts the key with the fewest uses. Among keys with the same count, it evicts the one used longest ago. A frequency bucket is the set of all keys that currently share one use count, ordered from least recent at its start to most recent at its end. A map goes from each count to its bucket.

A use of key k with count f moves the key from bucket f to the end of bucket f + 1. An integer called the minimum frequency names the smallest count that has a non-empty bucket. Eviction takes the first key of that bucket. When bucket f empties and f was the minimum, the moved node is now in bucket f + 1, so the minimum becomes f + 1. A brand-new key sets the minimum to 1.

State

State The Two Caches Keep

Each design keeps a small set of fields, and the operations must restore the same facts after every call.

  • byKey is the map from a key to its node, so no operation searches a list.
  • first and last are the two sentinels, and first.after is the most recent node.
  • capacity is the fixed maximum number of keys, and eviction runs only when a new key arrives at that size.
  • countOf is the LFU map from a key to its use count.
  • bucketOf is the LFU map from a use count to its bucket of keys.
  • smallest is the minimum frequency, the smallest count that has a non-empty bucket.

The invariant for the LRU cache is that the map and the list hold exactly the same keys, each once. For the LFU cache, each key also sits in exactly one bucket, the bucket of its count.

The traces use shorter labels. The label order shows the LRU list from first.after toward last, and the label counts shows countOf.

Walkthrough

Tracing An LRU And An LFU Cache

Stepping Through The LRU Cache

The first trace uses an LRU cache with capacity 2 and the keys [1, 2, 1, 3, 2, 4]. The first two keys are puts of values 10 and 20, and the third is a get(1). The fourth is put(3, 30), the fifth is get(2) and the sixth is put(4, 40). The note on each step shows the order from most recent to least recent.

When key 3 arrives, the cache is full and key 2 is at the back, so key 2 leaves. The later get(2) is a miss and changes nothing. The final put evicts key 1, which has been at the back since key 3 arrived.

Stepping Through The LFU Cache

The second trace uses an LFU cache with capacity 2 and the keys [1, 2, 2, 1, 3, 4]. Steps one and two put keys 1 and 2. Step three is get(2) and step four is get(1), so both keys reach count 2, and key 2 reached it first. Step five puts key 3 and step six puts key 4.

When key 3 arrives, both keys sit in bucket 2, and the first key of that bucket is key 2, so key 2 leaves and key 3 enters bucket 1. The minimum becomes 1. When key 4 arrives, bucket 1 holds only key 3, so key 3 leaves.

Code

Implementing The Cache Moves

The LRU cache needs three private moves: cut a node out, push a node to the front, and evict the oldest node. All three are constant-time link rewrites. The LFU cache adds a fourth move, bump, after the first code block.

import java.util.*;

final class LruCore {
    private static final class Entry { int key, value; Entry before, after; }
    private final Entry first = new Entry(), last = new Entry();   // sentinels that never hold data
    private final Map<Integer, Entry> byKey = new HashMap<>();

    LruCore() { first.after = last; last.before = first; }

    private void cut(Entry e) { e.before.after = e.after; e.after.before = e.before; }

    private void pushFront(Entry e) {
        e.before = first; e.after = first.after;
        first.after.before = e; first.after = e;
    }

    private Entry evictOldest() {
        Entry victim = last.before;   // the node next to the back sentinel is the least recent one
        cut(victim);
        byKey.remove(victim.key);
        return victim;
    }
}

The LFU cache adds one more move. It takes a key out of its current bucket, adds one to the count, and appends the key to the next bucket.

import java.util.*;

final class LfuCore {
    private final Map<Integer, Integer> countOf = new HashMap<>();
    private final Map<Integer, LinkedHashSet<Integer>> bucketOf = new HashMap<>();
    private int smallest;   // the minimum frequency

    void bump(int key) {
        int c = countOf.get(key);
        LinkedHashSet<Integer> from = bucketOf.get(c);
        from.remove(key);
        if (from.isEmpty()) { bucketOf.remove(c); if (smallest == c) smallest = c + 1; }
        countOf.put(key, c + 1);
        bucketOf.computeIfAbsent(c + 1, x -> new LinkedHashSet<>()).add(key);   // end of the set is the most recent
    }
}

All four moves cost O(1) expected, and none of them searches a list.

When to use it

Recognizing When A Cache Fits

Spotting The Pattern

Use this design when a lookup by key and an eviction by usage must both be constant time. The invariant is a fact that every operation must restore before it returns. Here it states that the map and the list hold the same keys once each, the list order matches the order of use, and the minimum frequency, when present, matches a non-empty bucket.

A false friend is a technique that looks right for the job and breaks one promise of the contract. A TreeMap from timestamp to key finds the oldest entry, but each refresh costs O(log n) and two uses in the same tick collide. A priority queue keyed by count makes ties arbitrary and cannot remove an arbitrary key in constant time.

Checking The Java Library Shortcut

The class LinkedHashMap with access order enabled and an overridden removeEldestEntry implements an LRU cache in a few lines. Its contract is narrower than the design above. A get counts as a structural change, so a get inside a loop over its keys throws ConcurrentModificationException. The call containsKey does not refresh the order, and there is no read that skips the refresh, so a peek operation needs another design.

Keys also need correct equals and hashCode. A key class that overrides neither is compared by identity, so a lookup with an equal-looking new object misses. A key that changes after insertion lands in the wrong bucket. Use record keys or immutable fields.

The use count is an int. It overflows past 2147483647, and a cache that serves that many uses of one key needs a long. The exercises below cap the operation count far below that.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Move Node To Front

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Implement a class FrontList that stores distinct int keys in a doubly linked list with a front sentinel and a back sentinel. The operation addFront(key) inserts a new node at the front and returns the node. The operation moveToFront(node) detaches a node that is in the list and reinserts it at the front. The operation removeLast() removes the node at the back and returns its key. The operations frontToBack() and backToFront() return the keys as lists in each direction.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Keys are distinct int values, and each key is added once.
  • Node passed to moveToFront belongs to this list.
  • Empty list makes removeLast throw NoSuchElementException.
  • First node may be passed to moveToFront, and the list stays unchanged.
  • Cost is O(1) per operation, except the two walking operations.

Example 1. Input: add keys 1, 2, 3, then call moveToFront on the node of key 1 and removeLast(). Output: frontToBack() was [3, 2, 1] before the move and is [1, 3, 2] after it, and removeLast() returns 2.

Example 2. Input: the list [1, 3] and a call of moveToFront on the node of key 1, which is already first. Output: frontToBack() stays [1, 3] and backToFront() is [3, 1].

Prerequisites. The sentinel list and the node moves in this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. First exercise in the sequence: introduces the sentinel list and the detach-then-insert move without any map.

Hint

Which two links change when a node leaves, and which four change when it enters? Does the order of the two steps matter when the node is already first?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

The list owns two sentinel nodes, head and tail, that never hold a key. Because they always exist, every real node has a non-null prev and next, and no operation tests for null. The invariant is that following next from head reaches tail, and following prev from tail reaches head, and both walks visit the same real nodes in opposite order.

moveToFront runs two steps in a fixed order. The detach step makes the node's prev and next neighbors point at each other, which uses all four links around the node. The reinsert step then links the node between head and head.next, which again rewrites four links. Detaching first and inserting second also handles a node that is already first, because the detach step leaves it isolated and the insert step puts it back in the same place. The operation removeLast detaches tail.prev, so the least recent key leaves in constant time.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) per operation, because each operation rewrites a fixed number of links and never walks the list.
  • Space is O(1) auxiliary per operation, because only the nodes themselves exist and no operation allocates more than one node.
import java.util.*;

public final class FrontList {
    /** One list node. The key is fixed; the two links change as the node moves. */
    static final class Node {
        final int key;
        Node prev, next;
        Node(int key) { this.key = key; }
    }

    // The sentinels never hold data, so real nodes always have two non-null neighbors.
    private final Node head = new Node(-1);
    private final Node tail = new Node(-1);
    private int size;

    FrontList() {
        // An empty list is head linked to tail in both directions.
        head.next = tail;
        tail.prev = head;
    }

    /**
     * Creates a node for key at the front and returns it.
     * Time: O(1). Space: O(1). Invariant: forward and backward walks agree.
     */
    Node addFront(int key) {
        Node node = new Node(key);
        insertAfterHead(node);
        size++;
        return node;
    }

    /**
     * Moves a node that is currently in this list to the front.
     * Time: O(1), eight link writes at most. Space: O(1).
     * Invariant: the node appears exactly once after the call.
     */
    void moveToFront(Node node) {
        // Detach first so the old neighbors stop pointing at the node.
        detach(node);
        // Reinsert second; this also works when the node was already first.
        insertAfterHead(node);
    }

    /** Removes and returns the key at the back. Time: O(1). Space: O(1). */
    int removeLast() {
        // An empty list has no back node to remove.
        if (size == 0) throw new NoSuchElementException("empty list");
        Node last = tail.prev;
        detach(last);
        size--;
        return last.key;
    }

    private void detach(Node n) {
        // The two neighbors now point at each other; the node's own links are overwritten on reinsert.
        n.prev.next = n.next;
        n.next.prev = n.prev;
    }

    private void insertAfterHead(Node n) {
        // Link the node to its new neighbors first, then link the neighbors back to it.
        n.prev = head;
        n.next = head.next;
        head.next.prev = n;
        head.next = n;
    }

    List<Integer> frontToBack() {
        List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
        // Follow next links until the tail sentinel is reached.
        for (Node n = head.next; n != tail; n = n.next) out.add(n.key);
        return out;
    }

    List<Integer> backToFront() {
        List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
        // Follow prev links until the head sentinel is reached.
        for (Node n = tail.prev; n != head; n = n.prev) out.add(n.key);
        return out;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: three adds, move the oldest to the front, then remove the back.
        FrontList list = new FrontList();
        Node one = list.addFront(1);
        list.addFront(2);
        Node three = list.addFront(3);
        if (!list.frontToBack().equals(List.of(3, 2, 1))) throw new AssertionError("after adds");
        list.moveToFront(one);
        if (!list.frontToBack().equals(List.of(1, 3, 2))) throw new AssertionError("after move");
        if (list.removeLast() != 2) throw new AssertionError("removeLast");
        // Example 2: moving the node that is already first changes nothing.
        list.moveToFront(one);
        if (!list.frontToBack().equals(List.of(1, 3))) throw new AssertionError("first node move");
        if (!list.backToFront().equals(List.of(3, 1))) throw new AssertionError("backward walk");
        // Removing from an empty list fails loudly.
        FrontList empty = new FrontList();
        try { empty.removeLast(); throw new AssertionError("expected failure"); } catch (NoSuchElementException expected) { }

        // Random operations against an ArrayList model that stores the front at index 0.
        Random rnd = new Random(3306);
        for (int round = 0; round < 200; round++) {
            FrontList real = new FrontList();
            List<Integer> model = new ArrayList<>();
            Map<Integer, Node> nodes = new HashMap<>();
            int nextKey = 0;
            for (int step = 0; step < 150; step++) {
                int op = rnd.nextInt(3);
                if (op == 0 && model.size() < 12) {
                    // Add a fresh key at the front in both structures.
                    nodes.put(nextKey, real.addFront(nextKey));
                    model.add(0, nextKey++);
                } else if (op == 1 && !model.isEmpty()) {
                    // Move a random present key to the front in both structures.
                    int key = model.get(rnd.nextInt(model.size()));
                    real.moveToFront(nodes.get(key));
                    model.remove(Integer.valueOf(key));
                    model.add(0, key);
                } else if (op == 2 && !model.isEmpty()) {
                    // Remove from the back in both structures.
                    int expected = model.remove(model.size() - 1);
                    if (real.removeLast() != expected) throw new AssertionError("removeLast mismatch");
                    nodes.remove(expected);
                }
                // Both walks must match the model, and the backward walk must be its reverse.
                if (!real.frontToBack().equals(model)) throw new AssertionError("forward mismatch");
                List<Integer> reversed = new ArrayList<>(model);
                Collections.reverse(reversed);
                if (!real.backToFront().equals(reversed)) throw new AssertionError("backward mismatch");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

LRU Cache With Peek

LeetCode 146

Problem Specification. Implement a class RecencyCache with a fixed capacity. The operation get(key) returns the value or -1 and refreshes the key. The operation put(key, value) stores the value, refreshes the key, and evicts the least recent key first when a new key arrives at capacity. The operation peek(key) returns the value or -1 and does not refresh. The operation keysByRecency() returns the stored keys with the most recent first.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Capacity is an int with 1 <= capacity <= 10^4.
  • Keys and values are int, with values in the range 0 to 10^9, so -1 means absent.
  • Overwrite of a stored key refreshes it and never evicts.
  • Cost is O(1) expected for get, put and peek.

Example 1. Input: capacity 2 and the calls put(1, 10), put(2, 20), get(1) and put(3, 30). Output: get(1) returns 10, keysByRecency() is [3, 1] and peek(2) is -1.

Example 2. Input: capacity 2 and the calls put(1, 10), put(2, 20), peek(1) and put(3, 30). Output: peek(1) returns 10 and keysByRecency() is [3, 2], so key 1 was evicted, because peek did not refresh it.

Prerequisites. The move-to-front exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. Adds an operation that reads without refreshing, so order becomes observable and the refresh moves from every read to only get and overwrite.

Hint

Which of the three read and write operations touch the links? Which one must leave them alone?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

The state is a map from key to node and a doubly linked list with two sentinels, ordered from most recent at the front to least recent at the back. The invariant is that the map holds exactly the keys in the list, each once, and the list order is the order of last refresh. The operations get and an overwriting put both detach the node and reinsert it at the front, so they refresh. The operation peek reads the node's value and touches no link, so it does not refresh. That one difference is the changed contract, and it makes the order observable through keysByRecency.

A put of a new key first checks index.size() == capacity. If the cache is full, it detaches tail.prev, which is the least recent node, and removes that key from the map before inserting the new node. The eviction happens before the insert, so the map never holds more than capacity keys.

The main method also checks the library shortcut. A LinkedHashMap built with access order and a removeEldestEntry override matches the same random script, which shows that the shortcut honors this contract for get and put only. It has no operation that reads without refreshing the order. The main method also shows why a key class needs equals and hashCode.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) expected per operation, because a hash lookup plus a fixed number of link writes does all the work; keysByRecency is O(size) because it reports every key.
  • Space is O(capacity), because the map and the list each hold at most capacity entries.
import java.util.*;

public final class RecencyCache {
    private static final class Node {
        int key, value;
        Node prev, next;
    }

    private final int capacity;
    private final Map<Integer, Node> index = new HashMap<>();
    // Sentinels hold no data; head.next is the most recent node and tail.prev the least recent.
    private final Node head = new Node();
    private final Node tail = new Node();

    RecencyCache(int capacity) {
        // A cache of zero entries is outside this contract.
        if (capacity < 1) throw new IllegalArgumentException("capacity must be at least 1");
        this.capacity = capacity;
        head.next = tail;
        tail.prev = head;
    }

    private void unlink(Node n) {
        // Connect the two neighbors to each other.
        n.prev.next = n.next;
        n.next.prev = n.prev;
    }

    private void linkFront(Node n) {
        // Place the node directly after head and repair both neighbors.
        n.prev = head;
        n.next = head.next;
        head.next.prev = n;
        head.next = n;
    }

    /**
     * Returns the value and refreshes the key, or -1 when absent.
     * Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1). Invariant: the list holds exactly the map's keys.
     */
    int get(int key) {
        Node n = index.get(key);
        // A miss changes nothing.
        if (n == null) return -1;
        // A hit moves the node to the front.
        unlink(n);
        linkFront(n);
        return n.value;
    }

    /** Returns the value without refreshing, or -1 when absent. Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1). */
    int peek(int key) {
        Node n = index.get(key);
        // No link is touched, so the order stays the same.
        return n == null ? -1 : n.value;
    }

    /**
     * Stores the value and refreshes the key; evicts the least recent key when a new key arrives at capacity.
     * Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1).
     */
    void put(int key, int value) {
        Node n = index.get(key);
        if (n != null) {
            // Overwrite keeps the node, replaces the value and refreshes the order.
            n.value = value;
            unlink(n);
            linkFront(n);
            return;
        }
        if (index.size() == capacity) {
            // Evict the back node first, so the map never exceeds capacity.
            Node victim = tail.prev;
            unlink(victim);
            index.remove(victim.key);
        }
        n = new Node();
        n.key = key;
        n.value = value;
        index.put(key, n);
        linkFront(n);
    }

    /** Lists keys with the most recent first. Time: O(size). Space: O(size) for the result. */
    List<Integer> keysByRecency() {
        List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
        // Walk from the front sentinel to the back sentinel.
        for (Node n = head.next; n != tail; n = n.next) out.add(n.key);
        return out;
    }

    /** Library shortcut: access-ordered LinkedHashMap that drops the eldest entry after an insert. */
    static final class Shortcut extends LinkedHashMap<Integer, Integer> {
        private final int limit;
        Shortcut(int limit) {
            // The third constructor argument true selects access order instead of insertion order.
            super(16, 0.75f, true);
            this.limit = limit;
        }
        @Override
        protected boolean removeEldestEntry(Map.Entry<Integer, Integer> eldest) {
            return size() > limit;
        }
    }

    /** Key class with no equals or hashCode, so lookup uses object identity. */
    static final class RawKey {
        final int id;
        RawKey(int id) { this.id = id; }
    }

    record ValueKey(int id) { }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: get refreshes, so key 2 is evicted when key 3 arrives.
        RecencyCache a = new RecencyCache(2);
        a.put(1, 10);
        a.put(2, 20);
        if (a.get(1) != 10) throw new AssertionError("get value");
        a.put(3, 30);
        if (!a.keysByRecency().equals(List.of(3, 1))) throw new AssertionError("example 1 order");
        if (a.peek(2) != -1) throw new AssertionError("evicted key");
        // Example 2: peek does not refresh, so key 1 is evicted even though it was just read.
        RecencyCache b = new RecencyCache(2);
        b.put(1, 10);
        b.put(2, 20);
        if (b.peek(1) != 10) throw new AssertionError("peek value");
        b.put(3, 30);
        if (!b.keysByRecency().equals(List.of(3, 2))) throw new AssertionError("example 2 order");
        // Overwriting refreshes: key 2 moves ahead of key 3.
        b.put(2, 25);
        if (!b.keysByRecency().equals(List.of(2, 3)) || b.peek(2) != 25) throw new AssertionError("overwrite refresh");

        // The shortcut matches the contract for get and put: access order refreshes on get, containsKey does not.
        Shortcut s = new Shortcut(2);
        s.put(1, 10);
        s.put(2, 20);
        s.containsKey(1);
        s.put(3, 30);
        if (s.containsKey(1)) throw new AssertionError("containsKey must not refresh");
        s.clear();
        s.put(1, 10);
        s.put(2, 20);
        s.get(1);
        s.put(3, 30);
        if (s.containsKey(2) || !s.containsKey(1)) throw new AssertionError("get must refresh");
        // A get during iteration of an access-ordered map is a structural change.
        Shortcut it = new Shortcut(5);
        it.put(1, 1);
        it.put(2, 2);
        it.put(3, 3);
        try {
            for (int k : it.keySet()) it.get(1);
            throw new AssertionError("expected ConcurrentModificationException");
        } catch (ConcurrentModificationException expected) { }
        // Keys without equals and hashCode miss; a record key hits.
        Map<Object, Integer> keyed = new HashMap<>();
        keyed.put(new RawKey(1), 1);
        if (keyed.get(new RawKey(1)) != null) throw new AssertionError("identity lookup");
        keyed.put(new ValueKey(1), 2);
        if (keyed.get(new ValueKey(1)) != 2) throw new AssertionError("value lookup");

        // Random scripts against a list model whose index 0 is the most recent entry.
        Random rnd = new Random(1460);
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            int cap = 1 + rnd.nextInt(4);
            RecencyCache real = new RecencyCache(cap);
            Shortcut lib = new Shortcut(cap);
            List<int[]> model = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = 0; step < 120; step++) {
                int key = rnd.nextInt(7);
                int op = rnd.nextInt(3);
                int at = -1;
                // Find the key in the model by scanning, which is the slow but obviously right way.
