                for (int i = 0; i < model.size(); i++) if (model.get(i)[0] == key) at = i;
                if (op == 0) {
                    int expected = at < 0 ? -1 : model.get(at)[1];
                    if (real.get(key) != expected) throw new AssertionError("get mismatch");
                    Integer got = lib.get(key);
                    if ((got == null ? -1 : got) != expected) throw new AssertionError("shortcut get mismatch");
                    // A hit moves the entry to index 0.
                    if (at >= 0) model.add(0, model.remove(at));
                } else if (op == 1) {
                    int value = rnd.nextInt(100);
                    real.put(key, value);
                    lib.put(key, value);
                    if (at >= 0) model.remove(at);
                    // A new key at capacity removes the last entry first.
                    else if (model.size() == cap) model.remove(model.size() - 1);
                    model.add(0, new int[] {key, value});
                } else {
                    int expected = at < 0 ? -1 : model.get(at)[1];
                    if (real.peek(key) != expected) throw new AssertionError("peek mismatch");
                }
                // The observable order must match after every operation.
                List<Integer> want = new ArrayList<>();
                for (int[] e : model) want.add(e[0]);
                if (!real.keysByRecency().equals(want)) throw new AssertionError("order mismatch");
                List<Integer> libKeys = new ArrayList<>(lib.keySet());
                Collections.reverse(libKeys);
                if (!libKeys.equals(want)) throw new AssertionError("shortcut order mismatch");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Capacity One And Repeated Get

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Implement a class SlotCache that stores its list links in int arrays indexed by slot number, with a head slot and a tail slot as sentinels. The operations get and put follow the least recently used rule. The operations forwardCount() and backwardCount() return the number of data slots reached by walking from each sentinel to the other. Both must equal the number of stored keys after every operation, including capacity one and repeated get calls on the same key.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Capacity is an int with 1 <= capacity <= 100.
  • Keys and values are int, and a miss returns -1.
  • Walk fails with an exception when it visits more than capacity data slots, because a legal walk from one sentinel to the other crosses at most capacity + 1 links.
  • Overwrite of the stored key keeps the size and never evicts.

Example 1. Input: capacity 1 and the calls put(5, 50), get(5) twice, and put(6, 60). Output: the later get(5) is -1, get(6) is 60, and both counts are 1.

Example 2. Input: capacity 1 and the calls put(5, 50) and put(5, 51). Output: get(5) is 51 and both counts are 1.

Prerequisites. The two exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The cache shrinks to the smallest legal size, where the front node and back node are the same, and the check moves from values to the link structure.

Hint

What would happen to a slot's links if a refresh only inserted and never detached? At capacity one, which slot is both the first and the last?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

This version stores the links in int arrays instead of node objects. Slots 0 to capacity - 1 hold entries, and two extra slots, head and tail, serve as sentinels. The map takes a key to its slot. The invariant is that every occupied slot appears exactly once on the forward walk from head and exactly once on the backward walk from tail, and both walks have length size.

Repeated get of the same key is safe because the refresh is detach followed by insert, and detaching a slot always leaves it unlinked before the insert links it again. A refresh that only inserted would add a second link to the same slot and create a cycle. At capacity one the only slot is both the first and the last, so tail's previous slot is that slot. The eviction path detaches it, removes its key from the map, and reuses the same slot for the new key. An overwrite of the stored key never reaches the eviction path, so it keeps size one.

The methods forwardCount and backwardCount walk each direction and stop with an exception when they visit more than capacity data slots. A full legal walk crosses capacity + 1 links, because capacity data slots and two sentinels form a chain of capacity + 2 nodes. So a broken cycle fails the check and cannot loop forever.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) expected for get and put, because the map lookup and the link writes are constant; the two count methods are O(size) and exist for checking.
  • Space is O(capacity), because the four int arrays and the map all have at most capacity entries.
import java.util.*;

public final class SlotCache {
    private final int capacity;
    private final int[] keyAt, valueAt, before, after;
    private final Map<Integer, Integer> slotOf = new HashMap<>();
    // head and tail are slot numbers just past the data slots.
    private final int head, tail;
    private int size;

    SlotCache(int capacity) {
        if (capacity < 1) throw new IllegalArgumentException("capacity must be at least 1");
        this.capacity = capacity;
        keyAt = new int[capacity];
        valueAt = new int[capacity];
        before = new int[capacity + 2];
        after = new int[capacity + 2];
        head = capacity;
        tail = capacity + 1;
        // An empty list links the two sentinels to each other.
        after[head] = tail;
        before[tail] = head;
    }

    private void cut(int s) {
        // Neighbors skip the slot; its own links are rewritten on the next insert.
        after[before[s]] = after[s];
        before[after[s]] = before[s];
    }

    private void putFirst(int s) {
        // Four writes place slot s directly after head.
        before[s] = head;
        after[s] = after[head];
        before[after[head]] = s;
        after[head] = s;
    }

    /**
     * Returns the value and refreshes the key, or -1 when absent.
     * Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1). Invariant: each slot is linked exactly once.
     */
    int get(int key) {
        Integer s = slotOf.get(key);
        if (s == null) return -1;
        // Detach then insert: skipping the detach would link the slot twice.
        cut(s);
        putFirst(s);
        return valueAt[s];
    }

    /**
     * Stores or overwrites a value; at capacity a new key reuses the slot of the least recent entry.
     * Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1).
     */
    void put(int key, int value) {
        Integer existing = slotOf.get(key);
        if (existing != null) {
            // Overwrite keeps the slot and refreshes the order.
            valueAt[existing] = value;
            cut(existing);
            putFirst(existing);
            return;
        }
        int s;
        if (size < capacity) {
            // A free slot exists, so take the next unused number.
            s = size++;
        } else {
            // The cache is full: the slot just before tail holds the least recent key.
            s = before[tail];
            cut(s);
            slotOf.remove(keyAt[s]);
        }
        keyAt[s] = key;
        valueAt[s] = value;
        slotOf.put(key, s);
        putFirst(s);
    }

    int size() { return size; }

    /** Counts slots reachable forward from head; fails if a walk visits more than capacity data slots. */
    int forwardCount() {
        int count = 0;
        for (int s = after[head]; s != tail; s = after[s]) {
            if (++count > capacity) throw new IllegalStateException("cycle or duplicate link");
        }
        return count;
    }

    /** Counts slots reachable backward from tail; fails if a walk visits more than capacity data slots. */
    int backwardCount() {
        int count = 0;
        for (int s = before[tail]; s != head; s = before[s]) {
            if (++count > capacity) throw new IllegalStateException("cycle or duplicate link");
        }
        return count;
    }

    private static void check(SlotCache c, int expectedSize) {
        // The map, both walks and the size field must agree.
        if (c.size() != expectedSize || c.forwardCount() != expectedSize || c.backwardCount() != expectedSize) {
            throw new AssertionError("link count mismatch");
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: capacity one, repeated get, then a second key evicts the first.
        SlotCache one = new SlotCache(1);
        one.put(5, 50);
        if (one.get(5) != 50 || one.get(5) != 50) throw new AssertionError("repeated get");
        check(one, 1);
        one.put(6, 60);
        if (one.get(5) != -1 || one.get(6) != 60) throw new AssertionError("eviction at capacity one");
        check(one, 1);
        // Example 2: overwriting the only key keeps one link and does not evict.
        SlotCache two = new SlotCache(1);
        two.put(5, 50);
        two.put(5, 51);
        if (two.get(5) != 51) throw new AssertionError("overwrite value");
        check(two, 1);

        // Random scripts mostly made of get, against a model that stamps each touch with a counter.
        Random rnd = new Random(5301);
        for (int round = 0; round < 400; round++) {
            int cap = 1 + rnd.nextInt(3);
            SlotCache real = new SlotCache(cap);
            Map<Integer, Integer> values = new HashMap<>();
            Map<Integer, Long> stamp = new HashMap<>();
            long clock = 0;
            for (int step = 0; step < 150; step++) {
                int key = rnd.nextInt(cap + 2);
                if (rnd.nextInt(4) != 0) {
                    // A get returns the stored value or -1 and refreshes the stamp on a hit.
                    int expected = values.getOrDefault(key, -1);
                    if (real.get(key) != expected) throw new AssertionError("get mismatch");
                    if (expected != -1) stamp.put(key, ++clock);
                } else {
                    int value = rnd.nextInt(100);
                    real.put(key, value);
                    if (!values.containsKey(key) && values.size() == cap) {
                        // Evict the key with the smallest stamp.
                        int oldest = -1;
                        long best = Long.MAX_VALUE;
                        for (Map.Entry<Integer, Long> e : stamp.entrySet()) {
                            if (e.getValue() < best) { best = e.getValue(); oldest = e.getKey(); }
                        }
                        values.remove(oldest);
                        stamp.remove(oldest);
                    }
                    values.put(key, value);
                    stamp.put(key, ++clock);
                }
                check(real, values.size());
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

LFU Cache With Frequency Query

LeetCode 460

Problem Specification. Implement a class FrequencyCache with a fixed capacity. The operations get(key) and put(key, value) each count one use of the key when it is present, and a new key starts at count 1. When a new key arrives at capacity, the cache evicts the key with the smallest count, and ties go to the least recently used key among them. The operation frequency(key) returns the count or 0, and it counts no use.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Capacity is an int with 0 <= capacity <= 10^4, and capacity 0 accepts every operation and stores nothing.
  • Keys and values are int, with values in 0 to 10^9; a miss returns -1.
  • Operations number at most 200000, so no count can overflow an int.
  • Cost is O(1) expected per operation.

Example 1. Input: capacity 2 and the calls put(1, 10), put(2, 20), get(1) and put(3, 30). Output: frequency(1) is 2, frequency(2) is 0 and frequency(3) is 1.

Example 2. Input: capacity 2 and the calls put(1, 10), put(2, 20), get(2), get(1) and put(3, 30). Output: both old keys have count 2 and key 2 was used first, so a later get(2) is -1 and get(1) is 10.

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. Eviction depends on use counts first and recency second, so one list becomes one list per count plus a pointer to the smallest count.

Hint

When the list of keys with the smallest count empties, which count becomes the smallest? Which key sits at the start of its bucket after several uses?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach.

The state has three parts. A map takes each key to its value and its use count. A second map takes each count to an insertion-ordered set of the keys with that count, and each set serves as the list for its bucket. An integer minFreq names the smallest count that has a non-empty bucket. The invariant is that every stored key sits in exactly one bucket, the bucket matching its count, and minFreq is the smallest count of any stored key.

The helper touch moves a key from bucket f to bucket f + 1 and puts it at the end of the new bucket, which makes it the most recent key there. If bucket f becomes empty and f equals minFreq, then minFreq becomes f + 1, because the key that just moved is the only candidate for a new minimum. Both get and an overwriting put call touch. A new key always enters bucket 1 and sets minFreq to 1, which is correct after the eviction because the new key is the smallest count of any entry. At capacity, the victim is the first key of the minFreq bucket, which is the least recent among the keys with the smallest count. The operation frequency only reads the map, so it changes nothing. With capacity 0, put returns before touching any state, so get returns -1 and frequency returns 0.

The count is an int, and the main method shows that Integer.MAX_VALUE + 1 wraps negative. The constraints cap the script at 200000 operations, so no count can reach that limit.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) expected per operation, because a bucket lookup, a set removal, a set insertion and the first-element read each take constant time.
  • Space is O(capacity), because each stored key owns one entry and one bucket slot.
import java.util.*;

public final class FrequencyCache {
    private static final class Entry {
        int value;
        int count = 1;
        Entry(int value) { this.value = value; }
    }

    private final int capacity;
    private final Map<Integer, Entry> entries = new HashMap<>();
    // Each bucket keeps keys in insertion order, so the first key is the least recent one.
    private final Map<Integer, LinkedHashSet<Integer>> buckets = new HashMap<>();
    private int minFreq;

    FrequencyCache(int capacity) {
        if (capacity < 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("capacity must not be negative");
        this.capacity = capacity;
    }

    private void touch(int key, Entry e) {
        LinkedHashSet<Integer> old = buckets.get(e.count);
        old.remove(key);
        if (old.isEmpty()) {
            // The old bucket is gone; if it was the minimum, the moved key defines the new minimum.
            buckets.remove(e.count);
            if (minFreq == e.count) minFreq++;
        }
        e.count++;
        // Adding to the end makes the key the most recent one in its new bucket.
        buckets.computeIfAbsent(e.count, f -> new LinkedHashSet<>()).add(key);
    }

    /**
     * Returns the value and counts one use, or -1 when absent.
     * Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1). Invariant: each key sits in the bucket of its count.
     */
    int get(int key) {
        Entry e = entries.get(key);
        if (e == null) return -1;
        touch(key, e);
        return e.value;
    }

    /**
     * Stores a value; an existing key counts one use, a new key at capacity evicts first.
     * Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1).
     */
    void put(int key, int value) {
        // Capacity 0 is accepted and ignored.
        if (capacity == 0) return;
        Entry e = entries.get(key);
        if (e != null) {
            e.value = value;
            touch(key, e);
            return;
        }
        if (entries.size() == capacity) {
            // The victim is the first key of the smallest-count bucket.
            LinkedHashSet<Integer> lowest = buckets.get(minFreq);
            int victim = lowest.iterator().next();
            lowest.remove(victim);
            if (lowest.isEmpty()) buckets.remove(minFreq);
            entries.remove(victim);
        }
        entries.put(key, new Entry(value));
        buckets.computeIfAbsent(1, f -> new LinkedHashSet<>()).add(key);
        // The new key has count 1, which is now the smallest count.
        minFreq = 1;
    }

    /** Returns the use count of key, or 0 when absent; reading it counts no use. Time: O(1). Space: O(1). */
    int frequency(int key) {
        Entry e = entries.get(key);
        return e == null ? 0 : e.count;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: key 1 is used twice, so the single-use key 2 is evicted.
        FrequencyCache a = new FrequencyCache(2);
        a.put(1, 10);
        a.put(2, 20);
        if (a.get(1) != 10) throw new AssertionError("get value");
        a.put(3, 30);
        if (a.frequency(1) != 2 || a.frequency(2) != 0 || a.frequency(3) != 1) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        // Example 2: keys 1 and 2 both reach count 2, and key 2 was used first, so key 2 is evicted.
        FrequencyCache b = new FrequencyCache(2);
        b.put(1, 10);
        b.put(2, 20);
        b.get(2);
        b.get(1);
        b.put(3, 30);
        if (b.get(2) != -1 || b.get(1) != 10) throw new AssertionError("example 2 tie");
        // Capacity 0 accepts every operation and stores nothing.
        FrequencyCache zero = new FrequencyCache(0);
        zero.put(1, 10);
        if (zero.get(1) != -1 || zero.frequency(1) != 0) throw new AssertionError("capacity 0");
        // The count is an int that wraps past its maximum, which the operation limit prevents.
        if (Integer.MAX_VALUE + 1 >= 0) throw new AssertionError("int wrap");

        // Random scripts against a list model that scans for the smallest count, then the oldest touch.
        Random rnd = new Random(4601);
        for (int round = 0; round < 400; round++) {
            int cap = rnd.nextInt(4);
            FrequencyCache real = new FrequencyCache(cap);
            List<long[]> model = new ArrayList<>();
            long clock = 0;
            for (int step = 0; step < 120; step++) {
                int key = rnd.nextInt(6);
                int op = rnd.nextInt(3);
                int at = -1;
                for (int i = 0; i < model.size(); i++) if (model.get(i)[0] == key) at = i;
                if (op == 0) {
                    long expected = at < 0 ? -1 : model.get(at)[1];
                    if (real.get(key) != expected) throw new AssertionError("get mismatch");
                    if (at >= 0) { model.get(at)[2]++; model.get(at)[3] = ++clock; }
                } else if (op == 1) {
                    int value = rnd.nextInt(100);
                    real.put(key, value);
                    if (cap == 0) continue;
                    if (at >= 0) {
                        model.get(at)[1] = value;
                        model.get(at)[2]++;
                        model.get(at)[3] = ++clock;
                    } else {
                        if (model.size() == cap) {
                            // Evict the smallest count, breaking ties by the oldest touch.
                            int worst = 0;
                            for (int i = 1; i < model.size(); i++) {
                                long[] x = model.get(i), y = model.get(worst);
                                if (x[2] < y[2] || (x[2] == y[2] && x[3] < y[3])) worst = i;
                            }
                            model.remove(worst);
                        }
                        model.add(new long[] {key, value, 1, ++clock});
                    }
                } else {
                    long expected = at < 0 ? 0 : model.get(at)[2];
                    if (real.frequency(key) != expected) throw new AssertionError("frequency mismatch");
                }
                // After every operation all six keys must report the model's count.
                for (int k = 0; k < 6; k++) {
                    long want = 0;
                    for (long[] e : model) if (e[0] == k) want = e[2];
                    if (real.frequency(k) != want) throw new AssertionError("count mismatch");
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified

Unlocked Combinations

A product team needs a session cache that drops the oldest idle session, a raffle that draws a uniform winner and lets entrants withdraw, and a reader that pages through a nested menu one item at a time. Each of these three needs pairs two structures from this chapter. Three pairings are taught below. Use from several threads at once waits for a later chapter.

Pairings Taught In This Chapter

The lesson Locate By Key And Order By Recency joins the hash map with the doubly linked list. The map finds a node by key, and the list owns the recency order and removes a node in constant time. Its four exercises relink a node, resize a cache, check capacity one and log the evicted keys of a frequency cache.

The lesson Find A Slot And Pick From Array joins the hash map with the dynamic array. The map stores the slot of each value, and the array gives uniform selection and last-slot repair. Its four exercises remove by swap, pick a value different from a given one, remove from the last slot and hold duplicate values.

The lesson Traversal State Across Public Calls joins iterators with the tree walk, wrappers and nested lists. The next-element invariant has to hold across every call. Its four exercises iterate a tree stored in a node pool, wrap a source that cannot restart, flatten nested lists under a depth limit and rotate among several sources.

Pairings That Wait For A Later Chapter

Thread-safe designs change which operations are atomic and which lock owns each field. A later chapter on specialized services takes them, and this chapter assigns no problems to them.

Locate By Key And Order By Recency

ProblemWhat Each Technique AddsSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Why A Busy Cache Slows Down

A web service keeps the 10,000 most recently used user profiles in memory. Each request asks for one profile by its user id. A hit must return the profile and mark it as the freshest entry. A miss loads the profile from the database and, when the cache is full, drops the entry nobody has touched for the longest time.

The first version works in testing. Under production traffic, the cache itself becomes the slowest part of the request, and the profile at the database is not the cause. Each hit walks through thousands of entries before it returns. The question of this lesson is how one structure can answer "where is this key" and "which entry is oldest" at the same time, both in constant time.

What Each Technique Adds

What Each Earlier Lesson Contributes

Three earlier lessons of this chapter supply the parts. The lesson One Method Repairs Each Link taught that a single private method owns every pointer change, so no public operation touches a link by hand. The lesson Pair A Map With Ordered Storage taught that a hash map finds an entry by key while a second structure keeps the order. The lesson Evict The Least Recent Entry taught that the entry at the cold end of the order is the one to remove when the capacity is full.

This lesson joins the three. The map finds a node in O(1) average time. The doubly linked list removes that node and puts it at the hot end in O(1) time, because each node knows both of its neighbours. The new idea is that both structures hold the very same node objects.

Simple approach

Keeping Recency In A Plain List

The direct design stores the entries in one ArrayList, with the freshest entry at index 0. A lookup scans for the key. When it finds the entry, it removes the entry and inserts it again at index 0.

static int getNaive(java.util.ArrayList<int[]> entries, int key) {
    for (int i = 0; i < entries.size(); i++) {
        // Each entry is {key, value}; the scan stops at the first matching key.
        if (entries.get(i)[0] == key) {
            int[] hit = entries.remove(i);   // shifts every later entry one slot left
            entries.add(0, hit);             // shifts every entry one slot right
            return hit[1];
        }
    }
    return -1;
}

With entries {7,70}, {3,30}, {9,90} in that order, getNaive(entries, 9) returns 90 and leaves the list as {9,90}, {7,70}, {3,30}. The result is correct. Each call also touches every entry that sits in front of the match.

Predict first: A cache holds n entries and receives n requests, each for the oldest key. How many entries does the plain list touch for the whole run, and what does adding a HashMap from key to index change?

Each request scans n entries and then shifts n entries twice, so the run costs O(n^2). A HashMap from key to index removes the scan only. Every removal and reinsertion shifts the indexes of the entries behind it, so the stored indexes become wrong and the shifts still cost O(n).

Bottleneck

Two Questions That Need Two Structures

The plain list answers "which entry is oldest" in O(1), because the oldest entry sits at the end. It answers "where is this key" in O(n), because it must scan. Moving the hit to the front adds another O(n) for the shifts. A cache with 100,000 entries and a million requests performs on the order of 10^11 element moves, and the cost grows with the cache size.

A hash map alone has the opposite weakness. It finds a key in O(1) average time, yet it stores no order, so finding the oldest entry means visiting all n entries in O(n). A singly linked list also fails. Removing a node from the middle needs the node before it, and finding that node by walking from the head costs O(n). Each single structure answers one question quickly and the other slowly. The design needs both answers at once, and neither structure supplies both alone.

Key idea

One Node Shared By Two Structures

Sharing Node Identity

The fix is to keep two structures over the same objects. The map goes from each key to a node object, and the doubly linked list is made of those same node objects. The map does not store a copy of the node, and it does not store an index into a list. Because of this shared node identity, a map lookup returns the exact node that sits inside the list. That node already holds prev and next, so removing it needs no search, only two pointer writes.

Moving a node to the hot end is a detach followed by an insert. The detach connects the old neighbours to each other. The insert connects the node to the head and to the old first node.

Placing Sentinel Nodes

Two sentinel nodes make those writes uniform. A sentinel is a dummy node that never holds data. One sentinel marks the hot end and one marks the cold end. Every real node then has a real prev and a real next, even when the list holds one entry, so the detach and insert code has no null check and no special case for the first or last node.

Stating The Combined Invariant

The combined invariant says three things at once. The key set of the map equals the set of real nodes in the list. The list length equals the map size. A forward walk from the head sentinel and a backward walk from the tail sentinel visit the same nodes in opposite orders. Every operation ends by restoring this invariant, and the tests in this chapter check it after each call.

State

State Held By The Combined Cache

The class holds four fields and one private node class. The fields are reachable from the public operations, and the node class is the element type of the list.

  • index is a HashMap<Integer, Node> that maps each key to its node.
  • head is the sentinel before the hottest node, so head.next is the freshest entry.
  • tail is the sentinel after the coldest node, so tail.prev is the next entry to evict.
  • capacity is the largest number of entries the cache may hold.
  • Node is a private class holding key, value, prev and next.

The node stores its own key for one reason. When the cache evicts tail.prev, the code must delete that entry from the map, and the map needs the key. Without the stored key, eviction would search the map for the value and cost O(n).

Walkthrough

Tracing Hits, Misses And Eviction

Capacity Two With One Eviction

The first trace runs a cache of capacity 2 on the five requests listed in the cells. The requests are put 1, put 2, get 1, put 3 and get 2. The note on each step names the list as it reads from the hot end to the cold end.

The two puts leave the list as 2, then 1. The get 1 finds node 1 through the map, detaches it and inserts it at the hot end, so the list reads 1, 2. The put 3 finds the cache full, so it evicts the cold node 2, removes key 2 from the map and inserts node 3 at the hot end. The final get 2 misses and returns -1.

Capacity Three With Repeated Gets

The second trace uses capacity 3. It starts with three puts, put 1, put 2 and put 3, which fill the cache. It then calls get 1 twice in a row and closes with put 4. The first call moves node 1 to the hot end. The second call detaches that node and inserts it again at the same place, and the list stays correct. Then put 4 evicts key 2, because node 2 is the coldest node once key 1 is hot again.

Code

Implementing The Cache With Two Repair Methods

final class LruCache {
    private static final class Node {
        final int key;
        int value;
        Node prev, next;
        Node(int key, int value) { this.key = key; this.value = value; }
    }

    private final java.util.Map<Integer, Node> index = new java.util.HashMap<>();
    private final Node head = new Node(0, 0);   // sentinel on the hot side
    private final Node tail = new Node(0, 0);   // sentinel on the cold side
    private final int capacity;

    LruCache(int capacity) {
        this.capacity = capacity;
        head.next = tail;
        tail.prev = head;
    }

    // Repair method one: removes n from its position; n keeps stale links until reinserted.
    private void detach(Node n) {
        n.prev.next = n.next;
        n.next.prev = n.prev;
    }

    // Repair method two: places n directly after the head sentinel.
    private void insertFront(Node n) {
        n.prev = head;
        n.next = head.next;
        head.next.prev = n;
        head.next = n;
    }

    int get(int key) {
        Node n = index.get(key);
        if (n == null) return -1;
        detach(n);          // the map gave the exact node, so no search is needed
        insertFront(n);
        return n.value;
    }

    void put(int key, int value) {
        if (capacity == 0) return;   // a cache of capacity 0 stores nothing; without this guard eviction would detach a sentinel
        Node n = index.get(key);
        if (n != null) {            // update counts as a use
            n.value = value;
            detach(n);
            insertFront(n);
            return;
        }
        if (index.size() == capacity) {
            Node cold = tail.prev;   // coldest real node
            detach(cold);
            index.remove(cold.key);  // the stored key makes this removal O(1)
        }
        n = new Node(key, value);
        index.put(key, n);
        insertFront(n);
    }
}

The get and put operations each run in O(1) average time, because the map lookup is O(1) average and the pointer writes are O(1). The space is O(capacity) for the nodes plus the map. A detach rewrites two links and an insert at the front rewrites four. The put guard for capacity 0 matters because eviction would otherwise detach a sentinel and corrupt the list. The two private methods are the only code that changes prev and next, which is the rule from One Method Repairs Each Link.

When to use it

Recognizing When A Map Needs A List

Spotting The Cue

Use this pair when a problem asks for lookup by key and also for removal or reordering by position, with both in constant time. The cue words are "least recently used", "most recently used", "move to front" and "evict the oldest". An invariant is a fact that every operation must restore before it returns. Here the invariant is the combined one: the map keys and the list nodes are the same set, and the list length equals the map size.

Avoiding The False Friends

A false friend is a design that looks like it fits the cue and breaks a promise of the contract. Four designs qualify.

An ArrayDeque with remove(Object) keeps order, yet each removal scans the deque and costs O(n). A PriorityQueue ordered by timestamp costs O(log n) per touch, and an entry that is touched again leaves a stale copy behind. A singly linked list loses the O(1) detach because it has no prev. A second map from key to timestamp finds the key and still needs an O(n) scan for the minimum.

Handling Java Details

The class LinkedHashMap built with accessOrder set to true implements this same pair. Production code should prefer it, and an interview asks you to build the pair by hand. In your own build, keep the key inside the node, delete from the map before the node object is lost, and never let a public operation touch a link directly. A stale Node reference that stays in the map after eviction corrupts every later lookup. The same pair extends to frequency order when the list is replaced by one list per frequency, which the last exercise uses.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Detach And Reinsert

Author exercise

Problem Specification. A doubly linked list stores distinct integer keys between a head sentinel and a tail sentinel. Given the keys in order from head to tail and one key k that is present, move the node of k directly after the head sentinel using one detach and one insert. Return the keys in head-to-tail order after the move. The method must keep all four neighbour links correct: the old predecessor's next, the old successor's prev, and the moved node's own prev and next. Verify the result with a forward walk from the head and a backward walk from the tail, and require the backward walk to be the reverse of the forward walk.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Length is 1 <= keys.length <= 1000.
  • Keys are distinct int values.
  • Key k is present in keys.
  • Mutation is allowed on the list nodes, and keys is read only.
  • Return value is a new int[] of the same length.

Example 1. Input keys = [4,7,1,9] and k = 1, output [1,4,7,9].

Example 2. Input keys = [4,7,1,9] and k = 9, the cold-end node, output [9,4,7,1].

Prerequisites. The detach and insertFront methods in this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. First exercise in the sequence: the move is one detach and one insert, and a two-direction walk proves every link.

Hint

After the detach, the old predecessor and the old successor must point at each other. Which sentinel does the old successor equal when the moved node is the last real node?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The method builds one doubly linked list between two sentinels and a map from key to node. The map hands over the exact node of k, so no scan is needed. The detach connects the old predecessor and the old successor to each other. The insert connects the node to the head sentinel and to the old first node, and it fixes the old first node's prev and the head's next. Those are the four neighbour links. The invariant is that for every real node n, n.prev.next == n and n.next.prev == n. A forward walk and a backward walk then give the same keys in opposite orders, and the method asserts this after the move. A node already at the hot end is detached and reinserted without harm, and a node at the cold end works because the tail sentinel plays the successor.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(n) for building the list and O(1) for the move itself, because the detach and insert make a constant number of writes.
  • Space is O(n) for the nodes and the map, which the input size already requires.
import java.util.*;

public final class DetachReinsert {
    private static final class Node {
        final int key;
        Node prev, next;
        Node(int key) { this.key = key; }
    }

    /**
     * Moves the node of k directly after the head sentinel and returns the keys head to tail.
     * Time: O(n) to build, O(1) for the move. Space: O(n) for nodes and the map.
     * Invariant: for every node n, n.prev.next == n and n.next.prev == n.
     */
    static int[] moveToFront(int[] keys, int k) {
        // Two sentinels make every real node have a real prev and next.
        Node head = new Node(-1), tail = new Node(-1);
        head.next = tail;
        tail.prev = head;
        Map<Integer, Node> index = new HashMap<>();
        // Build the list by appending each key before the tail sentinel.
        for (int key : keys) {
            Node n = new Node(key);
            n.prev = tail.prev;
            n.next = tail;
            tail.prev.next = n;
            tail.prev = n;
            index.put(key, n);
        }
        // The map returns the exact node, so no scan happens.
        Node n = index.get(k);
        // Detach: the old predecessor and the old successor link to each other (two writes).
        n.prev.next = n.next;
        n.next.prev = n.prev;
        // Insert: link the node to the head and to the old first node (four more writes in total).
        n.prev = head;
        n.next = head.next;
        head.next.prev = n;
        head.next = n;
        // Walk both directions and prove that every link agrees.
        int[] forward = new int[keys.length];
        int i = 0;
        for (Node cur = head.next; cur != tail; cur = cur.next) {
            if (cur.prev.next != cur || cur.next.prev != cur) throw new AssertionError("link mismatch");
            forward[i++] = cur.key;
        }
        if (i != keys.length) throw new AssertionError("forward length");
        int j = keys.length;
        for (Node cur = tail.prev; cur != head; cur = cur.prev) {
            if (j == 0 || forward[--j] != cur.key) throw new AssertionError("backward is not the reverse");
        }
        if (j != 0) throw new AssertionError("backward length");
        return forward;
    }

    /** Oracle: remove from an ArrayList and insert at index 0. */
    static int[] oracle(int[] keys, int k) {
        List<Integer> list = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int x : keys) list.add(x);
        list.remove(Integer.valueOf(k));
        list.add(0, k);
        int[] out = new int[list.size()];
        for (int i = 0; i < out.length; i++) out[i] = list.get(i);
        return out;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // The two examples from the problem, recomputed here.
        if (!Arrays.equals(moveToFront(new int[] {4, 7, 1, 9}, 1), new int[] {1, 4, 7, 9})) throw new AssertionError("example 1");
        if (!Arrays.equals(moveToFront(new int[] {4, 7, 1, 9}, 9), new int[] {9, 4, 7, 1})) throw new AssertionError("example 2");
        // Boundary shapes: one node, and the key already at the hot end.
        if (!Arrays.equals(moveToFront(new int[] {5}, 5), new int[] {5})) throw new AssertionError("single node");
        if (!Arrays.equals(moveToFront(new int[] {4, 7}, 4), new int[] {4, 7})) throw new AssertionError("already first");
        // Random check against the list oracle with a fixed seed.
        Random rnd = new Random(33091);
        for (int t = 0; t < 2000; t++) {
            int n = 1 + rnd.nextInt(12);
            List<Integer> pool = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int i = 0; i < 40; i++) pool.add(i);
            Collections.shuffle(pool, rnd);
            int[] keys = new int[n];
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) keys[i] = pool.get(i);
            int k = keys[rnd.nextInt(n)];
            if (!Arrays.equals(moveToFront(keys, k), oracle(keys, k))) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

LRU Cache With Resize

LeetCode 146

Problem Specification. Design a cache with three operations. get(key) returns the value for the key, or -1 when absent, and marks the key as most recently used. put(key, value) inserts or updates the key, marks it most recently used, and evicts the least recently used key when the size would exceed the capacity. resize(newCapacity) sets the capacity to newCapacity and evicts from the least recent end until the size is at most newCapacity. It returns the evicted keys as an int[] in eviction order. When newCapacity is 0, the cache stores nothing and every put does nothing.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Initial capacity is 1 <= capacity <= 1000.
  • New capacity is 0 <= newCapacity <= 1000.
  • Keys and values are int values.
  • Eviction by put is silent and is not part of any returned array.
  • Cost of get and put is O(1) average, and resize costs O(evicted + 1).

Example 1. Input capacity 3, then put(1,10), put(2,20), put(3,30), get(1), resize(1). Output 10 for the get and [2,3] for the resize.

Example 2. Input capacity 2, then put(5,50), put(6,60), get(5), resize(0), get(5). Output 50, then [6,5], then -1.

Prerequisites. The cache in this lesson and the Detach And Reinsert exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. The capacity becomes mutable, so eviction moves from one step inside put to a loop whose removed keys are reported.

Hint

The cold end is tail.prev. A loop that repeats the same eviction step as put already gives eviction order.

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The cache keeps a map from key to node and a doubly linked list between two sentinels, with the hottest node next to the head. Two private methods, detach and insertFront, make every link change. A get or an update detaches the node and reinserts it at the hot end. A put of a new key first evicts tail.prev when the size equals the capacity, then inserts the new node. The new resize stores the new capacity and then repeats the same eviction step while the size exceeds it, appending each evicted key to the result. Eviction order is therefore coldest first. The combined invariant holds after every step: the map keys equal the list nodes, and the list length equals the map size. With capacity 0, a put returns at once, so the cache stays empty.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) average for get and put, and O(e + 1) for a resize that evicts e keys, since each eviction is one detach and one map removal.
  • Space is O(capacity) for the nodes and the map, and O(e) for the returned array.
import java.util.*;

public final class LruResize {
    private static final class Node {
        final int key;
        int value;
        Node prev, next;
        Node(int key, int value) { this.key = key; this.value = value; }
    }

    private final Map<Integer, Node> index = new HashMap<>();
    private final Node head = new Node(0, 0);
    private final Node tail = new Node(0, 0);
    private int capacity;

    LruResize(int capacity) {
        this.capacity = capacity;
        head.next = tail;
        tail.prev = head;
    }

    /** Detaches n from the list. Time: O(1). Space: O(1). Invariant: neighbours link to each other. */
    private void detach(Node n) {
        // The old predecessor and successor now point at each other.
        n.prev.next = n.next;
        n.next.prev = n.prev;
    }

    /** Inserts n after the head sentinel. Time: O(1). Space: O(1). */
    private void insertFront(Node n) {
        // Four writes: n.prev, n.next, the old first node's prev, and the head's next.
        n.prev = head;
        n.next = head.next;
        head.next.prev = n;
        head.next = n;
    }

    /** Removes the coldest node and returns its key. Time: O(1) average. */
    private int evictCold() {
        Node cold = tail.prev;
        detach(cold);
        // The stored key makes the map removal O(1).
        index.remove(cold.key);
        return cold.key;
    }

    int get(int key) {
        Node n = index.get(key);
        // A miss changes nothing.
        if (n == null) return -1;
        // A hit moves the exact node to the hot end.
        detach(n);
        insertFront(n);
        return n.value;
    }

    void put(int key, int value) {
        // Capacity 0 stores nothing.
        if (capacity == 0) return;
        Node n = index.get(key);
        if (n != null) {
            // An update counts as a use.
            n.value = value;
            detach(n);
            insertFront(n);
            return;
        }
        // A new key at full size evicts silently.
        if (index.size() == capacity) evictCold();
        n = new Node(key, value);
        index.put(key, n);
        insertFront(n);
    }

    /** Sets the capacity and evicts coldest first until the size fits. Time: O(e + 1). */
    int[] resize(int newCapacity) {
        capacity = newCapacity;
        List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
        // Each pass is one eviction, so the loop costs O(e).
        while (index.size() > capacity) out.add(evictCold());
        int[] r = new int[out.size()];
        for (int i = 0; i < r.length; i++) r[i] = out.get(i);
        return r;
    }

    int size() { return index.size(); }

    /** Verifies the combined invariant and returns the keys hot to cold. */
    int[] checkAndKeys() {
        List<Integer> fwd = new ArrayList<>();
        for (Node c = head.next; c != tail; c = c.next) {
            if (c.prev.next != c || c.next.prev != c) throw new AssertionError("links");
            if (index.get(c.key) != c) throw new AssertionError("map does not own this node");
            fwd.add(c.key);
        }
        if (fwd.size() != index.size()) throw new AssertionError("length differs from map size");
        int[] r = new int[fwd.size()];
        for (int i = 0; i < r.length; i++) r[i] = fwd.get(i);
        return r;
    }

    /** Oracle: a plain list of {key, value}, hot end at index 0. */
    static final class Model {
        final List<int[]> list = new ArrayList<>();
        int capacity;
        Model(int capacity) { this.capacity = capacity; }
        int find(int key) {
            for (int i = 0; i < list.size(); i++) if (list.get(i)[0] == key) return i;
            return -1;
        }
        int get(int key) {
            int i = find(key);
            if (i < 0) return -1;
            int[] e = list.remove(i);
            list.add(0, e);
            return e[1];
        }
        void put(int key, int value) {
            if (capacity == 0) return;
            int i = find(key);
            if (i >= 0) list.remove(i);
            else if (list.size() == capacity) list.remove(list.size() - 1);
            list.add(0, new int[] {key, value});
        }
        int[] resize(int c) {
            capacity = c;
            List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
            while (list.size() > capacity) out.add(list.remove(list.size() - 1)[0]);
            int[] r = new int[out.size()];
            for (int i = 0; i < r.length; i++) r[i] = out.get(i);
            return r;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 from the problem.
        LruResize a = new LruResize(3);
        a.put(1, 10); a.put(2, 20); a.put(3, 30);
        if (a.get(1) != 10) throw new AssertionError("e1 get");
        if (!Arrays.equals(a.resize(1), new int[] {2, 3})) throw new AssertionError("e1 resize");
        if (a.get(2) != -1 || a.get(1) != 10) throw new AssertionError("e1 after");
        // Example 2 from the problem.
        LruResize b = new LruResize(2);
        b.put(5, 50); b.put(6, 60);
        if (b.get(5) != 50) throw new AssertionError("e2 get");
        if (!Arrays.equals(b.resize(0), new int[] {6, 5})) throw new AssertionError("e2 resize");
        b.put(7, 70);
        if (b.get(5) != -1 || b.get(7) != -1 || b.size() != 0) throw new AssertionError("e2 after");
        // Growing the capacity evicts nothing.
        if (b.resize(4).length != 0) throw new AssertionError("grow");
        // Random scripts compared with the list model, with a fixed seed.
        Random rnd = new Random(33092);
        for (int t = 0; t < 400; t++) {
            int cap = 1 + rnd.nextInt(5);
            LruResize c = new LruResize(cap);
            Model m = new Model(cap);
            for (int s = 0; s < 200; s++) {
                int op = rnd.nextInt(10);
                int k = rnd.nextInt(8);
                if (op < 4) {
                    if (c.get(k) != m.get(k)) throw new AssertionError("get " + t + " " + s);
                } else if (op < 8) {
                    int v = rnd.nextInt(100);
                    c.put(k, v);
                    m.put(k, v);
                } else {
                    int nc = rnd.nextInt(6);
                    if (!Arrays.equals(c.resize(nc), m.resize(nc))) throw new AssertionError("resize " + t + " " + s);
                }
                int[] keys = c.checkAndKeys();
                if (keys.length != m.list.size()) throw new AssertionError("size");
                for (int i = 0; i < keys.length; i++) if (keys[i] != m.list.get(i)[0]) throw new AssertionError("order");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Repeated Get And Capacity One

Author exercise

Problem Specification. Run a script of cache operations on the cache from the previous exercise. Each operation is an int[]: {0,key} is a get, {1,key,value} is a put, and {2,newCapacity} is a resize. After every operation, walk the list forward from the head sentinel and backward from the tail sentinel. Every node must appear exactly once in each walk, the backward walk must be the reverse of the forward walk, and the walk length must equal the cache size. Return the keys of the final forward walk, hottest first.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Initial capacity is 1 <= capacity <= 20.
  • Operations number at most 200.
  • Keys are in the range 0..9.
  • Repeated get on the same key must leave the list unchanged after the first call.
  • Resize to 0 leaves an empty list and an empty map.

Example 1. Input capacity 1, then put(1,10), get(1) three times, and put(2,20). Output [2]. A following resize(0) gives the output [].

Example 2. Input capacity 3, then put for keys 1, 2 and 3, then get(1) three times. Output [1,3,2].

Prerequisites. The two exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The tests check a structural fact, one appearance per node in both directions, instead of only checking returned values.

Hint

A detach followed by an insert at the same place must still leave the node linked once. With one entry, which sentinel does head.next equal?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The solution reuses the map and sentinel list and adds a checker that runs after every operation. The checker walks forward from the head and counts each node, stopping at the tail sentinel, then walks backward from the tail and requires the reverse sequence. It also requires that the map holds each walked node under its own key, that no key repeats, and that the walk length equals the map size. A repeated get detaches a node and inserts it at the same place, and the checker shows that the node still appears once. With capacity 1, the only real node sits between both sentinels, so head.next and tail.prev are the same node. The resize to 0 then evicts that node and leaves the sentinels pointing at each other. The invariant is the combined one: map keys and list nodes are the same set, once each.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) average per operation, plus O(size) for each walk, which only the checker pays.
  • Space is O(size) for the nodes, the map and the sets used by the checker.
import java.util.*;

public final class RepeatedGetCapacityOne {
    private static final class Node {
        final int key;
        int value;
        Node prev, next;
        Node(int key, int value) { this.key = key; this.value = value; }
    }

    private final Map<Integer, Node> index = new HashMap<>();
    private final Node head = new Node(-1, 0);
    private final Node tail = new Node(-1, 0);
    private int capacity;

    RepeatedGetCapacityOne(int capacity) {
        this.capacity = capacity;
        head.next = tail;
        tail.prev = head;
    }

    private void detach(Node n) {
        // Connect the old neighbours to each other.
        n.prev.next = n.next;
        n.next.prev = n.prev;
    }

    private void insertFront(Node n) {
        // Link the node to the head and to the old first node.
        n.prev = head;
        n.next = head.next;
        head.next.prev = n;
        head.next = n;
    }

    private void evictCold() {
        Node cold = tail.prev;
        detach(cold);
        index.remove(cold.key);
    }

    /** Time: O(1) average. Space: O(1). Invariant: the hit node is linked exactly once afterwards. */
    int get(int key) {
        Node n = index.get(key);
        if (n == null) return -1;
        detach(n);
        insertFront(n);
        return n.value;
    }

    void put(int key, int value) {
        if (capacity == 0) return;
        Node n = index.get(key);
        if (n != null) {
            n.value = value;
            detach(n);
            insertFront(n);
            return;
        }
        if (index.size() == capacity) evictCold();
        n = new Node(key, value);
        index.put(key, n);
        insertFront(n);
    }

    void resize(int newCapacity) {
        capacity = newCapacity;
        // Evict coldest first until the size fits.
        while (index.size() > capacity) evictCold();
    }

    /** Walks both ways, proves one appearance per node, and returns the forward keys. Time: O(size). */
    int[] verify() {
        List<Node> fwd = new ArrayList<>();
        Set<Node> seen = Collections.newSetFromMap(new IdentityHashMap<>());
        for (Node c = head.next; c != tail; c = c.next) {
            // A repeated node would loop forever, so cap the walk at size + 1 steps.
            if (!seen.add(c)) throw new AssertionError("node appears twice forward");
            if (fwd.size() > index.size()) throw new AssertionError("forward walk too long");
            fwd.add(c);
        }
        List<Node> back = new ArrayList<>();
        for (Node c = tail.prev; c != head; c = c.prev) {
            if (back.size() > index.size()) throw new AssertionError("backward walk too long");
            back.add(c);
        }
        if (fwd.size() != index.size() || back.size() != index.size()) throw new AssertionError("walk length");
        Collections.reverse(back);
        for (int i = 0; i < fwd.size(); i++) {
            if (fwd.get(i) != back.get(i)) throw new AssertionError("backward is not the reverse");
            if (index.get(fwd.get(i).key) != fwd.get(i)) throw new AssertionError("map and list differ");
        }
        int[] r = new int[fwd.size()];
        for (int i = 0; i < r.length; i++) r[i] = fwd.get(i).key;
        return r;
    }

    /** Runs the script, verifying after every operation, and returns the final forward keys. */
    static int[] finalKeys(int capacity, int[][] ops) {
        RepeatedGetCapacityOne c = new RepeatedGetCapacityOne(capacity);
        for (int[] op : ops) {
            if (op[0] == 0) c.get(op[1]);
            else if (op[0] == 1) c.put(op[1], op[2]);
            else c.resize(op[1]);
            // One check per operation.
            c.verify();
        }
        return c.verify();
    }

    /** Oracle: list model with hot end at index 0 that returns the keys only. */
    static int[] oracle(int capacity, int[][] ops) {
        List<int[]> list = new ArrayList<>();
        int cap = capacity;
        for (int[] op : ops) {
            int idx = -1;
            if (op[0] != 2) for (int i = 0; i < list.size(); i++) if (list.get(i)[0] == op[1]) idx = i;
            if (op[0] == 0) {
                if (idx >= 0) list.add(0, list.remove(idx));
            } else if (op[0] == 1) {
                if (cap == 0) continue;
                if (idx >= 0) list.remove(idx);
                else if (list.size() == cap) list.remove(list.size() - 1);
                list.add(0, new int[] {op[1], op[2]});
            } else {
                cap = op[1];
                while (list.size() > cap) list.remove(list.size() - 1);
            }
        }
        int[] r = new int[list.size()];
        for (int i = 0; i < r.length; i++) r[i] = list.get(i)[0];
        return r;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: capacity one with repeated gets, then a resize to zero.
        int[][] e1 = {{1, 1, 10}, {0, 1}, {0, 1}, {0, 1}, {1, 2, 20}};
        if (!Arrays.equals(finalKeys(1, e1), new int[] {2})) throw new AssertionError("e1");
        int[][] e1b = {{1, 1, 10}, {0, 1}, {0, 1}, {0, 1}, {1, 2, 20}, {2, 0}};
        if (finalKeys(1, e1b).length != 0) throw new AssertionError("e1 resize to zero");
        // Example 2: three keys and three repeated gets of the oldest.
        int[][] e2 = {{1, 1, 1}, {1, 2, 2}, {1, 3, 3}, {0, 1}, {0, 1}, {0, 1}};
        if (!Arrays.equals(finalKeys(3, e2), new int[] {1, 3, 2})) throw new AssertionError("e2");
        // Random scripts compared with the list model, with a fixed seed.
        Random rnd = new Random(33093);
        for (int t = 0; t < 600; t++) {
            int cap = 1 + rnd.nextInt(4);
            int len = rnd.nextInt(60);
            int[][] ops = new int[len][];
            for (int i = 0; i < len; i++) {
                int op = rnd.nextInt(10);
                if (op < 5) ops[i] = new int[] {0, rnd.nextInt(6)};
                else if (op < 9) ops[i] = new int[] {1, rnd.nextInt(6), rnd.nextInt(50)};
                else ops[i] = new int[] {2, rnd.nextInt(5)};
            }
            if (!Arrays.equals(finalKeys(cap, ops), oracle(cap, ops))) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

LFU Cache With Eviction Log

LeetCode 460

Problem Specification. Design a cache with fixed capacity and two operations. get(key) returns the value or -1, and counts as one use of the key. put(key, value) inserts or updates the key, and an update counts as one use. A new key starts with use count 1. When a new key arrives and the cache is full, the cache evicts the key with the lowest use count. Among keys with equal use counts, it evicts the least recently used one. Each eviction appends the evicted key to a log. Run a script of operations and return the log as an int[].

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Capacity is 1 <= capacity <= 1000 and never changes.
  • Operations are {0,key} for get and {1,key,value} for put.
  • Script length is at most 10,000 operations.
  • Log order is eviction order.
  • Cost of each operation is O(1) average.

Example 1. Input capacity 2, then put(1,1), put(2,2), get(1), put(3,3), get(3), put(4,4). Output [2,1].

Example 2. Input capacity 2, then put(1,1), put(2,2), put(1,5), put(3,3). Output [2], because the update raised the use count of key 1.

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The single recency list becomes a family of lists indexed by use count, while the map still holds the shared nodes.

Hint

Keep one recency list for each use count, and remember the smallest use count that still has a node. Which list does a touched node leave, and which one does it join?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. This is the same ownership as before with one list for each use count. A map from key to node finds the node. A second map from use count to a doubly linked list holds the nodes with that count, hottest first. The state also holds minCount, the smallest count that has a node. A touch detaches the node from its count list, raises its count by one, and inserts it at the hot end of the next count list. If the old list is now empty and it was the minimum list, minCount moves up by one. A new key arrives with count 1 and sets minCount to 1. When the cache is full, the victim is the cold end of the minCount list, which is the least recently used node among the least frequently used. The key is added to the log, removed from the map and detached from its list. The combined invariant is that each node sits in the list for its own count, and the map keys equal the union of all list nodes.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) average per operation, since each operation does a few map lookups and a constant number of pointer writes.
  • Space is O(capacity) for the nodes, the maps and the count lists, plus O(e) for the log.
import java.util.*;

public final class LfuLog {
    private static final class Node {
        final int key;
        int value, count = 1;
        Node prev, next;
        Node(int key, int value) { this.key = key; this.value = value; }
    }

    /** Doubly linked list of nodes with one use count, hottest next to head. */
    private static final class Chain {
        final Node head = new Node(-1, 0), tail = new Node(-1, 0);
        int size;
        Chain() { head.next = tail; tail.prev = head; }
        void addFront(Node n) {
            n.prev = head;
            n.next = head.next;
            head.next.prev = n;
            head.next = n;
            size++;
        }
        void remove(Node n) {
            n.prev.next = n.next;
            n.next.prev = n.prev;
            size--;
        }
    }

    private final Map<Integer, Node> index = new HashMap<>();
    private final Map<Integer, Chain> byCount = new HashMap<>();
    private final List<Integer> log = new ArrayList<>();
    private final int capacity;
    private int minCount;

    LfuLog(int capacity) { this.capacity = capacity; }

    /** Moves n to the list of the next count. Time: O(1) average. Space: O(1) average. */
    private void touch(Node n) {
        Chain old = byCount.get(n.count);
        old.remove(n);
        // The minimum list may have just emptied, so the minimum count rises with the node.
        if (old.size == 0) {
            byCount.remove(n.count);
            if (minCount == n.count) minCount++;
        }
        n.count++;
        byCount.computeIfAbsent(n.count, c -> new Chain()).addFront(n);
    }

    int get(int key) {
        Node n = index.get(key);
        if (n == null) return -1;
        touch(n);
        return n.value;
    }

    void put(int key, int value) {
        Node n = index.get(key);
        if (n != null) {
            // An update counts as a use.
            n.value = value;
            touch(n);
            return;
        }
        if (index.size() == capacity) {
            // The victim is the coldest node of the smallest count.
            Chain low = byCount.get(minCount);
            Node victim = low.tail.prev;
            low.remove(victim);
            if (low.size == 0) byCount.remove(minCount);
            index.remove(victim.key);
            log.add(victim.key);
        }
        n = new Node(key, value);
        index.put(key, n);
        byCount.computeIfAbsent(1, c -> new Chain()).addFront(n);
        minCount = 1;
    }

    /** Runs the script and returns the log of evicted keys in eviction order. */
    static int[] evicted(int capacity, int[][] ops) {
        LfuLog c = new LfuLog(capacity);
        for (int[] op : ops) {
            if (op[0] == 0) c.get(op[1]);
            else c.put(op[1], op[2]);
        }
        int[] r = new int[c.log.size()];
        for (int i = 0; i < r.length; i++) r[i] = c.log.get(i);
        return r;
    }

    /** Oracle: entries {key, value, count, tick}; evict the smallest count, then the smallest tick. */
    static int[] oracle(int capacity, int[][] ops) {
        List<int[]> list = new ArrayList<>();
        List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
        int tick = 0;
        for (int[] op : ops) {
            tick++;
            int[] hit = null;
            for (int[] e : list) if (e[0] == op[1]) hit = e;
            if (op[0] == 0) {
                if (hit != null) { hit[2]++; hit[3] = tick; }
            } else if (hit != null) {
                hit[1] = op[2]; hit[2]++; hit[3] = tick;
            } else {
                if (list.size() == capacity) {
                    int[] victim = list.get(0);
                    for (int[] e : list) if (e[2] < victim[2] || (e[2] == victim[2] && e[3] < victim[3])) victim = e;
                    list.remove(victim);
                    out.add(victim[0]);
                }
                list.add(new int[] {op[1], op[2], 1, tick});
            }
        }
        int[] r = new int[out.size()];
        for (int i = 0; i < r.length; i++) r[i] = out.get(i);
        return r;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: key 2 goes first, then the tie between keys 1 and 3 goes to the older one.
        int[][] e1 = {{1, 1, 1}, {1, 2, 2}, {0, 1}, {1, 3, 3}, {0, 3}, {1, 4, 4}};
        if (!Arrays.equals(evicted(2, e1), new int[] {2, 1})) throw new AssertionError("e1");
        // Example 2: an update raises the count of key 1.
        int[][] e2 = {{1, 1, 1}, {1, 2, 2}, {1, 1, 5}, {1, 3, 3}};
        if (!Arrays.equals(evicted(2, e2), new int[] {2})) throw new AssertionError("e2");
        // Capacity one evicts the previous key every time.
        int[][] e3 = {{1, 1, 1}, {1, 2, 2}, {1, 3, 3}};
        if (!Arrays.equals(evicted(1, e3), new int[] {1, 2})) throw new AssertionError("capacity one");
        // Random scripts compared with the list model, with a fixed seed.
        Random rnd = new Random(33094);
        for (int t = 0; t < 800; t++) {
            int cap = 1 + rnd.nextInt(4);
            int len = rnd.nextInt(80);
            int[][] ops = new int[len][];
            for (int i = 0; i < len; i++) {
                if (rnd.nextInt(10) < 4) ops[i] = new int[] {0, rnd.nextInt(7)};
                else ops[i] = new int[] {1, rnd.nextInt(7), rnd.nextInt(50)};
            }
            if (!Arrays.equals(evicted(cap, ops), oracle(cap, ops))) throw new AssertionError("random " + t);
        }
    }
}verified

Find A Slot And Pick From Array

ProblemWhat Each Technique AddsSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

Why A Random Pick Gets Slower

A video platform keeps a pool of live streaming servers. Servers join the pool when traffic grows and leave when they fail a health check. Every incoming request asks for one server chosen uniformly at random from the pool. In the morning the pool holds 20 servers and nothing is noticeable. By the evening the pool holds 50,000 servers, joins and removals arrive thousands of times per second, and the routing code shows up first in every profile.

The pool must answer three questions quickly. Is this server already in the pool? Can the pool drop this server right now? Which server is the uniform random choice for the next request? One standard collection answers each question well, and no single standard collection answers all three. This lesson asks how to combine two collections so that insert, remove and random pick each cost constant time on average.

What Each Technique Adds

What The Map And Array Each Add

The lesson Pair A Map With Ordered Storage showed that a hash map can point into ordered storage, and the lesson Pick A Random Value In Constant Time showed that a dense array turns one random integer into one uniform element. Both ideas are needed here, and each covers what the other cannot.

The hash map answers membership and location. Given a server identifier, the map returns the array position that holds it, in constant expected time. An array cannot do that without scanning. The dynamic array answers selection and cheap removal at its end. Any position from 0 to size minus 1 is equally likely to be drawn, and removing the last element moves nothing. A map cannot do either, because its iteration order is not a numbered sequence. Together, the map supplies the address of a value and the array supplies a dense numbering of all values.

Simple approach

Keeping The Pool In One List

The direct plan keeps the servers in a single ArrayList. Insert scans the list to reject duplicates. Remove searches for the value and deletes it. The random pick draws an index.

final class ListPool {
    private final java.util.ArrayList<Integer> servers = new java.util.ArrayList<>();
    private final java.util.Random rng = new java.util.Random(7);

    boolean insert(int id) {
        if (servers.contains(id)) return false;     // linear scan for a duplicate
        servers.add(id);
        return true;
    }

    boolean remove(int id) {
        return servers.remove(Integer.valueOf(id)); // linear search, then shift the tail left
    }

    int getRandom() {
        return servers.get(rng.nextInt(servers.size())); // one draw, one read
    }
}

On a pool of 50,000 servers, a removal near the front searches a few positions and then shifts almost 50,000 elements one place left. A removal of a missing server scans all 50,000 and finds nothing. Only the random pick is fast.

Predict first: A pool holds 50,000 servers in an ArrayList. Which of insert, remove and getRandom has a cost that grows with the pool size, and what part of each call causes it?

Insert and remove both grow, at O(n) per call. Insert pays for the duplicate scan with contains. Remove pays for the search and then for shifting every later element one place left. Only getRandom stays at O(1), because it reads one index.

Bottleneck

Counting Where The List Spends Its Time

Two separate costs hide in the list version, and both are O(n). The first cost is finding a value. The contains call and the search inside remove(Object) compare the argument with elements one after another, and a missing value costs the whole list. The second cost is closing the gap. ArrayList.remove keeps the elements contiguous by moving every later element one position left. That shift costs O(n) for a removal near the front and O(1) only for a removal at the very end.

A pool with 50,000 servers and 5,000 removals per second performs about 250 million element moves per second on the shifting alone. Replacing the list with a HashSet fixes the first cost and breaks the third operation, because a set has no numbered positions. Drawing a random element from a set means iterating to the k-th element, which is O(n) again. The bottleneck is that one structure supplies either fast lookup or numbered positions, never both.

Key idea

Keep Each Value's Slot In The Map

Giving Every Value A Slot

Store the values in a dense array and store, for every value, the index where it lives. That index is the value's slot. A hash map from value to slot gives constant expected lookup. The array gives a uniform random pick, because every index from 0 to size minus 1 is a real value with no holes.

Removing By Swap-With-Last

Removal is the only hard part, because deleting from the middle leaves a hole. Avoid the hole by choosing which element leaves the array. Copy the last element into the removed element's slot, then shrink the array by one. That move is called swap-with-last. It costs O(1) because it touches one slot and the end of the array, and the array stays dense.

Repairing What The Move Broke

Swap-with-last moves one value to a new position, so the map entry of the moved value is now stale. The operation must rewrite that entry to the removed slot, and then delete the removed value's own entry. The fact that must hold after every public call is agreement between the two structures. Every index i below the size satisfies slotOf.get(items[i]) == i, and the map holds exactly size entries. A single missed repair breaks agreement, and the next lookup silently points at the wrong element.

State

State The Pool Keeps

The pool owns four fields, and the invariant ties them together. No caller reads these fields directly, so every operation can restore the invariant before it returns.

  • items is the int array of current values, and positions 0..size-1 are meaningful.
  • size is the count of current values and the first unused index.
  • slotOf is the hash map from a value to the index where items holds it.
  • rng is the Random that supplies the index for a pick.

The invariant is the agreement from the insight: slotOf.get(items[i]) == i for every i < size, and slotOf.size() == size. An insert appends at size and records that index. A remove performs swap-with-last and repairs one entry. A pick reads only items, so it changes nothing.

Walkthrough

Tracing Two Removals From The Pool

Removing A Value From The Middle

Start with the pool [5, 8, 2, 9], whose map holds 5 at slot 0, 8 at slot 1, 2 at slot 2 and 9 at slot 3. Remove 8. The map answers that 8 sits at slot 1, and the last element is 9 at slot 3.

The operation copies 9 into slot 1, so the array reads [5, 9, 2, 9] for a moment. The map entry for 9 still says slot 3, so the operation rewrites it to slot 1. The operation then deletes the key 8 and lowers size to 3. The meaningful part of the array is [5, 9, 2], and the old 9 at slot 3 is a stale leftover that no lookup can reach.

Removing The Last Element

Start with the pool [4, 7, 1] and remove 1. The map answers slot 2, and the last slot is also 2. The removed value and the moved value are the same element.

The copy would write 1 onto itself, which changes nothing. The map repair would set the entry for 1 to slot 2, and the delete that follows removes the key anyway. Everything depends on the order. If the delete ran first and the repair second, the repair would add the key 1 back, and the map would hold one entry too many.

Code

Implementing The Pool With Swap-With-Last

final class IndexedPool {
    private int[] items = new int[4];
    private int size = 0;
    private final java.util.HashMap<Integer, Integer> slotOf = new java.util.HashMap<>();
    private final java.util.Random rng;

    IndexedPool(long seed) { rng = new java.util.Random(seed); }

    /** Appends id when absent. Time O(1) amortized, Space O(1) extra. Invariant: agreement holds on exit. */
    boolean insert(int id) {
        if (slotOf.containsKey(id)) return false;           // reject duplicates in O(1)
        if (size == items.length) items = java.util.Arrays.copyOf(items, size * 2); // doubling gives amortized O(1)
        items[size] = id;                                   // the new value takes the first unused slot
        slotOf.put(id, size);                               // record where it lives
        size++;
        return true;
    }

    /** Removes id by swap-with-last. Time O(1) expected, Space O(1). Invariant: agreement holds on exit. */
    boolean remove(int id) {
        Integer slot = slotOf.get(id);                      // locate the value in O(1)
        if (slot == null) return false;                     // absent values change nothing
        int last = items[size - 1];                         // the value that will fill the hole
        items[slot] = last;                                 // overwrite the removed value
        slotOf.put(last, slot);                             // repair the moved value's slot first
        slotOf.remove(id);                                  // then delete the removed key, so a self-move stays deleted
        size--;                                             // the old last index is no longer meaningful
        return true;
    }

    /** Returns a uniform current value. Time O(1), Space O(1). Throws IllegalStateException when empty. */
    int getRandom() {
        if (size == 0) throw new IllegalStateException("empty pool");   // nextInt(0) would throw a less clear error
        return items[rng.nextInt(size)];                    // every index below size holds a real value
    }
}

The cost of each operation is constant on average. The hash map lookup is O(1) expected, the array write is O(1), and the array doubling is O(1) amortized over many inserts. The space is O(n) for the array and the map together. The order of put before remove in remove is deliberate and matters only for the case where the removed value is the last element.

When to use it

When A Map And Array Fit

Recognizing The Pattern

Reach for this pair when a problem asks for membership checks, deletion and uniform random selection in the same data structure, all at constant expected cost. The cue is the combination of "random element" with "remove by value". Either request alone has a simpler structure.

An invariant is a fact that every public operation must restore before it returns, and here the invariant is agreement between the map and the array. Write the invariant first and let it dictate the order of the steps inside remove.

Finding The False Friend

A false friend is a structure that looks like it solves the problem and fails on one required operation. Two qualify here. A HashSet gives constant lookup and removal, yet it has no numbered positions, so a uniform pick costs O(n). An ArrayList gives a constant random pick, yet remove(int) shifts the tail and remove(Object) also searches, so removal costs O(n).

Avoiding Java Hazards

The overloads remove(int index) and remove(Object o) on a list are easy to confuse when the values are integers. Comparing two Integer objects with == works only for small cached values, so compare with equals or unbox first. The structure fits poorly when the order of elements matters or when removals need ordering by age. Chapter 33 handles recency order with the map and linked list pair instead.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Remove By Swap

Author exercise

Problem Specification. A pool stores distinct int values in a dense list items and a map slotOf from each value to its index in items. Agreement holds when slotOf.get(items.get(i)) == i for every index i and slotOf.size() == items.size(). Implement boolean remove(int value) that returns false and changes nothing when value is absent. Otherwise it removes value in O(1) expected time by moving the last element into the vacated index, repairing the moved value's entry in slotOf, and shrinking the list. After every call to insert or remove, a check method agrees() must return true.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Values are distinct int values in the range 0..10^9.
  • Size is at most 10^5.
  • Mutation is allowed on items and slotOf only.
  • Order of the remaining elements is unspecified after a removal.
  • Cost of each operation is O(1) expected.

Example 1. Input is the inserts 5, 8, 2, 9 in that order, then remove(8). The output is true, items = [5,9,2] and the map sends 5 to 0, 9 to 1 and 2 to 2.

Example 2. Input is remove(7) and then remove(5) on that state. The output is false with nothing changed, then true with items = [2,9].

Prerequisites. The IndexedPool fields and the agreement invariant in this lesson.

What This Exercise Changes. First exercise on the pair: the removal now repairs a second structure, and a check method proves agreement after each call.

Hint

Which single map entry describes an element whose index just changed? Does the removed value's own entry still describe a real element?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The list items stays dense, so a removal must not leave a hole. The method reads the removed value's index from slotOf, copies the last element into that index and deletes the last list position. That copy changes the index of exactly one value, the moved one, so the method rewrites its map entry. The method then deletes the removed key. Agreement holds before the call and after it, which is why a check method can run after every operation. The method calls list.remove(list.size() - 1) with an int argument, so Java deletes by index.
