A call with an Integer argument would search by value instead, and the assertions in main cover the index overload.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) expected per call, because one map lookup, one list write, one map write and one map delete each cost constant time.
  • Space is O(1) extra per call, because the method stores two integers and the structure itself holds O(n).
import java.util.*;

public final class RemoveBySwap {
    static final class Pool {
        final ArrayList<Integer> items = new ArrayList<>();
        final HashMap<Integer, Integer> slotOf = new HashMap<>();

        /**
         * Appends value when absent. Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(1).
         * Invariant: slotOf.get(items.get(i)) == i for every i.
         */
        boolean insert(int value) {
            // A present value is rejected in O(1) by the map.
            if (slotOf.containsKey(value)) return false;
            // The new value takes index size, which the map records.
            slotOf.put(value, items.size());
            items.add(value);
            return true;
        }

        /**
         * Removes value by moving the last element into its index.
         * Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1). Invariant: agreement holds on exit.
         */
        boolean remove(int value) {
            // One map lookup finds the index of the removed value.
            Integer slot = slotOf.get(value);
            // An absent value leaves both structures untouched.
            if (slot == null) return false;
            // The last element is the one that fills the hole.
            int lastIndex = items.size() - 1;
            int moved = items.get(lastIndex);
            // Overwrite the hole; for the last index this writes the value onto itself.
            items.set(slot, moved);
            // The moved value now lives at the vacated index.
            slotOf.put(moved, slot);
            // The removed key must go last, so a self-move still ends deleted.
            slotOf.remove(value);
            // int argument: removes by index and shifts nothing, since it is the final position.
            items.remove(lastIndex);
            return true;
        }

        /** Checks agreement: each index maps back to itself and sizes match. */
        boolean agrees() {
            // The map holds exactly one entry per list element.
            if (slotOf.size() != items.size()) return false;
            // Every element's recorded slot equals its real index.
            for (int i = 0; i < items.size(); i++) {
                Integer s = slotOf.get(items.get(i));
                if (s == null || s != i) return false;
            }
            return true;
        }
    }

    static void check(boolean ok, String msg) { if (!ok) throw new AssertionError(msg); }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: insert 5, 8, 2, 9, then remove 8.
        Pool p = new Pool();
        for (int v : new int[]{5, 8, 2, 9}) check(p.insert(v) && p.agrees(), "insert");
        check(p.remove(8) && p.agrees(), "remove 8");
        check(p.items.equals(List.of(5, 9, 2)), "items after remove 8");
        check(p.slotOf.get(5) == 0 && p.slotOf.get(9) == 1 && p.slotOf.get(2) == 2, "slots");
        // Example 2: a missing value changes nothing, then remove 5.
        check(!p.remove(7) && p.items.equals(List.of(5, 9, 2)) && p.agrees(), "remove absent");
        check(p.remove(5) && p.items.equals(List.of(2, 9)) && p.agrees(), "remove 5");
        // The int overload removes by index even when the value equals an index.
        Pool q = new Pool();
        q.insert(0); q.insert(1); q.insert(2);
        check(q.remove(0) && q.items.equals(List.of(2, 1)) && q.agrees(), "index overload");
        // Random scripts against a plain list model, with agreement checked after every call.
        Random rnd = new Random(33);
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            Pool pool = new Pool();
            ArrayList<Integer> model = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = 0; step < 80; step++) {
                int v = rnd.nextInt(12);
                if (rnd.nextBoolean()) {
                    boolean expect = !model.contains(v);
                    if (expect) model.add(v);
                    check(pool.insert(v) == expect, "insert result");
                } else {
                    boolean expect = model.remove(Integer.valueOf(v));
                    check(pool.remove(v) == expect, "remove result");
                }
                check(pool.agrees(), "agreement");
                ArrayList<Integer> a = new ArrayList<>(pool.items), b = new ArrayList<>(model);
                Collections.sort(a); Collections.sort(b);
                check(a.equals(b), "same members");
            }
        }
        System.out.println("ok");
    }
}verified
Variation

Insert Delete GetRandom O(1) With A Different Pick

LeetCode 380

Problem Specification. Extend the pool with int getRandomExcluding(int value). It returns a current value chosen uniformly among all current values that differ from value. It returns -1 when no such value exists. The given value may be absent from the pool, and then every current value qualifies. The operation draws exactly one random integer and uses O(1) worst-case time, so it must not redraw in a loop.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Values are distinct int values in the range 0..10^9, so -1 never collides with a stored value.
  • Size is between 0 and 10^5.
  • Mutation is none; the operation reads state only.
  • Randomness comes from the one java.util.Random that the pool owns, created from a seed in the constructor.
  • Cost is O(1) expected, from one map lookup and one random draw.

Example 1. Input is the pool 3, 6, 9 and the call getRandomExcluding(6). The output is 3 or 9, each with probability 1/2.

Example 2. Input is the pool holding only 4 and the call getRandomExcluding(4). The output is -1.

Prerequisites. The Remove By Swap exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. The output contract changes from any current value to any value except one, so the random index range shrinks and one index is redirected.

Hint

When value is present, draw an index from a range one shorter than size. If the draw hits the slot of value, which other index has not yet been offered? When value is absent, every current value qualifies, so draw from the full range.

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. Let s be the excluded value's index when the value is present. The qualifying values then occupy every index except s, which is size - 1 indices. The method draws one integer r uniformly from 0..size-2. When r differs from s it returns items[r]. When r equals s it returns items[size-1], the one index that the range 0..size-2 can never reach when s is smaller than size - 1. Each qualifying index is then returned by exactly one draw value, so the pick is uniform. When s equals size - 1 the draw never equals s, and the pick is also uniform. When the value is absent the method draws from 0..size-1. A size of 0, or a size of 1 holding the excluded value, returns -1. The method draws once and has no loop, so the worst case is constant.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) expected, from one map lookup, one random draw and one array read, with no redraw loop.
  • Space is O(1) extra, because the method stores the index and the drawn integer.
import java.util.*;

public final class RandomExcluding {
    static final class Pool {
        int[] items = new int[2];
        int size = 0;
        final HashMap<Integer, Integer> slotOf = new HashMap<>();
        final Random rng;

        Pool(long seed) { rng = new Random(seed); }

        boolean insert(int v) {
            if (slotOf.containsKey(v)) return false;
            if (size == items.length) items = Arrays.copyOf(items, size * 2);
            items[size] = v;
            slotOf.put(v, size++);
            return true;
        }

        boolean remove(int v) {
            Integer s = slotOf.get(v);
            if (s == null) return false;
            int last = items[--size];
            items[s] = last;
            slotOf.put(last, s);
            slotOf.remove(v);
            return true;
        }

        /** Number of draw values for the excluded value; each draw value maps to one result. */
        int drawRange(int value) {
            // A present value removes one qualifying index; an absent value removes none.
            return slotOf.containsKey(value) ? size - 1 : size;
        }

        /** Maps a draw r in [0, drawRange) to a value; this is the whole pick, split out for exact checks. */
        int valueForDraw(int value, int r) {
            Integer s = slotOf.get(value);
            // Absent value: every index qualifies, so the draw is the index.
            if (s == null) return items[r];
            // The draw hit the excluded index: redirect to the last index, which the range never reaches.
            return r == s ? items[size - 1] : items[r];
        }

        /**
         * Uniform value different from value, or -1 when none exists.
         * Time: O(1) expected, from the map lookup. Space: O(1). Reads state only.
         */
        int getRandomExcluding(int value) {
            int range = drawRange(value);
            // No qualifying value: empty pool, or the only value is the excluded one.
            if (range == 0) return -1;
            // Exactly one draw, so there is no loop and the cost stays constant after the lookup.
            return valueForDraw(value, rng.nextInt(range));
        }
    }

    static void check(boolean ok, String msg) { if (!ok) throw new AssertionError(msg); }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: the pool 3, 6, 9 excluding 6 offers 3 and 9 once each across all draws.
        Pool p = new Pool(1);
        p.insert(3); p.insert(6); p.insert(9);
        check(p.drawRange(6) == 2, "range");
        TreeSet<Integer> seen = new TreeSet<>();
        for (int r = 0; r < p.drawRange(6); r++) check(seen.add(p.valueForDraw(6, r)), "duplicate result");
        check(seen.equals(new TreeSet<>(List.of(3, 9))), "example 1");
        // Example 2: a one-element pool excluding its only value returns -1.
        Pool one = new Pool(2);
        one.insert(4);
        check(one.getRandomExcluding(4) == -1, "example 2");
        check(one.getRandomExcluding(5) == 4, "absent value in one-element pool");
        check(new Pool(3).getRandomExcluding(1) == -1, "empty pool");
        // Random scripts: the exact set of results over all draws equals the model's qualifying values.
        Random rnd = new Random(380);
        for (int round = 0; round < 300; round++) {
            Pool pool = new Pool(round);
            ArrayList<Integer> model = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = 0; step < 60; step++) {
                int v = rnd.nextInt(10);
                if (rnd.nextInt(3) > 0) { if (pool.insert(v)) model.add(v); }
                else { if (pool.remove(v)) model.remove(Integer.valueOf(v)); }
                int ex = rnd.nextInt(11);
                ArrayList<Integer> want = new ArrayList<>();
                for (int m : model) if (m != ex) want.add(m);
                Collections.sort(want);
                ArrayList<Integer> got = new ArrayList<>();
                // Enumerating every draw value proves each qualifying value appears exactly once.
                for (int r = 0; r < pool.drawRange(ex); r++) got.add(pool.valueForDraw(ex, r));
                Collections.sort(got);
                check(got.equals(want), "uniform support");
                int pick = pool.getRandomExcluding(ex);
                check(want.isEmpty() ? pick == -1 : want.contains(pick), "pick membership");
            }
        }
        // Sampled frequencies stay near uniform for a fixed seed.
        Pool f = new Pool(99);
        for (int v : new int[]{10, 20, 30, 40}) f.insert(v);
        int[] counts = new int[4];
        for (int i = 0; i < 30000; i++) {
            int x = f.getRandomExcluding(20);
            check(x != 20 && x != -1, "excluded value returned");
            counts[x / 10 - 1]++;
        }
        check(counts[1] == 0, "excluded count");
        for (int idx : new int[]{0, 2, 3}) check(Math.abs(counts[idx] - 10000) < 500, "frequency " + counts[idx]);
        System.out.println("ok");
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Last-Slot Removal

Author exercise

Problem Specification. In a pool using swap-with-last, the removed value occupies the last index when the vacated index equals size - 1. Then the moved value and the removed value are the same element. Implement remove so that this case leaves the map without the removed key and without any extra entry. Show what goes wrong when the map repair runs after the key deletion. Include the case of a pool with exactly one element.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Values are distinct int values in the range 0..10^9.
  • Size before the call is at least 1.
  • Repair is one put for the moved value and one remove for the deleted key.
  • Result keeps slotOf.size() == items.size().

Example 1. Input is the pool [4,7,1] and remove(1). The output is true, items = [4,7], and the map holds only 4 and 7.

Example 2. Input is the pool [6] and remove(6). The output is true, items = [], and the map is empty.

Prerequisites. The Remove By Swap exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. The moved value and the removed value become the same element, so the order of the two map updates decides whether a deleted key survives.

Hint

When the removed index and the last index are equal, which of the two map updates should win?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. When the removed value sits at index size - 1, the moved value is the removed value. The repair slotOf.put(moved, slot) then targets the key about to be deleted. If the key deletion runs first and the repair second, the repair inserts the deleted key again, and the map holds one entry more than the array holds elements. The correct order repairs first and deletes second, so the final state has no entry for the removed key. A one-element pool is the same case with size - 1 equal to 0. The method then ends with size = 0 and an empty map. The solution also includes the faulty order, and the assertions show that the agreement check catches it.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) expected, because the order of two map updates adds no extra work.
  • Space is O(1) extra, because no temporary collection is created.
import java.util.*;

public final class LastSlotRemoval {
    static final class Pool {
        int[] items = new int[4];
        int size = 0;
        final HashMap<Integer, Integer> slotOf = new HashMap<>();

        void insert(int v) {
            if (slotOf.containsKey(v)) return;
            if (size == items.length) items = Arrays.copyOf(items, size * 2);
            items[size] = v;
            slotOf.put(v, size++);
        }

        /**
         * Correct order: repair the moved value, then delete the removed key.
         * Time: O(1) expected. Space: O(1). Invariant: agreement holds on exit.
         */
        boolean remove(int v) {
            Integer s = slotOf.get(v);
            if (s == null) return false;
            // When s == size - 1 this reads v itself: moved and removed values are identical.
            int moved = items[size - 1];
            items[s] = moved;
            // Repair first; for a self-move this writes a key that the next line deletes.
            slotOf.put(moved, s);
            slotOf.remove(v);
            size--;
            return true;
        }

        /** Faulty order kept for contrast: the repair resurrects a deleted key on a self-move. */
        boolean removeWrongOrder(int v) {
            Integer s = slotOf.get(v);
            if (s == null) return false;
            int moved = items[size - 1];
            items[s] = moved;
            slotOf.remove(v);
            slotOf.put(moved, s);
            size--;
            return true;
        }

        boolean agrees() {
            if (slotOf.size() != size) return false;
            for (int i = 0; i < size; i++) {
                Integer s = slotOf.get(items[i]);
                if (s == null || s != i) return false;
            }
            return true;
        }

        List<Integer> view() {
            ArrayList<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int i = 0; i < size; i++) out.add(items[i]);
            return out;
        }
    }

    static void check(boolean ok, String msg) { if (!ok) throw new AssertionError(msg); }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: remove the last of 4, 7, 1.
        Pool p = new Pool();
        for (int v : new int[]{4, 7, 1}) p.insert(v);
        check(p.remove(1) && p.view().equals(List.of(4, 7)) && p.agrees(), "example 1");
        check(p.slotOf.keySet().equals(Set.of(4, 7)), "map keys");
        // Example 2: remove the only element.
        Pool one = new Pool();
        one.insert(6);
        check(one.remove(6) && one.size == 0 && one.slotOf.isEmpty() && one.agrees(), "example 2");
        // The faulty order leaves a ghost key, and the agreement check detects it.
        Pool bad = new Pool();
        for (int v : new int[]{4, 7, 1}) bad.insert(v);
        bad.removeWrongOrder(1);
        check(bad.slotOf.containsKey(1) && !bad.agrees(), "faulty order must be caught");
        // Random scripts that favor last-slot removals, against a list model.
        Random rnd = new Random(92);
        for (int round = 0; round < 400; round++) {
            Pool pool = new Pool();
            ArrayList<Integer> model = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = 0; step < 60; step++) {
                if (rnd.nextInt(3) > 0 || model.isEmpty()) {
                    int v = rnd.nextInt(15);
                    if (!model.contains(v)) model.add(v);
                    pool.insert(v);
                } else {
                    // Half the removals target the newest element, which sits at the last index.
                    int v = rnd.nextBoolean() ? pool.items[pool.size - 1] : model.get(rnd.nextInt(model.size()));
                    model.remove(Integer.valueOf(v));
                    check(pool.remove(v), "remove present");
                }
                check(pool.agrees(), "agreement");
                ArrayList<Integer> a = new ArrayList<>(pool.view()), b = new ArrayList<>(model);
                Collections.sort(a); Collections.sort(b);
                check(a.equals(b), "members");
            }
        }
        System.out.println("ok");
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Duplicate Values Pool

LeetCode 381

Problem Specification. A multiset pool accepts repeated values. The array items holds one entry per occurrence. For each value the map keeps an int array of the indices where that value occurs, and the array grows by doubling. Implement boolean insert(int value) that returns true when the value was absent before, boolean remove(int value) that removes one occurrence and returns true when one existed, int countOf(int value) that returns the multiplicity, and int getRandom() that returns a value with probability equal to its multiplicity divided by size. All operations cost O(1) expected, with doubling amortized.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Values are int values in the range 0..10^9.
  • Size is at most 10^5 occurrences.
  • Index lists are plain int[] arrays with a count, with no set classes.
  • getRandom on an empty pool throws IllegalStateException.
  • Cost is O(1) expected per operation.

Example 1. Input is the inserts 3, 3, 8. The output is countOf(3) = 2, and getRandom() returns 3 with probability 2/3 and 8 with probability 1/3.

Example 2. Input is remove(3) then remove(5) on that state. The output is true with countOf(3) = 1, then false.

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. One value now owns several slots, so the map stores a growing list per value and every array slot remembers its position in that list.

Hint

An array slot that moves must update one entry in its value's index list. What extra number per array slot lets you find that entry in O(1)?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The array items holds one entry per occurrence, so a uniform index draw already picks each value with probability equal to its multiplicity divided by size. Each value owns a record with a growing int[] of the array indices where it occurs. The array posInList stores, for each array index, the position of that index inside its value's list. Removal detaches the last entry of the value's list, which names the array index s, and costs O(1). The last array index m then fills s. The moved value's list entry at posInList[m] is rewritten to s, and posInList[s] takes that position. When the moved value equals the removed value, its entry sits inside the same list, below the shrunken count, so the same two writes stay correct. An empty list deletes the map key, and insert returns true only when the key was absent.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) expected per operation, with array doubling amortized, because every step is a map lookup or an indexed write.
  • Space is O(n) for the array, the position array and the lists, since every occurrence owns one entry in each.
import java.util.*;

public final class DuplicateValuesPool {
    static final class Slots {
        int[] idx = new int[2];
        int n = 0;
        void add(int i) {
            // Doubling keeps appends amortized O(1).
            if (n == idx.length) idx = Arrays.copyOf(idx, n * 2);
            idx[n++] = i;
        }
    }

    static final class Pool {
        int[] items = new int[2];
        int[] posInList = new int[2];
        int size = 0;
        final HashMap<Integer, Slots> map = new HashMap<>();
        final Random rng;

        Pool(long seed) { rng = new Random(seed); }

        /**
         * Adds one occurrence; true when the value was absent. Time O(1) amortized. Space O(1) extra.
         * Invariant: items[map.get(v).idx[p]] == v and posInList[idx[p]] == p.
         */
        boolean insert(int v) {
            if (size == items.length) {
                items = Arrays.copyOf(items, size * 2);
                posInList = Arrays.copyOf(posInList, size * 2);
            }
            Slots list = map.get(v);
            boolean fresh = list == null;
            if (fresh) { list = new Slots(); map.put(v, list); }
            // The new occurrence takes array index size and the next list position.
            items[size] = v;
            posInList[size] = list.n;
            list.add(size);
            size++;
            return fresh;
        }

        /** Removes one occurrence. Time O(1) expected. Space O(1). */
        boolean remove(int v) {
            Slots list = map.get(v);
            if (list == null) return false;
            // The last list entry names the array index to vacate, so detaching it is O(1).
            int s = list.idx[--list.n];
            int m = size - 1;
            if (s != m) {
                // The last array element moves into s; its list entry must now name s.
                Slots other = map.get(items[m]);
                int p = posInList[m];
                other.idx[p] = s;
                posInList[s] = p;
                items[s] = items[m];
            }
            size--;
            // An exhausted list removes the key, so countOf reports 0.
            if (list.n == 0) map.remove(v);
            return true;
        }

        int countOf(int v) { Slots l = map.get(v); return l == null ? 0 : l.n; }

        /** Value drawn with probability count/size. Time O(1). Throws IllegalStateException when empty. */
        int getRandom() {
            if (size == 0) throw new IllegalStateException("empty pool");   // an empty pool has nothing to draw
            return items[rng.nextInt(size)];
        }

        boolean agrees() {
            int total = 0;
            for (Map.Entry<Integer, Slots> e : map.entrySet()) {
                Slots l = e.getValue();
                if (l.n == 0) return false;
                total += l.n;
                for (int p = 0; p < l.n; p++) {
                    int i = l.idx[p];
                    if (i < 0 || i >= size || items[i] != e.getKey() || posInList[i] != p) return false;
                }
            }
            return total == size;
        }
    }

    static void check(boolean ok, String msg) { if (!ok) throw new AssertionError(msg); }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: insert 3, 3, 8.
        Pool p = new Pool(5);
        // An empty pool throws IllegalStateException on getRandom.
        try { p.getRandom(); throw new AssertionError("empty getRandom must throw"); }
        catch (IllegalStateException expected) { }
        check(p.insert(3) && !p.insert(3) && p.insert(8), "inserts");
        check(p.countOf(3) == 2 && p.countOf(8) == 1 && p.agrees(), "counts");
        int threes = 0, draws = 30000;
        for (int i = 0; i < draws; i++) if (p.getRandom() == 3) threes++;
        check(Math.abs(threes - draws * 2 / 3) < 600, "proportional draw " + threes);
        // Example 2: remove one 3, then a missing 5.
        check(p.remove(3) && p.countOf(3) == 1 && p.agrees(), "remove 3");
        check(!p.remove(5) && p.agrees(), "remove 5");
        // Random scripts against a list model, checking counts and structure after each call.
        Random rnd = new Random(381);
        for (int round = 0; round < 400; round++) {
            Pool pool = new Pool(round);
            ArrayList<Integer> model = new ArrayList<>();
            for (int step = 0; step < 80; step++) {
                int v = rnd.nextInt(6);
                if (rnd.nextInt(5) < 3) {
                    boolean fresh = !model.contains(v);
                    model.add(v);
                    check(pool.insert(v) == fresh, "insert result");
                } else {
                    boolean had = model.remove(Integer.valueOf(v));
                    check(pool.remove(v) == had, "remove result");
                }
                check(pool.agrees(), "structure");
                check(pool.size == model.size(), "size");
                for (int x = 0; x < 6; x++) check(pool.countOf(x) == Collections.frequency(model, x), "count " + x);
            }
        }
        System.out.println("ok");
    }
}verified

Traversal State Across Public Calls

ProblemWhat Each Technique AddsSimple approachBottleneckKey ideaStateWalkthroughCodeWhen to use itExercises
Problem

A Parser That Loses One Token

A JSON parser reads tokens from a lexer through a small interface with hasNext() and next(). The parser must look at the upcoming token before it decides which rule to apply. A developer writes hasNext() so that it reads one token from the lexer and returns true when the read succeeds. The parser calls hasNext() once in a loop condition and next() once in the body. Every second token disappears, and the parser reports an error on valid input.

The lexer, the parser and the loop are each correct on their own. The failure sits between them, in the promise that a question about the upcoming element must not remove that element. Earlier chapters walked a tree or a nested structure inside one function, where the walk ran from start to finish without interruption. Here the caller controls the pace and the order of calls. This lesson asks what state a class must keep so that every public operation leaves the traversal ready for the next one.

What Each Technique Adds

What Traversal And Class Each Add

The traversal supplies the order and the position. A stack of unvisited tree nodes, or a stack of nested-list cursors, already knows which element comes next and how to find the one after it. Used inside one function, that knowledge lives in local variables and disappears when the function returns.

The class supplies the contract. It moves the position into fields, so the position survives between calls. It also fixes which operations may change the position, how often a caller may repeat an operation, and what a call on an exhausted structure must do. A caller may ask hasNext() five times in a row or call peek() before every next(). The traversal alone gives no answer to those calls, because it assumes one pass. The combination answers a single question: which fields must the class keep so that any legal sequence of calls returns the right elements in the right order.

Simple approach

Copying The Whole Traversal Into A List

The direct design runs the whole traversal once, stores every value in a list and then serves calls from the list. The class below does that for a binary search tree. The constructor performs an in-order walk, and next() returns the values one at a time.

import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;

final class EagerTreeIterator {
    record TreeNode(int val, TreeNode left, TreeNode right) {}

    private final List<Integer> values = new ArrayList<>();
    private int position = 0;

    EagerTreeIterator(TreeNode root) {
        collect(root);
    }

    private void collect(TreeNode node) {
        if (node == null) return;
        collect(node.left());
        values.add(node.val());
        collect(node.right());
    }

    boolean hasNext() {
        return position < values.size();
    }

    int next() {
        return values.get(position++);
    }
}

For a tree holding 2, 5 and 9, the constructor stores [2, 5, 9], and three calls to next() return those values in order. Repeated calls to hasNext() change nothing, so the class behaves correctly on small trees.

Bottleneck

The Constructor Pays For Every Node

Predict first: A tree holds 1,000,000 nodes, and a caller reads only the first value and then stops. How much time and memory does the constructor spend, and how much did the caller need?

The constructor spends O(n) time and O(n) memory, about a million list entries, before the first call returns. The caller needed one value, which a walk down the leftmost path of O(h) nodes can find, where h is the height.

The constructor takes O(n) time and the list takes O(n) space before the caller asks for anything. A caller that stops after one value still pays for all of it. A query engine that scans an index range and stops at the first matching row shows the cost clearly. The recursive walk also uses O(h) call frames, so a tree that leans to one side with a million nodes overflows the Java call stack inside the constructor.

The list is also a snapshot. If another part of the program inserts a node after construction, the iterator keeps returning the old values. The goal is a class that holds O(h) state, starts in O(h) time and produces each value on demand. That goal needs a state smaller than the full answer, and it adds a new requirement: the state must stay consistent after every call, in any order.

Key idea

Keep Only What The Next Call Needs

Hold The Unvisited Ancestors

The spine stack is a stack of tree nodes whose values are not yet returned. After construction and after every call, it holds the path from the root to the next node to return, restricted to nodes that are not yet returned. Its top is the next element. The constructor pushes the root and then every left child below it. A call to next() pops the top, pushes the left spine of that node's right child, and returns the popped value. Each node is pushed once and popped once, so next() costs O(1) amortized and O(h) in the worst case, and the stack uses O(h) space.

Store One Element Of Lookahead

A source that cannot restart, such as a network stream or a file reader, cannot give back an element once it has returned it. To let a caller look without consuming, the wrapper keeps a cached head. This is one slot that holds an element already pulled from the source but not yet returned to the caller. A call to peek() fills the slot if it is empty and returns it. A call to next() returns the slot and empties it, or pulls directly when the slot is empty. The slot is the only place where an element is both pulled and unreturned.

State The Rule Every Operation Restores

The next-element invariant says that between any two calls, the fields alone determine the next element to return, and they record exactly the elements not yet returned. A call to next() removes one element from that record. A call to hasNext() or peek() removes none. These two operations may move internal position, for example by skipping empty nested lists or by filling the cached head, but they return the same answer when repeated, and they never lose an element. The invariant is the condition that every public operation must restore before it returns.

State

Fields That Hold The Position

Each iterator in this lesson keeps a small set of fields, and every operation reads or writes only these.

  • stack holds the tree nodes or nested-list cursors that still have elements to give.
  • head is the one element already pulled from the source and not yet returned, valid only when hasHead is true.
  • hasHead tells whether head holds an element, so a legitimate value of zero is never confused with an empty slot.
  • source is the structure that supplies elements and cannot be restarted.

The size of stack equals the number of unfinished ancestors, so it never exceeds the height of the tree or the nesting depth of the list.

Walkthrough

Tracing The Stack And The Slot

A Search Tree Read In Order

Take the tree with root 6, left child 2, right child 9, and the values 1 and 4 below 2, with 3 below 4 on the left, and 8 below 9 on the left. The in-order values are 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 9. The constructor pushes 6, 2 and 1, so the stack holds three nodes and its top is 1. A call to next() pops 1 and finds no right child, so the stack shrinks to 6 and 2.

The next call pops 2 and pushes the left spine of its right child 4, which is 4 and 3. The stack grows to 6, 4 and 3, and its top is 3. The remaining calls pop 3, 4 and 6, and the pop of 6 pushes 9 and 8. The stack reaches depth zero after 9 is returned.

A Peek That Pulls Once

Wrap a source that holds 4, 7 and 9. The first peek() finds the slot empty and pulls 4, so the source has now been called once. The second peek() finds the slot full and returns 4 without calling the source. A call to next() returns 4 and empties the slot. A call to hasNext() asks the source whether more elements exist and does not pull. The next next() pulls 7 directly, because the slot is empty. A later peek() pulls 9 once.

Code

Two Iterators That Keep The Invariant

A Tree Iterator On Node Objects

final class IteratorState {
    record TreeNode(int val, TreeNode left, TreeNode right) {}

    static final class SpineIterator {
        private final java.util.ArrayDeque<TreeNode> stack = new java.util.ArrayDeque<>();

        SpineIterator(TreeNode root) { pushLeft(root); }

        private void pushLeft(TreeNode node) {
            for (; node != null; node = node.left()) stack.push(node); // each node enters the stack once
        }

        boolean hasNext() { return !stack.isEmpty(); } // reads state only, removes nothing

        int next() {
            if (stack.isEmpty()) throw new java.util.NoSuchElementException();
            TreeNode top = stack.pop();                // the top is the next element
            pushLeft(top.right());                     // restore the invariant for the following call
            return top.val();
        }
    }
}

A Wrapper That Can Look Ahead

final class PeekState {
    static final class Peeker {
        private final java.util.Iterator<Integer> source;
        private int head;
        private boolean hasHead;

        Peeker(java.util.Iterator<Integer> source) { this.source = source; }

        int peek() {
            if (!hasHead) { head = source.next(); hasHead = true; } // at most one pull per cached element
            return head;
        }

        boolean hasNext() { return hasHead || source.hasNext(); }   // never pulls

        int next() {
            if (hasHead) { hasHead = false; return head; }          // hand over the cached element
            return source.next();
        }
    }
}

Both classes check the invariant at the end of each operation. SpineIterator costs O(h) space and O(1) amortized time for next(). Peeker uses O(1) extra space and makes one source call for each element, no matter how often the caller peeks.

When to use it

When A Class Must Resume Traversal

Recognize The Call-By-Call Pattern

Use this design when a class exposes hasNext(), next() or a similar pair, the data is large or cannot restart, and callers repeat or interleave operations. An invariant is a fact about the fields that every public operation must restore before it returns. Here the fact is the next-element invariant. Write it down first, then check each operation against it, including the operation on an exhausted structure, which must throw NoSuchElementException and leave the fields unchanged.

Beware The Plausible Shortcuts

A false friend is a technique that fits the problem on the first example and breaks a condition of the contract. Eager flattening is one. It returns correct values on a static tree, and it costs O(n) before the first call and goes stale when the tree changes. A hasNext() that pulls an element and forgets it is another. It passes a test that calls hasNext() and next() in strict alternation, and it loses data when the caller calls hasNext() twice.

Java Details That Cause Failures

Use ArrayDeque for the stack, because Stack is a legacy synchronized class. Keep a hasHead flag, because an Integer head set to null hides a real value and a plain int has no empty state. Document what happens when the underlying structure changes after construction, since a stack of nodes reflects the tree at the time of the last next() and not later inserts.

Exercises

Exercises

Basic

Tree Iterator Over A Node Pool

LeetCode 173

Problem Specification. This variation of Binary Search Tree Iterator stores the tree in three parallel arrays, the node pool. Node i has the value val[i], the left child left[i] and the right child right[i], and the index -1 means no child. Implement a class PoolTreeIterator(int[] left, int[] right, int[] val, int root) with the operations hasNext(), next() and peekDepth(). The tree is a binary search tree, and next() returns the values in increasing order. Keep the unvisited left spine in an int stack. The operation peekDepth() returns the current size of that stack. The operation next() on an exhausted iterator throws NoSuchElementException.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Nodes number 0 <= n <= 10^5, and n equals the length of each array.
  • Values are distinct int numbers.
  • Root is a valid index, or -1 for an empty tree.
  • Nodes outside the tree rooted at root may exist in the pool and are never visited.
  • Cost of next() is O(1) amortized, and extra space is O(h).

Example 1. Input: a pool with val = [8,3,10,1,6,14,4], left = [1,3,-1,-1,6,-1,-1], right = [2,4,5,-1,-1,-1,-1] and root = 0. Output: the depths from peekDepth() are 3 after construction, then 2, 3, 2, 1, 1, 1, 0 after each of seven calls to next(), and the values returned are 1, 3, 4, 6, 8, 10, 14.

Example 2. Input: val = [5,6,7], left = [-1,-1,-1], right = [1,2,-1] and root = 0, a chain that leans right. Output: the depths are 1 after construction and 1, 1, 0 after the three calls, and the values are 5, 6, 7.

Prerequisites. The spine stack in this lesson and the lesson Keep Traversal State Between Calls.

What This Exercise Changes. The tree lives in index arrays and not in node objects, and a new operation exposes the stack size, so the stack must hold indexes and its size must stay exact.

Hint

After a call to next(), which nodes must enter the stack, and where does the walk stop?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The stack holds node indexes in an int array whose size is the pool length, since a path never has more nodes than the pool. The constructor walks from the root through left links and pushes every index on the way. The invariant is that the stack holds exactly the nodes on the path from the root to the next node to return that are not yet returned, with the next node on top. The operation next() pops the top, pushes the left spine of its right child to restore the invariant, and returns the popped value. The operation hasNext() tests top > 0 and changes nothing. The operation peekDepth() returns top, which equals the number of unfinished ancestors plus the next node. An exhausted next() throws before it touches any field, so the state stays valid.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) amortized for next(), because each node is pushed once and popped once across a full traversal, and O(h) for a single call in the worst case.
  • Space is O(h) in use, because the stack never holds more than one root-to-node path, and the array is allocated at the pool length for simplicity.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.NoSuchElementException;
import java.util.Random;

public final class PoolTreeIteratorDemo {
    static final class PoolTreeIterator {
        private final int[] left, right, val;
        // Stack of node indexes; top is the count of used slots.
        private final int[] stack;
        private int top = 0;

        PoolTreeIterator(int[] left, int[] right, int[] val, int root) {
            this.left = left;
            this.right = right;
            this.val = val;
            this.stack = new int[val.length];
            pushLeftSpine(root);
        }

        /** Pushes node and every left descendant; each node enters the stack once, so the total cost is O(n). */
        private void pushLeftSpine(int node) {
            // Walk down the left links until the index -1 marks the end of the spine.
            while (node != -1) {
                stack[top++] = node;
                node = left[node];
            }
        }

        /** Time: O(1). Space: O(1). Reads state only. */
        boolean hasNext() {
            // The stack is empty exactly when every value was returned.
            return top > 0;
        }

        /** Time: O(1) amortized, O(h) worst case. Space: O(h). Invariant: the top is the next node after the call. */
        int next() {
            // Throw before any write so the failure leaves the state valid.
            if (top == 0) throw new NoSuchElementException();
            // The top node holds the next value in increasing order.
            int node = stack[--top];
            // Restore the invariant: the left spine of the right child comes before everything still on the stack.
            pushLeftSpine(right[node]);
            return val[node];
        }

        /** Time: O(1). Space: O(1). Returns the stack size. */
        int peekDepth() {
            return top;
        }
    }

    /** Oracle: in-order values by recursion over the pool. */
    static void inorder(int[] l, int[] r, int[] v, int node, List<Integer> out) {
        if (node == -1) return;
        inorder(l, r, v, l[node], out);
        out.add(v[node]);
        inorder(l, r, v, r[node], out);
    }

    /** Oracle for the depth: nodes on the search path to the next value whose value is at least that value. */
    static int expectedDepth(int[] l, int[] r, int[] v, int root, int nextValue) {
        int depth = 0;
        int node = root;
        // Search for the next value like a normal tree lookup and count nodes not smaller than it.
        while (node != -1) {
            if (v[node] >= nextValue) depth++;
            if (v[node] == nextValue) break;
            node = nextValue < v[node] ? l[node] : r[node];
        }
        return depth;
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1 from the exercise: the depths and values are recomputed here.
        int[] val = {8, 3, 10, 1, 6, 14, 4};
        int[] left = {1, 3, -1, -1, 6, -1, -1};
        int[] right = {2, 4, 5, -1, -1, -1, -1};
        PoolTreeIterator it = new PoolTreeIterator(left, right, val, 0);
        int[] depths = {3, 2, 3, 2, 1, 1, 1, 0};
        int[] values = {1, 3, 4, 6, 8, 10, 14};
        if (it.peekDepth() != depths[0]) throw new AssertionError("initial depth");
        for (int i = 0; i < values.length; i++) {
            if (!it.hasNext()) throw new AssertionError("hasNext " + i);
            if (it.next() != values[i]) throw new AssertionError("value " + i);
            if (it.peekDepth() != depths[i + 1]) throw new AssertionError("depth " + i);
        }
        if (it.hasNext()) throw new AssertionError("should be exhausted");
        boolean threw = false;
        try { it.next(); } catch (NoSuchElementException e) { threw = true; }
        if (!threw) throw new AssertionError("exhausted next must throw");
        if (it.peekDepth() != 0) throw new AssertionError("failed next changed the state");

        // Example 2: a chain that leans right never holds more than one node.
        PoolTreeIterator chain = new PoolTreeIterator(new int[] {-1, -1, -1}, new int[] {1, 2, -1}, new int[] {5, 6, 7}, 0);
        int[] chainDepths = {1, 1, 1, 0};
        int[] chainValues = {5, 6, 7};
        if (chain.peekDepth() != chainDepths[0]) throw new AssertionError("chain initial");
        for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
            if (chain.next() != chainValues[i]) throw new AssertionError("chain value");
            if (chain.peekDepth() != chainDepths[i + 1]) throw new AssertionError("chain depth");
        }
        // A left-leaning chain holds every node at once; an empty tree holds none.
        PoolTreeIterator lean = new PoolTreeIterator(new int[] {1, 2, -1}, new int[] {-1, -1, -1}, new int[] {9, 8, 7}, 0);
        if (lean.peekDepth() != 3) throw new AssertionError("left chain");
        PoolTreeIterator empty = new PoolTreeIterator(new int[0], new int[0], new int[0], -1);
        if (empty.hasNext() || empty.peekDepth() != 0) throw new AssertionError("empty tree");

        // Random check: insert distinct values into a pool, replay random calls, compare with the oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(173);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 300; trial++) {
            int n = rnd.nextInt(40);
            int[] l = new int[n], r = new int[n], v = new int[n];
            java.util.Arrays.fill(l, -1);
            java.util.Arrays.fill(r, -1);
            java.util.HashSet<Integer> used = new java.util.HashSet<>();
            int count = 0;
            int root = -1;
            while (count < n) {
                int x = rnd.nextInt(1000);
                if (!used.add(x)) continue;
                v[count] = x;
                if (root == -1) root = count;
                else {
                    int cur = root;
                    // Standard binary search tree insertion into the pool.
                    while (true) {
                        if (x < v[cur]) { if (l[cur] == -1) { l[cur] = count; break; } cur = l[cur]; }
                        else { if (r[cur] == -1) { r[cur] = count; break; } cur = r[cur]; }
                    }
                }
                count++;
            }
            List<Integer> expected = new ArrayList<>();
            inorder(l, r, v, root, expected);
            PoolTreeIterator t = new PoolTreeIterator(l, r, v, root);
            int returned = 0;
            // Interleave hasNext, peekDepth and next in random order.
            for (int step = 0; step < 4 * n + 4; step++) {
                int op = rnd.nextInt(3);
                boolean more = returned < expected.size();
                if (op == 0) {
                    if (t.hasNext() != more) throw new AssertionError("hasNext oracle");
                } else if (op == 1) {
                    int want = more ? expectedDepth(l, r, v, root, expected.get(returned)) : 0;
                    if (t.peekDepth() != want) throw new AssertionError("depth oracle");
                } else if (more) {
                    if (t.next() != expected.get(returned)) throw new AssertionError("next oracle");
                    returned++;
                }
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Variation

Peeking Over A Source That Cannot Restart

LeetCode 284

Problem Specification. This variation of Peeking Iterator wraps an IntSource with the operations hasNext() and next(). The source cannot restart, and a public counter calls records how many times next() ran on it. Implement CountingPeeker(IntSource source) with the operations peek(), hasNext() and next(). Each operation must cause at most one call to source.next() each time an element becomes cached. A repeated peek() and any hasNext() cause none. When the source holds no more elements, peek() and next() throw NoSuchElementException.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Source length is 0 <= m <= 10^5 values of type int.
  • Counter calls is read-only for the wrapper and starts at 0.
  • Operations are called in any order, with any number of repeats.
  • Cost is O(1) time and O(1) extra space for every operation.

Example 1. Input: a source holding 4, 7, 9 and the calls peek, peek, hasNext, next, next, peek, next, hasNext. Output: the results are 4, 4, true, 4, 7, 9, 9, false, and calls is 1 after the first two operations and 3 at the end.

Example 2. Input: a source holding 5 and the calls hasNext three times, then next. Output: the results are true, true, true, 5, and calls is 0 before the call to next() and 1 after it.

Prerequisites. The cached head in this lesson and the tree exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. The source cannot restart and reports its own call count, so the proof of correctness shifts from the returned values to the number of pulls.

Hint

Which operation is allowed to touch the source, and what must stay true about the slot afterward?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The wrapper keeps one cached element in head and a flag hasHead. The operation peek() pulls from the source only when the slot is empty, stores the result and returns it, so repeated peeks return the stored value with no source call. The operation next() hands over the stored element and clears the flag, or pulls directly when the slot is empty. The operation hasNext() returns hasHead || source.hasNext(), and source.hasNext() is a question that the source answers without producing an element, so it never touches the counter. The invariant is that the elements not yet returned are the cached element, if any, followed by the rest of the source. Every pull happens at the moment an element leaves the source for the first time, so calls equals the number of elements returned plus one when the slot is full.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(1) for every operation, because each does a constant number of field reads and at most one source call.
  • Space is O(1) extra, because the wrapper stores one int and one flag.
import java.util.NoSuchElementException;
import java.util.Random;

public final class CountingPeekerDemo {
    interface IntSource {
        boolean hasNext();
        int next();
    }

    /** Source that cannot restart and counts its own next() calls. */
    static final class CountingSource implements IntSource {
        private final int[] data;
        private int index = 0;
        int calls = 0;

        CountingSource(int[] data) { this.data = data; }

        public boolean hasNext() { return index < data.length; }

        public int next() {
            if (index >= data.length) throw new NoSuchElementException();
            calls++;
            return data[index++];
        }
    }

    static final class CountingPeeker {
        private final IntSource source;
        private int head;
        private boolean hasHead;

        CountingPeeker(IntSource source) { this.source = source; }

        /** Time: O(1). Space: O(1). Pulls at most once, only when the slot is empty. */
        int peek() {
            // An empty slot is the only moment a pull may happen; the exhausted source throws here.
            if (!hasHead) {
                head = source.next();
                hasHead = true;
            }
            return head;
        }

        /** Time: O(1). Space: O(1). Never calls source.next(). */
        boolean hasNext() {
            // Either the slot holds an element or the source still has one.
            return hasHead || source.hasNext();
        }

        /** Time: O(1). Space: O(1). Invariant: unreturned elements are the slot followed by the source rest. */
        int next() {
            // Hand over the cached element first so no element is skipped or repeated.
            if (hasHead) {
                hasHead = false;
                return head;
            }
            return source.next();
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: results and the counter after each stage are recomputed here.
        CountingSource s = new CountingSource(new int[] {4, 7, 9});
        CountingPeeker p = new CountingPeeker(s);
        if (p.peek() != 4 || p.peek() != 4) throw new AssertionError("peek twice");
        if (s.calls != 1) throw new AssertionError("two peeks must pull once");
        if (!p.hasNext() || s.calls != 1) throw new AssertionError("hasNext must not pull");
        if (p.next() != 4 || s.calls != 1) throw new AssertionError("next uses the cache");
        if (p.next() != 7 || s.calls != 2) throw new AssertionError("next pulls directly");
        if (p.peek() != 9 || s.calls != 3) throw new AssertionError("peek pulls once");
        if (p.next() != 9 || s.calls != 3) throw new AssertionError("last next");
        if (p.hasNext()) throw new AssertionError("exhausted");

        // Example 2: repeated hasNext never touches the source.
        CountingSource one = new CountingSource(new int[] {5});
        CountingPeeker q = new CountingPeeker(one);
        for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) if (!q.hasNext()) throw new AssertionError("hasNext true");
        if (one.calls != 0) throw new AssertionError("no pull yet");
        if (q.next() != 5 || one.calls != 1) throw new AssertionError("single pull");

        // An empty source makes peek and next throw and records no call.
        CountingSource none = new CountingSource(new int[0]);
        CountingPeeker e = new CountingPeeker(none);
        boolean peekThrew = false;
        boolean nextThrew = false;
        try { e.peek(); } catch (NoSuchElementException ex) { peekThrew = true; }
        try { e.next(); } catch (NoSuchElementException ex) { nextThrew = true; }
        if (!peekThrew || !nextThrew || none.calls != 0) throw new AssertionError("empty source");

        // Random check: replay random operations against a list model and compare values and call counts.
        Random rnd = new Random(284);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 500; trial++) {
            int m = rnd.nextInt(12);
            int[] data = new int[m];
            for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) data[i] = rnd.nextInt(100);
            CountingSource src = new CountingSource(data);
            CountingPeeker w = new CountingPeeker(src);
            int consumed = 0;
            boolean cached = false;
            for (int step = 0; step < 40; step++) {
                int before = src.calls;
                int op = rnd.nextInt(3);
                boolean more = consumed < m;
                if (op == 0) {
                    if (w.hasNext() != more) throw new AssertionError("hasNext model");
                    if (src.calls != before) throw new AssertionError("hasNext pulled");
                } else if (op == 1) {
                    if (!more) {
                        boolean threw = false;
                        try { w.peek(); } catch (NoSuchElementException ex) { threw = true; }
                        if (!threw) throw new AssertionError("peek must throw");
                    } else {
                        if (w.peek() != data[consumed]) throw new AssertionError("peek model");
                        // A peek pulls once when the slot was empty and never otherwise.
                        if (src.calls - before != (cached ? 0 : 1)) throw new AssertionError("peek pulls");
                        cached = true;
                    }
                } else if (more) {
                    if (w.next() != data[consumed]) throw new AssertionError("next model");
                    // A next pulls once only when the slot was empty.
                    if (src.calls - before != (cached ? 0 : 1)) throw new AssertionError("next pulls");
                    consumed++;
                    cached = false;
                }
                // The counter equals the returned elements plus the cached one.
                if (src.calls != consumed + (cached ? 1 : 0)) throw new AssertionError("call total");
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Edge Cases

Flatten With Empty Lists And A Depth Limit

LeetCode 341

Problem Specification. This variation of Flatten Nested List Iterator uses a class Nest that holds either one integer or a list of Nest values, and a list may be empty. Implement FlatIterator(List<Nest> top, int maxDepth) with the operations hasNext() and next() that return the integers in left-to-right order. The top-level list has depth 1, and each list nested inside a list has one more than its parent. The constructor does not scan the input. The first call to hasNext() or next() that would enter a list with a depth greater than maxDepth throws IllegalArgumentException, and every later call throws it again. Calls before that point behave normally. Empty lists at the start, at the end and between integers produce no values.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Total size counting integers and lists is at most 10^5.
  • Limit satisfies 1 <= maxDepth <= 1000.
  • Integers are int values.
  • Repeated hasNext() calls return the same answer and consume nothing.
  • Exhausted next() throws NoSuchElementException.
  • Too deep is reported by IllegalArgumentException from hasNext() or next(), never by the constructor.

Example 1. Input: [[], 1, [[], 2, []], [], 3, []] with maxDepth = 3. Output: 1, 2, 3.

Example 2. Input: the same list with maxDepth = 2. Output: the first next() returns 1, and the following hasNext() throws IllegalArgumentException, because the empty list inside [[], 2, []] sits at depth 3.

Prerequisites. The next-element invariant in this lesson and the peeking exercise above.

What This Exercise Changes. Empty lists make the next element unknown without a search, and the depth limit is a failure that appears at the call that reaches the deep list, so the constructor stays cheap.

Hint

When is it safe for hasNext() to move past an empty list, and what must it store when it reaches an integer?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The constructor only stores the top-level iterator, so it costs O(1) and reads nothing. The iteration keeps a stack of Java iterators, one for each list that still has unread entries, plus a cached integer head. The operation hasNext() loops while no integer is cached. It pops finished iterators, which are empty lists or finished lists, pushes the iterator of any nested list it meets, and stores the first integer it reaches. The stack size is the depth of the list on top, so before it pushes a nested list it compares the new depth with maxDepth. A list that is too deep sets a failed flag and throws IllegalArgumentException, and every later call throws again. Moving past empty lists removes no integer from the sequence, and repeating the call finds the cached integer at once, so hasNext() is idempotent. The invariant is that the integers not yet returned are the cached one, if any, followed by what the stacked iterators will produce. The operation next() calls hasNext(), clears the cache and returns it.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(N) in total for N entries, including integers and lists, because each entry is read once. The depth comparison costs O(1) per list.
  • Space is O(d) for the stack, where d is at most maxDepth, plus O(1) for the cache.
import java.util.ArrayDeque;
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.Arrays;
import java.util.Iterator;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.NoSuchElementException;
import java.util.Random;

public final class BoundedFlattenDemo {
    /** Either one integer or a list of Nest values; a list may be empty. */
    static final class Nest {
        final Integer value;
        final List<Nest> items;

        private Nest(Integer value, List<Nest> items) { this.value = value; this.items = items; }

        static Nest of(int v) { return new Nest(v, null); }

        static Nest list(Nest... parts) { return new Nest(null, Arrays.asList(parts)); }

        boolean isInt() { return value != null; }
    }

    static final class FlatIterator {
        private final ArrayDeque<Iterator<Nest>> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
        private int head;
        private boolean hasHead = false;
        private boolean failed = false;
        private final int maxDepth;

        FlatIterator(List<Nest> top, int maxDepth) {
            // The constructor reads nothing; the depth limit is checked during the scan.
            this.maxDepth = maxDepth;
            stack.push(top.iterator());
        }

        /** Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(d). Skips empty lists and caches the next integer; removes nothing. */
        boolean hasNext() {
            // A list that was too deep keeps the iterator failed for every later call.
            if (failed) throw new IllegalArgumentException("depth exceeds " + maxDepth);
            // Keep going until an integer is cached or every iterator is finished.
            while (!hasHead && !stack.isEmpty()) {
                Iterator<Nest> top = stack.peek();
                if (!top.hasNext()) {
                    // A finished or empty list contributes nothing, so drop its cursor.
                    stack.pop();
                } else {
                    Nest n = top.next();
                    if (n.isInt()) {
                        // Cache the integer; it is pulled but not yet returned.
                        head = n.value;
                        hasHead = true;
                    } else {
                        // The stack size is the depth of the top list, so the nested list sits one deeper.
                        if (stack.size() + 1 > maxDepth) {
                            failed = true;
                            throw new IllegalArgumentException("depth " + (stack.size() + 1) + " exceeds " + maxDepth);
                        }
                        // A nested list becomes the new top cursor.
                        stack.push(n.items.iterator());
                    }
                }
            }
            return hasHead;
        }

        /** Time: O(1) amortized. Space: O(d). Invariant: unreturned integers are the cache then the stack. */
        int next() {
            if (!hasNext()) throw new NoSuchElementException();
            hasHead = false;
            return head;
        }
    }

    static int depthOf(List<Nest> list, int depth) {
        int best = depth;
        for (Nest n : list) if (!n.isInt()) best = Math.max(best, depthOf(n.items, depth + 1));
        return best;
    }

    static void flatten(List<Nest> list, List<Integer> out) {
        for (Nest n : list) {
            if (n.isInt()) out.add(n.value);
            else flatten(n.items, out);
        }
    }

    static Nest random(Random rnd, int depthLeft) {
        if (depthLeft == 0 || rnd.nextInt(3) == 0) return Nest.of(rnd.nextInt(50));
        int len = rnd.nextInt(4);
        Nest[] parts = new Nest[len];
        for (int i = 0; i < len; i++) parts[i] = random(rnd, depthLeft - 1);
        return Nest.list(parts);
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: empty lists at the start, interior and end, depth exactly at the limit.
        List<Nest> top = Arrays.asList(
            Nest.list(), Nest.of(1), Nest.list(Nest.list(), Nest.of(2), Nest.list()), Nest.list(), Nest.of(3), Nest.list());
        if (depthOf(top, 1) != 3) throw new AssertionError("the example depth is 3");
        FlatIterator it = new FlatIterator(top, 3);
        // Repeated hasNext calls return the same answer and consume nothing.
        if (!it.hasNext() || !it.hasNext() || !it.hasNext()) throw new AssertionError("hasNext repeat");
        int[] want = {1, 2, 3};
        for (int w : want) if (it.next() != w) throw new AssertionError("value " + w);
        if (it.hasNext()) throw new AssertionError("exhausted");
        boolean threw = false;
        try { it.next(); } catch (NoSuchElementException e) { threw = true; }
        if (!threw) throw new AssertionError("next must throw");

        // Example 2: with a limit of 2 the first next returns 1, and the following hasNext throws.
        FlatIterator shallow = new FlatIterator(top, 2);
        if (shallow.next() != 1) throw new AssertionError("value before the deep list");
        boolean rejected = false;
        try { shallow.hasNext(); } catch (IllegalArgumentException e) { rejected = true; }
        if (!rejected) throw new AssertionError("depth limit");
        // Every later call throws again.
        rejected = false;
        try { shallow.next(); } catch (IllegalArgumentException e) { rejected = true; }
        if (!rejected) throw new AssertionError("sticky failure");

        // Only empty lists: no values at all.
        FlatIterator hollow = new FlatIterator(Arrays.asList(Nest.list(Nest.list())), 3);
        if (hollow.hasNext()) throw new AssertionError("only empty lists");

        // Random check: compare with a recursive flatten, and the limit with a recursive depth.
        Random rnd = new Random(341);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 500; trial++) {
            int parts = rnd.nextInt(6);
            Nest[] arr = new Nest[parts];
            for (int i = 0; i < parts; i++) arr[i] = random(rnd, 5);
            List<Nest> input = Arrays.asList(arr);
            int limit = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6);
            int depth = depthOf(input, 1);
            if (depth > limit) {
                boolean bad = false;
                FlatIterator deep = new FlatIterator(input, limit);
                // A full drain enters every list, so it must reach the deep one.
                try { while (deep.hasNext()) deep.next(); } catch (IllegalArgumentException e) { bad = true; }
                if (!bad) throw new AssertionError("limit should reject depth " + depth);
                continue;
            }
            List<Integer> expected = new ArrayList<>();
            flatten(input, expected);
            FlatIterator f = new FlatIterator(input, limit);
            int at = 0;
            // Randomly repeat hasNext before each next to check that it never consumes.
            while (true) {
                int repeats = rnd.nextInt(3);
                boolean more = false;
                for (int i = 0; i <= repeats; i++) more = f.hasNext();
                if (more != (at < expected.size())) throw new AssertionError("hasNext oracle");
                if (!more) break;
                if (f.next() != expected.get(at)) throw new AssertionError("value oracle");
                at++;
            }
        }
    }
}verified
Pattern Recognition

Zigzag Over K Sources With Rotation Count

LeetCode 281

Problem Specification. This variation of Zigzag Iterator reads k int arrays in turns. Implement Zigzag(int[][] sources) with the operations hasNext(), next() and rotations(). The cursor names the source that supplies the next value. The rotation rule moves the cursor right after each call to next() while values remain. The cursor first steps forward one source, wrapping from the last source to the first, so it steps at least once per next(). It then keeps stepping one source at a time until it reaches a source with a value left. The second part of the rule is called skipExhausted, and it is a part of the rotation rule and not a public operation. Each single step counts as one rotation. The constructor also moves the cursor from source 0 to the first non-empty source, and those steps count. The operation rotations() returns the total so far.

Input Constraints. The limits are:

  • Sources number 1 <= k <= 1000, and any source may be empty.
  • Values total at most 10^5.
  • No step is taken when no values remain.
  • Exhausted next() throws NoSuchElementException.

Example 1. Input: [[1,2,3],[],[4],[5,6]]. Output: the values come out as 1, 4, 5, 2, 6, 3, and rotations() is 8 after the last call.

Example 2. Input: [[7,8]]. Output: the values are 7 and 8, and rotations() is 1, because the cursor takes its one required step after 7, which wraps back to source 0, and takes no step after 8. The input [[],[]] has no values and rotations() is 0.

Prerequisites. All three exercises above.

What This Exercise Changes. The class keeps a cursor over many sources and counts its own movement, so the invariant becomes "the cursor names a source with a value left".

Hint

Which source must the cursor name between two calls, and when does the cursor stop moving?

Algorithmic Solution (attempt the problem first)

Approach. The class keeps one read position for each source, a cursor, a count of values left and a counter of rotations. The invariant is that whenever values remain, the cursor names a source whose position is before its end. The constructor runs skipExhausted, which advances the cursor one source at a time while the current source has no value, and each step adds one to the counter. The operation next() reads the value at the cursor, advances that position and lowers the remaining count. If values remain, it moves the cursor forward one step, counts it and calls skipExhausted, so the invariant holds again. When nothing remains, no step is taken, which keeps the cursor from looping on empty sources. The operation hasNext() tests the remaining count and changes nothing.

Complexity.

  • Time is O(k) worst case for one call, because the cursor may pass every source once, and the total of all rotations is at most the number of values times k.
  • Space is O(k) for the read positions, plus O(1) for the other fields, and the sources are not copied.
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.NoSuchElementException;
import java.util.Random;

public final class ZigzagRotationsDemo {
    static final class Zigzag {
        private final int[][] sources;
        private final int[] pos;
        private int cursor = 0;
        private int remaining = 0;
        private int rotations = 0;

        Zigzag(int[][] sources) {
            this.sources = sources;
            this.pos = new int[sources.length];
            for (int[] s : sources) remaining += s.length;
            // Establish the invariant right after construction.
            if (remaining > 0) skipExhausted();
        }

        /** Moves the cursor forward, one counted step at a time, until it names a source with a value left. */
        private void skipExhausted() {
            // The loop ends because remaining > 0 guarantees some source still has a value.
            while (pos[cursor] == sources[cursor].length) {
                cursor = (cursor + 1) % sources.length;
                rotations++;
            }
        }

        /** Time: O(1). Space: O(1). Reads state only. */
        boolean hasNext() {
            return remaining > 0;
        }

        /** Time: O(k) worst case. Space: O(1). Invariant: the cursor names a source with a value, unless nothing remains. */
        int next() {
            if (remaining == 0) throw new NoSuchElementException();
            int value = sources[cursor][pos[cursor]++];
            remaining--;
            // Rotate only while values remain so the cursor never spins on empty sources.
            if (remaining > 0) {
                cursor = (cursor + 1) % sources.length;
                rotations++;
                skipExhausted();
            }
            return value;
        }

        /** Time: O(1). Space: O(1). */
        int rotations() {
            return rotations;
        }
    }

    /** Oracle: round-by-round order, and rotation count from cyclic distances between the used sources. */
    static int[] oracle(int[][] src, List<Integer> values) {
        List<Integer> used = new ArrayList<>();
        int longest = 0;
        for (int[] s : src) longest = Math.max(longest, s.length);
        for (int round = 0; round < longest; round++) {
            for (int j = 0; j < src.length; j++) {
                if (round < src[j].length) {
                    values.add(src[j][round]);
                    used.add(j);
                }
            }
        }
        int k = src.length;
        int total = 0;
        int previous = 0;
        // The first distance starts at source 0; later ones start at the previous source and wrap a full circle on a tie.
        for (int idx = 0; idx < used.size(); idx++) {
            int j = used.get(idx);
            if (idx == 0) total += j;
            else {
                int d = ((j - previous) % k + k) % k;
                total += d == 0 ? k : d;
            }
            previous = j;
        }
        return new int[] {total};
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        // Example 1: values and rotation count are recomputed from the sources.
        int[][] a = {{1, 2, 3}, {}, {4}, {5, 6}};
        Zigzag z = new Zigzag(a);
        int[] want = {1, 4, 5, 2, 6, 3};
        for (int w : want) {
            if (!z.hasNext() || z.next() != w) throw new AssertionError("value " + w);
        }
        if (z.hasNext()) throw new AssertionError("exhausted");
        if (z.rotations() != 8) throw new AssertionError("rotations of example 1");
        boolean threw = false;
        try { z.next(); } catch (NoSuchElementException e) { threw = true; }
        if (!threw) throw new AssertionError("exhausted next must throw");

        // Example 2: one source wraps once after the first value and stops after the last.
        Zigzag single = new Zigzag(new int[][] {{7, 8}});
        if (single.next() != 7 || single.next() != 8 || single.rotations() != 1) throw new AssertionError("single source");
        // No values: no steps at all.
        Zigzag hollow = new Zigzag(new int[][] {{}, {}});
        if (hollow.hasNext() || hollow.rotations() != 0) throw new AssertionError("all empty");

        // Random check: compare order and rotation count with the round-based oracle.
        Random rnd = new Random(281);
        for (int trial = 0; trial < 500; trial++) {
            int k = 1 + rnd.nextInt(6);
            int[][] src = new int[k][];
            for (int j = 0; j < k; j++) {
                src[j] = new int[rnd.nextInt(5)];
                for (int i = 0; i < src[j].length; i++) src[j][i] = rnd.nextInt(100);
            }
            List<Integer> expected = new ArrayList<>();
            int expectedRotations = oracle(src, expected)[0];
            Zigzag t = new Zigzag(src);
            for (int v : expected) {
                // hasNext twice proves it consumes nothing.
                if (!t.hasNext() || !t.hasNext()) throw new AssertionError("hasNext");
                if (t.next() != v) throw new AssertionError("order oracle");
            }
            if (t.hasNext()) throw new AssertionError("leftover");
            if (t.rotations() != expectedRotations) throw new AssertionError("rotation oracle");
        }
    }
}verified
